NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
The Applicant, TheracosBio LLC, has submitted a New Drug Application (NDA) for Brenzavvy
(bexagliflozin) tablets as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve glycemic control in adults
with type 2 diabetes (T2DM). The recommended dosage is 20 mg orally once daily.
Bexagliflozin is a small molecule inhibitor of the sodium-glucose linked transporter 2 (sodiumglucose co-transporter-2, SGLT2), the transporter responsible for reabsorption of the majority of
glucose from the renal glomerular filtrate in the renal proximal tubule. By inhibiting SGLT2,
bexagliflozin reduces renal reabsorption of filtered glucose and lowers the renal threshold for
glucose, and thereby increases urinary glucose excretion. Bexagliflozin also reduces sodium
reabsorption and increases the delivery of sodium to the distal tubule. There are currently four
FDA-approved SGLT2 inhibitors indicated for improved glycemic control in adults with T2DM.
Bexagliflozin is a new molecular entity (NME), and the Applicant is submitting this NDA using
the 505(b)(1) approval pathway.
T2DM is a serious and life-threatening condition that if left untreated leads to an increased risk
of morbidity and mortality. Despite numerous available therapeutic options, a significant number
of patients have difficulty achieving adequate glycemic control. Landmark clinical trials in both
type 1 diabetes and type 2 diabetes demonstrated that improved glycemic control as assessed by
hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) leads to substantial reduction in the incidence of microvascular
complications, including diabetic retinopathy, nephropathy, and neuropathy (Control et al. 1993;
Ohkubo et al. 1995; UKPDS 1998). Improved glycemic control demonstrated by reduction of
HbA1c is considered a surrogate for reduction of microvascular complications of diabetes and
has served as the basis for traditional approval of drugs and biologic products for a glycemic
control indication in people with diabetes (FDA 2022).
The Applicant demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness for the proposed indication
with data from six phase 3, adequate and well-controlled investigations, including four placebocontrolled trials and two active controlled trials.
Bexagliflozin was compared to placebo in subjects with inadequately controlled T2DM as
monotherapy (C-450), as add-on to metformin (C-419), and as add-on to standard of care
therapies in subjects with inadequately controlled T2DM and increased cardiovascular risk (C476) and moderate renal impairment (C-448).
The primary efficacy endpoint for glycemic control in these trials was the mean change from
baseline in HbA1c. The placebo-adjusted estimate of the treatment effect in trials C-450, C-419,
and C-476 (all conducted primarily in patients with normal renal function) ranged from -0.38%
to -0.48%. The results were statistically significant and clinically meaningful, and collectively
represent substantial evidence of effectiveness. In the active-controlled trials, bexagliflozin was
statistically non-inferior to sitagliptin (C-423) and glimepiride (C-480) as an add-on to
metformin, although glimepiride was only titrated up to a maximum dose of 6 mg, which is less
than the maximum approved dose of 8 mg in the US. In C-423 the estimated treatment difference
versus sitagliptin was 0.08 (-0.06, 0.22). In C-480 the estimated treatment versus glimepiride was
4
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

-0.03 (-0.20, 0.13). In both trials, the upper bound of the 95% confidence interval did not exceed
the prespecified non-inferiority margin of 0.35. In the dedicated renal impairment trial (C-448)
enrolling subjects with stage 3 chronic kidney disease (moderate renal impairment, defined as
estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) ≥30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2), the placebo-adjusted
estimate of the treatment effect was -0.27% (95% CI: -0.44, -0.09). This result was statistically
significant. In contrast, no treatment effect was observed in a subgroup analysis of subjects with
eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73m2 in trial C-476, the trial of patients with increased cardiovascular risk.
This trial was not designed to evaluate glycemic control in patients with renal impairment;
therefore, we place more weight on the demonstrated glycemic effects in the dedicated renal
impairment efficacy and safety trial.
Statistically significant decreases in body weight versus placebo were observed in trials where
bexagliflozin was added to standard of care therapy in subjects with renal impairment (C-448)
and increased CV risk (C-476). Statistically significant reduction in systolic blood pressure
(SBP) with bexagliflozin versus placebo was observed in the CV risk trial (C-476).
Safety findings were generally consistent with those of other SGLT2 inhibitor products.
Consistent with other members of the SGLT2 inhibitor class, the Warnings and Precautions
section of labeling will include the risk of ketoacidosis, urosepsis and pyelonephritis, Fournier’s
gangrene, genital mycotic infections, volume depletion, and hypoglycemia with concomitant use
of insulin or an insulin secretagogue.
An increased incidence of nontraumatic amputation was observed compared to placebo in a trial
of patients with increased CV risk (hazard ratio: 1.64 (0.7, 3.8)). Lower limb amputation is a
recognized complication of diabetes mellitus, and an increased risk of amputation has been
identified with some, but not all, members of the SGLT2 inhibitor class. A statement advising
prescribers to consider factors that may increase the risk of amputation before initiating
bexagliflozin and to monitor patients for infections or ulcers of lower limbs also appears
appropriate to mitigate this potential risk and will be included in the Warnings and Precautions
section of labeling.
Section 6 of the prescribing information will include subsections to describe imbalances
unfavorable to bexagliflozin for the following adverse events: bone fracture, sepsis, rash, and
dermatitis.
The Applicant conducted a program-wide meta-analysis to evaluate the cardiovascular risk of
exposure to bexagliflozin. The primary endpoint was time-to-first event of a four-component
composite endpoint consisting of CV death, nonfatal stroke, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or
hospitalization for unstable angina. The estimated hazard ratio of the composite endpoint
associated with bexagliflozin relative to control was 0.80 (95% CI: 0.58, 1.09). Exposure to
bexagliflozin is not expected to substantially increase the risk of MACE in patients with T2DM.
Key review issues in the evaluation of benefit/risk included efficacy of bexagliflozin in subjects
with moderate renal impairment, increased risk of nontraumatic amputation with bexagliflozin,
overall safety of bexagliflozin, and quality of the safety database. Refer to the Sections 2.2
(Conclusions Regarding Benefit and Risk), 3.1 (Review Issue List), and the linked subsections of
6 (Assessment of Effectiveness) and 7 (Risk and Risk Management) for details.

5
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

All review disciplines, the clinical division director, and the office director (signatory) support
approval of the application. Refer to Sections II (Interdisciplinary Assessment) and III
(Appendices) for details of the individual discipline reviews.
In summary, the application demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness of bexagliflozin
as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve glycemic control in adults with T2DM. The
approved dosage is 20 mg orally once daily. Bexagliflozin is not recommended for use in
patients with an eGFR <30 mg/mL/1.73 m2 and is contraindicated in patients on dialysis,
because there is a progressive decline in urinary glucose excretion with accompanying loss of
pharmacodynamic blood glucose-lowering effect with increased severity of renal impairment, as
well as a reduction in urine output. As such, there will be minimal, if any, efficacy in this
population always outweighed by the risks. Major safety issues are adequately communicated in
labeling. Approval will include a Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) postmarketing
requirement (PMR) issued to assess the safety and efficacy of bexagliflozin in pediatric patients
≥10 to <18 years of age.

T2DM is a serious and life-threatening condition that if left

Conclusions and Reasons

Current
Treatment
Options

•

Despite the many available treatment options, many patients
continue to have difficulty achieving adequate glycemic
control. Further, T2DM is a progressive disorder and patients
typically need additional agents added as the disease
progresses. Many patients with T2DM also have renal
impairment, and therefore, it is important to have available
treatment options for this population.

•

•

•

There are currently 12 pharmacologic classes of
antihyperglycemic medications (generally with multiple
members in each class), approved to improve glycemic
control in patients with T2DM (American Diabetes
Association Professional Practice et al. 2022b). There are
different safety concerns for each class.
Published guidelines have generally recommended initiating
treatment with lifestyle interventions and metformin, with the
choice of additional therapies and/or alternatives based on
factors such as baseline cardiovascular (CV) and renal
comorbidities, efficacy, hypoglycemia risk, impact on weight,
patient preference, and cost (American Diabetes Association
Professional Practice et al. 2022b). Because T2DM is a
progressive disease, maintenance of glycemic targets with
monotherapy is often possible for only a few years, after
which combination therapy is necessary. Initiation of some
antihyperglycemic products are not recommended for use in
patients with moderate renal impairment and/or may require
dose adjustment in this patient population.
There are currently four sodium-glucose cotransporter 2
(SGLT2) inhibitor products approved in the United States
(ertugliflozin, dapagliflozin, canagliflozin, empagliflozin)
(Janssen 2020; AstraZeneca 2021; Merck 2021; Ingelheim
2022).
While all approved antihyperglycemic medications for T2DM
have been shown to improve glycemic control, some have
also been shown to improve CV and renal outcomes,
including several SGLT2 inhibitor and glucagon-like peptide
(GLP-1) receptor agonist products (Janssen 2020;
AstraZeneca 2021; Ingelheim 2022; Lilly 2022; Nordisk
2022a; Nordisk 2022b).

Six phase 3 trials that met the criteria for adequate and well- •
controlled investigations were submitted to establish efficacy
of bexagliflozin 20 mg for the proposed indication.
These trials used HbA1c, an average measure of glycemic
control over 2-3 months and an established surrogate for
reduction in microvascular complications, as the key efficacy
measure of effectiveness. Studies such as UKPDS indicate
that a reduction in HbA1c is considered clinically meaningful
because it corresponds with decreased rates of
microvascular disease in proportion to the improvement in
HbA1c.
Bexagliflozin was statistically superior to placebo as
monotherapy, with a placebo-adjusted change from baseline
in hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) of -0.38% (-0.63%, -0.13%;
p=0.002), and as add-on to metformin, with a placeboadjusted change from baseline of -0.48% (-0.69%, -0.26%;
•
p<0.0001). This magnitude of improvement is in line with
previously approved anti-diabetics.
Bexagliflozin was also noninferior to sitagliptin [0.08% (-0.06,
0.22)] and glimepiride [-0.03% (-0.20, 0.13)], two products
•
that are approved for the treatment of T2DM, when evaluated
using a noninferiority margin of 0.35% for HbA1c.

subjects with stage 3 chronic kidney disease (CKD)

The submitted clinical trial data provides substantial
evidence of effectiveness of bexagliflozin for the
proposed indication, as an adjunct to diet and exercise to
improve glycemic control in adults with T2DM. Data from
the six phase 3 trials, which evaluated the use of
bexagliflozin as monotherapy and in combination with
other antihyperglycemic products, showed clinically
meaningful improvements in HbA1c compared to placebo
and noninferiority compared to active comparators. In
addition, several trials enrolled distinct patient
populations, including patients with increased CV risk and
patients with renal impairment. In all trials, improvements
in HbA1c were considered clinically meaningful, and the
benefit of this product would be relevant to many patients
with T2DM.
Effiicacy data support approval of dosing in patients with
impaired renal function (eGFR 30-60 mL/min/1.73 m2),
providing another therapeutic option for patients with both
T2DM and CKD.
In certain trials, efficacy data demonstrate that
bexaglflozin results in reduced body weight and/or SBP,
which are other effects generally associated with the
class.
The safety and efficacy of bexagliflozin in pediatric
patients ≥10 to <18 years of age is currently unknown but
will be studied.

Uncertainties of Benefit:
• The efficacy of bexagliflozin for pediatric patients with T2DM

• Pivotal trial populations underrepresented some racial

approved maximum dose of 8 mg daily. The impact on
noninferiority conclusions is unknown.
• It is unknown if a bexagliflozin dose higher than 20 mg would
lead to further improvement in efficacy and acceptable safety
as a higher dose was not tested in the phase 2 or phase 3
trials.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
The Applicant submitted six adequate and well-controlled investigations that establish clinical benefit for bexagliflozin for improving
glycemic control in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus, including four placebo-controlled trials and two active-controlled trials.
The four placebo-controlled trials (C-419, C-448, C-450, and C-476) demonstrated superiority of bexagliflozin to placebo for the
primary endpoint of change in HbA1c, from baseline to Week 24. Assessing change in HbA1c from baseline over at least 24 weeks
relative to a control is the standard approach to showing benefit for a glycemic control indication for type 2 diabetes mellitus. HbA1c
reflects average glucose control over the preceding 2-3 months and is an established surrogate for reduction in microvascular
complications. Reductions in HbA1c were both statistically and clinically significant. Bexagliflozin was statistically superior to
placebo as monotherapy, with a placebo-adjusted change from baseline in HbA1c of -0.4% (-0.6%, -0.1%; p=0.002) in C-450, and as
add-on to metformin, with a placebo-adjusted change from baseline in HbA1c of -0.5% (-0.7%, -0.3%; p<0.0001) in C-419. In
addition, bexagliflozin was statistically superior to placebo on change from baseline in HbA1c to Week 24 in patients with increased
CV risk on standard of care therapy for glycemic control (placebo-adjusted change from baseline of -0.4% [-0.5%, -0.4%; p<0.0001])
in C-476. The observed treatment effect is comparable to that of some of the other approved oral agents for glycemic control in
patients with T2DM. Reduction in microvascular disease in T2DM is proportional to reduction in HbA1c.
While statistically significant, glycemic efficacy was more modest in C-448, the trial of patients with moderate renal impairment (i.e.,
eGFR ≥30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2), with a placebo-adjusted change from baseline in HbA1c of -0.3% (-0.4%, -0.1%; p=0.002).
Bexagliflozin also performed comparably to sitagliptin and glimepiride, two other approved treatments for type 2 diabetes in two
active-controlled trials (C-423 and C-480). Trial C-423 compared bexagliflozin to sitagliptin at the recommended dose for patients
without renal impairment (100 mg). Trial C-480 compared bexagliflozin to glimepiride titrated from 2 mg up to a maximum dose of
6 mg. In C-423 the estimated treatment difference versus sitagliptin was 0.1 (-0.1, 0.2). In C-480 the estimated treatment versus
glimepiride was 0.0 (-0.2, 0.1). In both trials, the upper bound of the 95% confidence interval did not exceed the prespecified noninferiority margin of 0.35. One limitation is that the glimepiride comparator was not titrated up to the maximum U.S. approved daily
dose of 8 mg and glimepiride was not titrated after Week 6 in the 60-week trial.
Bexagliflozin use was associated with statistically significant reductions in body weight compared to placebo in both the renal
impairment trial (C-448) and the increased CV risk trial (C-476). In C-476, bexagliflozin was also associated with reduction in cuff
systolic blood pressure versus placebo.
Safety findings were generally consistent with those of other SGLT2 inhibitor products and can be adequately mitigated by labeling,
including with a Medication Guide for patients, as has been done with the approved SGLT2 inhibitors.
Consistent with other approved products in the SGLT2 inhibitor class, the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling will include
the risk of ketoacidosis, urosepsis and pyelonephritis, Fournier’s gangrene, genital mycotic infections, volume depletion, and
12
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

hypoglycemia with concomitant use of insulin or an insulin secretagogue. In the clinical trials, a higher incidence of subjects assigned
to bexagliflozin than placebo experienced one or more events of urinary tract infections, genital mycotic infections, volume depletion,
and hypoglycemia. Events of ketoacidosis were observed in clinical trials with bexagliflozin. Fournier’s gangrene is a rare, serious
event associated with the class.
Lower limb amputation is a recognized complication of diabetes mellitus, and an increased risk of lower limb amputation has been
identified with some, but not all, members of the SGLT2 inhibitor class. Data from the increased CV risk trial (C-476) indicated an
increased incidence of amputation with bexagliflozin treatment as compared to placebo (HR: 1.64 (0.70, 3.82)). The risk of
amputation is higher in patients with a baseline history of prior amputation, PAD, and neuropathy, compared to patients without such
disease history. The majority of amputations in both groups in C-476 were below-the-knee. Description of the observed imbalance in
amputation warrants inclusion in labeling.
Given the more modest treatment effect on HbA1c in patients with renal impairment in trial C-448, the safety of bexagliflozin in
subjects with moderate renal impairment was considered a key review issue. Overall, considering the safety data from both the
dedicated renal impairment trial (C-448) and the subgroup form the increased CV risk trial (C-476), no new safety issues were
identified in subjects with stage 3 CKD, and the benefits outweigh the risks in this population.
Changes in laboratory parameters in C-448 were generally consistent with data from other trials of bexagliflozin. The magnitude of
transient increases in serum creatinine and corresponding decreases in eGFR was greater in trial C-448. Communication in labeling
can adequately inform prescribers regarding these changes in laboratory parameters.
In conclusion, the applicant demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness to support approval of the application. The benefits of
treatment with bexagliflozin on glycemic control outweigh the potential risks in the indicated population when bexagliflozin is used
according to the approved labeling.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
TheracosBio LLC (hereafter referred to as the Applicant) has developed the sodium-glucose
cotransporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitor bexagliflozin as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve
glycemic control in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). The SGLT2 protein is the
predominant transporter responsible for reabsorption of glucose from the glomerular filtrate back
into circulation. By inhibiting SGLT2, bexagliflozin reduces renal reabsorption of filtered
glucose and lowers the renal threshold for glucose, thereby increasing urinary glucose excretion.
The proposed marketed product is a blue tablet containing 20 mg of bexagliflozin. The tablet is
to be taken once daily with or without food. There are currently four FDA-approved SGLT2
inhibitors indicated for improved glycemic control in adults with T2DM. Bexagliflozin is a new
molecular entity (NME), and the Applicant is submitting this new drug application (NDA) using
the 505(b)(1) approval pathway.

3.1. Review Issue List
The review team identified four main review issues that had a significant impact of the overall
determination of approvability. In depth analyses of these key review issues can be found in
Sections 6.3 and 7.7.

3.1.1. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Benefit
3.1.1.1. Efficacy of Bexagliflozin in Subjects With Moderate
Renal Impairment
See Section 6.3.1.

3.1.2. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
3.1.2.1. Increased Risk of Nontraumatic Amputation With
Bexagliflozin Treatment
See Section 7.7.1.
3.1.2.2. Safety of Bexagliflozin in Subjects With Moderate
Renal Impairment
See Section 7.7.2.
14
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

3.1.2.3. Safety Database Quality Concerns
See Section 7.7.3.

3.2. Approach to the Review
Six adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trials conducted in subjects with T2DM provide
substantial evidence of effectiveness for the pursued glycemic control indication (Trials C-419,
C-423, C-448, C-450, C-476, and C-480; Table 3). The NDA also included a phase 2b dosefinding trial (C-449) to support dose selection and a phase 2b trial that utilized a noncommercial
formulation of bexagliflozin (C-418). Other trials completed in the clinical development program
either did not have a sufficient treatment duration, such that they would contribute meaningfully
to the assessment of efficacy, did not enroll subjects with T2DM, or were conducted for the
evaluation of bexagliflozin for the treatment of essential hypertension (e.g., C-603A, C-603B).
The safety evaluation for this Application was based on the review of pooled safety data from
three placebo-controlled trials enrolling subjects who were either treatment naïve or inadequately
controlled on metformin (C-419, C-449, and C-450), termed the placebo-controlled pool, and the
review of phase 3 trial data from active-controlled trials (C-423 and C-480) and trials enrolling
specific populations (C-448 and C-476). The placebo-controlled pool was utilized to evaluate the
safety and tolerability of 20 mg bexagliflozin relative to placebo in a similar patient population
and was used to inform commonly occurring adverse reactions in Section 6 of the labeling. Trial
C-448, which enrolled subjects with stage 3 chronic kidney disease (CKD), and Trial C-476,
which enrolled subjects with increased cardiovascular (CV) risk, were evaluated independently
to assess safety in these distinct patient populations. Trial C-476 was of special interest in this
safety review, as it was the largest (1,699 subjects in the safety population) and longest (median
duration 2.4 years) trial in the bexagliflozin clinical program. It therefore provided the most
robust assessment for various adverse events of special interest (AESIs). Analyses from C-448
and C-476 are presented independently and used to inform labeling where indicated.

SGLT2 is the predominant transporter responsible for reabsorption of glucose from the glomerular

Drug Information
Absorption
Absolute bioavailability of bexagliflozin was not determined. The tablet formulation had about 16% lower AUC and 55%
lower Cmax compared to the immediate release capsule formulation that was used in early phase studies (see Section
14.2.6).
2-4 hours following oral administration
High fat meal delayed bexagliflozin Tmax by 1-2 hours and increased Bexagliflozin Cmax by 31% (90% CI: 17%, 48%). There
is no significant impact on bexagliflozin AUC.
Distribution
The apparent volume of distribution is approximately 300 L in healthy subjects
Bexagliflozin is approximately 93% bound to plasma protein. Neither renal nor hepatic impairment substantially alters protein
binding
Bexagliflozin is a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP).
Elimination
91.6% of total radioactivity was recovered, 51.1% in feces, predominantly as bexagliflozin, and 40.5% in urine, largely as the
3′-O-glucuronide metabolite.
The apparent oral clearance is 19 L/h by population pharmacokinetic modeling.
The apparent elimination half-life was about 12 h.
Bexagliflozin is mainly metabolized by UGT1A9 and, to a lesser extent, CYP3A.
The proportion of radioactivity recovered as bexagliflozin in urine and feces was 1.5% and 28.7%, respectively.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Based on a population PK analysis, body weight does not have a clinically relevant effect on the PK of bexagliflozin.
Based on a population PK analysis, age does not have a clinically relevant effect on the PK of bexagliflozin.
In T2DM patients with mild (eGFR: 60-89 mL/min/1.73 m2), moderate (eGFR: 30-59 mL/min/1.73 m2), and severe renal
impairment (eGFR: 15-29 mL/min/1.73 m2), the AUC of bexagliflozin increased by 6.8%, 33.8% and 53.9%, respectively,
compared to T2DM patients with normal renal function. The Cmax was not affected. Consistent with the mechanism of action,
the 24-hour urinary glucose excretion in T2DM patients with mild, moderate, and severe renal impairment was 17%, 60%,
and 83% lower, respectively, than in T2DM patients with normal renal function.
Based on safety and efficacy data in patients with moderate renal impairment, no dose adjustment is needed in patients with
mild and moderate renal impairment (i.e., eGFR ≥30 mL/min/1.73 m2). Bexagliflozin is not recommended in patients with
severe renal impairment, as it is not expected to be effective in these patients (See Sections 6.3 and 8.1).
The use of bexagliflozin has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment. In subjects with moderate hepatic
impairment, the AUC of bexagliflozin increased by 28.3%, and Cmax increased by 6.3% compared to subjects with normal
hepatic function. No dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild and moderate hepatic impairment.

5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Bexagliflozin is an inhibitor of SGLT2 indicated for the treatment of T2DM. SGLT2 is a
transmembrane protein that actively cotransports extracellular sodium and hexose sugars into
cells by relying on the electrochemical potential of sodium ions across the renal tubular (apical)
cell membrane. Inhibition of SGLT2 prevents glucose reuptake from the renal proximal tubule,
leading to glucosuria and a reduction in plasma glucose concentration.
Figure 1. Bexagliflozin Mechanism of Action

Source: (Fioretto et al. 2015)
Abbreviations: SGLT; sodium-glucose cotransporter.

Nonclinical evidence of effectiveness was obtained from in vitro studies evaluating the activity
of bexagliflozin on human SGLT2, and from in vivo studies evaluating levels of glucosuria,
blood glucose, and hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) in healthy animals and/or diabetic animal models.
In vitro, bexagliflozin selectively inhibited human SGLT2 as compared to SGLT1 with a half
maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) of 2 nM and 5.6 µM, respectively, representing a ~2,400fold selectivity for SGLT2 over SGLT1 (Table 6).
Table 6. Activity and Selectivity of Bexagliflozin

Source: Applicant’s submission
Abbreviations: Bexagliflozin; IC50, half maximal inhibitory concentration; SGLT; sodium-glucose cotransporter.

In vivo, bexagliflozin elicited dose-dependent glucosuria in normal rats, dogs, and monkeys at
exposures equivalent to the clinical dose, with a half maximal effective dose (ED50) ranging
between 0.14 and 0.38 mg/kg (Figure 2).
24
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Figure 2. Bexagliflozin-Induced Glucosuria in Healthy Animals

Source: Applicant’s submission
Abbreviations: ED50, half maximal effective dose; UGE, urinary glucose excretion.

Secondary to glucosuria, bexagliflozin reduced plasma glucose (by area under the concentrationtime curve [AUC]) in rats and dogs following an oral glucose challenge (Table 7 and Table 8).
Table 7. Blood Glucose Reduction in Rats

Source: Applicant’s submission
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve.

Table 8. Blood Glucose Reduction in Dogs

Source: Applicant’s submission

In diabetic mouse (db/db) and rat (ZDF) models, bexagliflozin 0.1 mg/kg to 3 mg/kg dosedependently reduced HbA1c after four weeks of oral treatment at exposures equivalent to the
clinical dose (Figure 3).

6. Assessment of Effectiveness
6.1. Dose and Dose Responsiveness
The Applicant’s proposed dose for bexagliflozin is 20 mg once daily, taken in the morning with
or without food. The proposed dosing regimen is acceptable for approval based on a favorable
efficacy and safety profile.
We had recommended the evaluation of two dosage strengths in the phase 3 program (meeting
minutes dated September 21, 2016); however, only a 20 mg dosage strength was evaluated. It is
unclear whether a higher dosage strength could provide additional glycemic control without
significant additional tolerability-related events.
The selection of the 20-mg dose was primarily based on the HbA1c reduction from baseline in
T2DM subjects from a 12-week, phase 2, dose-ranging study (C-449). In the study, subjects with
T2DM received placebo or bexagliflozin doses ranging from 5 to 20 mg. A dose-response
relationship across bexagliflozin dosages of 5 mg to 20 mg was observed, and the unadjusted
mean changes from baseline in HbA1c at Week 12 were +0.23%, -0.28%, -0.40%, and -0.48% in
the placebo, 5 mg, 10 mg, and 20 mg groups, respectively (Figure 4). A maximum drug effect
(Emax) model was fitted to the observed data with an estimated maximum mean reduction (95%
CI) of -0.82% (-0.51%, -1.1%) and an estimated ED50 (95% CI) of 3.0 mg (0, 7.3 mg). After
adjusting for placebo mean changes from baseline, 20 mg bexagliflozin was predicted to achieve
76.8% of the maximum pharmacological effect in T2DM subjects. Additionally, the placeboadjusted least-square mean treatment differences in HbA1c at Week 12 were -0.55%, -0.68%,
and -0.80% for 5 mg, 10 mg, and 20 mg, respectively (refer to Section 11.5 in the C-449 study
report). The reduction in HbA1c at the proposed dose of 20 mg was considered statistically
significant and clinically meaningful.

The potential effectiveness of the 20-mg dose was also supported by dose-response relationships
of 24-hour urinary glucose excretion (UGE; PD effect of glucosuria) in healthy subjects and
fasting plasma glucose in subjects with T2DM (C-445 and C-402). The mean change in UGE as
a function of dose ranging from 5 mg to 90 mg was well fit by an Emax model (Figure 5). The
estimated maximum UGE was 80.7 g per day (95% CI: 71.7 to 89.8) and the estimated ED50 was
5.2 mg (95% CI: 2.8 to 7.6). A dose of 20 mg was predicted to achieve 79% of the maximum
pharmacological effect in nondiabetic subjects. The changes in fasting plasma glucose (FPG)
were determined in T2DM subjects at bexagliflozin doses ranging from 5 mg to 50 mg in a 28day study (Figure 6). Based on the fitted Emax model, the 20-mg dose is expected to produce
~90% of the maximum effect in lowering FPG.

6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate
Efficacy
6.2.1. Study Design
Overview
The six phase 3 trials used to support efficacy of bexagliflozin were all multicenter, randomized,
double-blinded, controlled trials that met the regulatory standards for adequate and wellcontrolled studies. These studies included four placebo-controlled trials and two active28
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

controlled trials. The placebo-controlled trials evaluated bexagliflozin as monotherapy (C-450),
as add-on to metformin (C-419), as add-on to standard antihyperglycemic medications in
subjects with stage 3 CKD (C-448), and as add-on to standard antihyperglycemic medications in
subjects with increased CV risk (C-476). The active-controlled trials compared bexagliflozin to
glimepiride (C-480) or sitagliptin (C-423) as add-on to metformin. All trials were completed at
the time of this NDA review. The Applicant states that all clinical trials were conducted in
compliance with good clinical practice, applicable laws, and regulations, and in accordance with
the Declaration of Helsinki (1996). Protocol synopses for each trial are presented in Section 15.1.

Objectives
The stated primary objective of all phase 3 trials was to assess the effect of bexagliflozin relative
to comparator (placebo or active) on the change in HbA1c from baseline to the primary
assessment timepoint (Week 24 in C-419, C-450, C-423, C-448, and C-476, and Week 60 in C480). Key secondary objectives differed between trials but were generally related to evaluation
of the effect of bexagliflozin on body weight, systolic blood pressure (SBP), and/or FPG.

Trial Design
The following sections present an overview of noteworthy trial design features in the phase 3
clinical program.
Run-In Period
All trials included a one- or two-week, single-blind, placebo run-in period to assess compliance
and uncontrolled hyperglycemia prior to randomization. At the start of the run-in period, subjects
received diet and exercise counseling and were provided with a glucometer and instructions for
daily use. Subjects were not eligible for randomization if more than one dose (or two doses in C480) of run-in medication was missed. Subjects were also generally not eligible for enrollment if
they experienced uncontrolled hyperglycemia during the run-in period (e.g., FPG ≥250 mg/dL on
two or more consecutive days, signs and symptoms of uncontrolled hyperglycemia).
Study Treatments
Of the six phase 3, pivotal trials, four trials were placebo-controlled (C-419, C-450, C-448, C476) and two trials were active-controlled (C-480 utilized glimepiride and C-423 utilized
sitagliptin as the active-comparator treatments). All trials evaluated bexagliflozin 20 mg utilizing
the to-be-commercialized tablet formulation.
Dose Modification of Study Treatments
As the Applicant only proposes to commercialize one dosage strength (20 mg) of bexagliflozin,
no dose modification for bexagliflozin occurred in any trial.
In C-423, subjects assigned to the active comparator group received sitagliptin 100 mg.
In C-480, optimal titration of glimepiride was required, and a titration algorithm was detailed in
the study protocol. Briefly, subjects who successfully completed the run-in period were
randomized to receive either bexagliflozin tablets with placebo glimepiride capsules (Group 1) or
placebo bexagliflozin tablets and active glimepiride capsules (Group 2). Subjects were to receive
a starting dose of 2 mg glimepiride or placebo glimepiride at randomization. At Weeks 2, 4, and
29
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

6, subjects had in-clinic visits for the assessment of glimepiride up-titration and safety
evaluation. Glimepiride doses were increased if a subject met the following two criteria:
(1) ≥50% of documented fasting self-monitoring blood glucose (SMBG) measurements
˃110 mg/dL
(2) No severe or documented symptomatic hypoglycemia events (defined as an event during
which typical symptoms of hypoglycemia are accompanied by a measured plasma
glucose concentration ≤70 mg/dL)
Subjects were advised to contact the clinical site in the case of persistent hyperglycemia (more
than three consecutive daily fasting SMBG measures ≥270 mg/dL) such that glimepiride doses
could be increased earlier than the next scheduled study visit. After Week 6 of treatment, no
further increases in glimepiride were to occur, and rescue medication could be prescribed by the
investigator if deemed necessary for the well-being of the subject. As C-480 was double-blinded,
subjects assigned to receive placebo glimepiride received mock titrations at Weeks 2, 4, and 6
based on the noted glycemic criteria.
In all trials that allowed background antihyperglycemic therapy (C-419, C-423, C-448, C-476, C480), dosing was to remain stable over the course of the trial unless a change was deemed
necessary for the well-being of the subject (see “Rescue Medication” below). Protocols stated
that changes to dosage and administration that occurred during trials should be documented.
Stratification
Stratification factors varied by trial as summarized below:

C-419: HbA1c ≤8.5 or not; United States or Japan clinical site
C-423: HbA1c ≤8.5 or not
C-448: HbA1c ≤8.5 or not; baseline insulin use or not; estimated glomerular filtration
rate (eGFR) 30 to 44 or 45 to 59 mL/min/1.73 m2
C-450: HbA1c ≤8.5 or not; treatment-naïve or not
C-476: HbA1c ≤9.5 or not; body mass index (BMI) <25 kg/m2 or not; eGFR
<60 mL/min/1.73 m2 or not; history of heart failure (HF) or not
C-480: HbA1c ≤8.5 or not; metformin use only or metformin use +1 additional
antihyperglycemic agent; eGFR ≥90 mL/min/1.73 m2 or not

Rescue Medication
Subjects were to receive counseling regarding appropriate diet and exercise to aid in glycemic
control throughout all phase 3 trials. In addition, subjects were advised to take daily fasting
SMBG measurements. Rescue medication was recommended if, after diet and exercise
counseling, subjects had uncontrolled hyperglycemia. While specific rescue criteria differed by
trial,1 criteria were generally as follows:
(1) More than three consecutive, daily fasting SMBG measures of ≥270 mg/dL from baseline
to Week 6, ≥240 mg/dL from Week 6 to Week 12, or ≥200 mg/dL from Week 12 to end
of treatment.
(2) Fasting SMBG values ≥250 mg/dL and associated with clinical signs or symptoms of
hyperglycemia (e.g., weight loss, blurred vision, increased thirst, increased urination, or
fatigue), and the signs or symptoms are severe.
In C-476, subjects were to be managed with diet and exercise counseling or rescue medication (if
above criteria were met) for the first 24 weeks. After the primary endpoint measurement at Week
24, changes to the medical therapy for hyperglycemia could occur at investigator discretion
based on SMBG, FPG, and HbA1c information using local standards of care for subjects with
T2DM.
Study Endpoints
The primary endpoint in all phase 3 trials was change from baseline in HbA1c to the prespecified
timepoint at Week 24 (except C-480, at Week 60). HbA1c is a surrogate endpoint for
microvascular disease risk reduction and is a validated surrogate endpoint (FDA 2022) to support
glycemic control indications in patients with diabetes. In the bexagliflozin clinical program,
HbA1c was measured using a high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) method certified
to be Level 1 by the National Glycohemoglobin Standardization Program (NGSP) and
standardized to the Diabetes Control and Complications Trial (DCCT) reference assay.
A summary of secondary endpoints as described in the statistical analysis plan (SAP) for each
trial is presented in Table 10. To control for type I error, a hierarchical testing approach was
carried out for the testing of secondary endpoints. No formal hypothesis testing occurred for
secondary efficacy endpoints in C-419 and it is thus excluded from Table 10. Secondary
endpoints evaluated for exploratory purposes in C-419 included changes in FPG, SBP, body
weight, and proportion of subjects with HbA1c ≤7% over time.

1

C-419 and C-423 also recommended rescue treatment if HbA1c was >8.0% between weeks 12-24. C-450 did not
specify a minimum fasting SMBG values or specify “severe” signs and symptoms for rescue criteria to be met for
criterium #2. In C-476, two consecutive, daily, fasting SMBG values above the noted thresholds could prompt
rescue treatment. C-476 also did not include rescue criteria regarding clinical signs and symptoms of hyperglycemia.

6.2.2. Statistical Analysis Plan
This section provides a brief description of key SAP elements. Please see Section 12.2 for a
detailed description of the SAP and its development history.

Analysis Set for Efficacy
The intent-to-treat (ITT) analysis set included all subjects who were randomized and was
preplanned to serve as the primary set for the efficacy analyses. All analyses of the ITT analysis
set were based on the randomization assignment.

Primary Analysis Method for the Primary and Key Secondary Efficacy
Endpoints
During the IND stage, the Applicant first proposed an LOCF analysis, and then an MMRM
method for the primary analysis of the primary or secondary endpoints with missing data. FDA
continuously advised against the LOCF, and the MMRM approach that does not appropriately
address missing data, as it treats the behavior of missing data for those subjects who are offtreatment to be the same as that of observed data for those subjects who are on-treatment in the
same treatment group. The Applicant’s prespecified primary estimand for the bexagliflozin
T2DM HbA1c studies is the treatment policy estimand, which is FDA’s preferred estimand. An
appropriate statistical method that aligns with the treatment policy estimand in handling missing
data is the retrieved- dropout-based pattern mixture multiple imputation method (MI-RD). To
35
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

estimate the treatment policy estimand, efficacy data should be collected and utilized in analyses
on all randomized treated subjects regardless of treatment adherence, rescue status, or other post
baseline status. Missing data should be imputed in a fashion consistent with what the
measurement would have been, had it been measured.
Disagreeing with the proposed MMRM approach in the Applicant’s preNDA meeting package
(meeting date December 5, 2019), FDA recommended that where there are a sufficient number
of retrieved dropouts (subjects who discontinued treatment before the final assessment window
but still had an efficacy measurement in the final assessment window). FDA prefers a multiple
imputation approach that models the missing final assessment of nonretrieved dropouts (subjects
who discontinued treatment before the final assessment window and did not have a measurement
in this assessment window) based on known measurements from the retrieved dropouts. The
Applicant’s pivotal trials were all completed and unblinded (database locked at on November 22,
2019) at the time of the preNDA meeting. Clinical study reports (CSRs) had been finalized
before the FDA’s specific recommendation of retrieved drop-out approach or return-to-baseline
approach (if insufficient retrieved dropouts) analysis advice was delivered. Across the six HbA1c
studies, an insufficient number of retrieved dropouts rendered the MI-RD approach analysis not
feasible. Following the FDA’s recommendation at a preNDA meeting, the Applicant used the
multiple imputation based on return-to-baseline approach (MI-RTB) in handling missing data as
post-hoc analyses. As analysis methods have been continuously recommended by FDA to
estimate the appropriate treatment effect for evaluation of efficacy, these post-hoc analyses were
acceptable.
A three-step MI-RTB plus analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) approach was adopted by the
Applicant as the primary analysis method for both the primary and key secondary efficacy
endpoints; the same method was used by the FDA statistical reviewer in this NDA review. The
three steps are:
(1) MI-RTB to impute missing data: based on the baseline of the subject for an endpoint,
with variance estimated from an ANCOVA model of nonmissing Week 24 (Week 60 for
Study C-480) HbA1c for the treatment arms. The ANCOVA model will include the same
variables as the primary analysis model. The variance, 𝜎 2 , for the Week 24 (Week 60 for
Study C-480) data for each treatment arm will be used as the variance in an imputation
model 𝑋𝑊𝑒𝑒𝑘 24 𝑜𝑟 60 ~ 𝑁(𝑋𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒𝑙𝑖𝑛𝑒 , 𝜎 2 ) to impute the missing Week 24 (Week 60 for
Study C-480) data.
(2) ANCOVA model analysis: for each iteration of the multiply imputed full dataset, the
primary and secondary endpoints were analyzed using an ANCOVA model with
treatment and stratification factors as fixed effects and the corresponding baseline value
as a covariate. The least squares (LS) mean differences between bexagliflozin 20 mg and
a comparator in each study, with the associated two-sided 95% CIs were estimated from
the ANCOVA model.
(3) Rubin’s rule to combine the results: Rubin’s rule was applied to combine and synthesize
the results from step 2 to estimate an overall treatment effect and corresponding
variability.

36
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Noninferiority Margin for the Active-Controlled Trials
For the active-controlled trials C-423 and C-480, a noninferiority margin of 0.35% was
preplanned by the Applicant and agreed upon by the FDA review team at the clinical
development stage. See the Applicant’s justifications for the margin selection in Section 12.2.

Multiple Testing Procedures
In each of the six pivotal studies, the study SAP prespecified a hierarchical testing sequence of
primary endpoint and key secondary endpoints to control for study-wise type I error due to
multiplicity. For each endpoint in the sequence, a two-sided statistical test was to be conducted at
the 5.0% significance level. In each study, if the primary endpoint met the statistical significance,
then the key secondary endpoints were to be tested sequentially in the order as listed in Table 10
and Table 136 [Section 16].

Tipping Point Sensitivity Analyses for the Primary Efficacy Endpoints
For each of the four superiority trials (C-450, C-419, C-448, and C-476), a two-dimensional
delta-based tipping point analysis was carried out to assess the robustness of inferences from the
primary analysis method to deviations from its underlying assumptions, targeting the same
primary estimand.
For each of the two active-controlled trials (C-423 and C-480), the FDA statistical reviewer
performed a one-dimensional tipping point analysis: based on the RTB-MI imputed datasets,
penalizing the missing outcomes on the bexagliflozin arm by applying a delta of size equal to the
null hypothesis of the noninferiority test (i.e., the noninferiority margin of 0.35%) while keeping
the MI-RTB imputed active-control arm missing outcomes unchanged.

Subgroup Analyses
For each of the preplanned subgrouping factors (age, gender, race, region and baseline HbA1c
for all studies, see Section 16 preplanned subgroups of each individual study), a by-subgroup
analysis was firstly performed for each subgroup level using the primary analysis method (with
modifications on covariates when necessary) to derive a sample estimate of the subgroup
treatment effect; subsequently, a Bayesian hierarchical model was used to derive a shrinkage
estimate of the subgroup treatment effect using the sample estimates. Forests plots are used to
present both the “sample” and “shrinkage” estimates and corresponding confidence intervals.
To investigate for potential effect modification of baseline HbA1c levels, an additional
exploratory simple linear regression analysis treating baseline HbA1c as a continuous variable
was carried out for each of the four placebo-controlled studies. The response variable was the
change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24, the predictor variables included treatment, baseline
HbA1c levels, and a treatment by baseline HbA1c levels interaction. The size and the
significance level of the interaction term (difference in by-arm baseline HbA1c slopes) from this
model fitting is an indicator of the potential modification effect.

37
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

6.2.3. Results of Analyses
6.2.3.1. Demographics, Baseline Characteristics, and
Disposition
6.2.3.1.1. Demographics and Baseline Characteristics
Relevant demographics and baseline clinical characteristics of the pivotal efficacy trials are
shown in Table 11. Characteristics between drug and comparator groups were generally balanced
in all trials. Trials enrolling subjects who were either treatment-naïve or inadequately controlled
on metformin (i.e., C-419, C-423, C-450, C-480) were generally similar, with a mean age of
approximately 55 to 60 years and an average diabetes duration of approximately 9 years. In
contrast, subjects enrolled in C-448 and C-476 were older (average age of approximately 65 to
70 years) with a longer duration of diabetes (approximately 15 years) with a significant amount
of baseline insulin use (approximately 50 to 55%). Proportions of male and female subjects
varied by trial. Trial C-476, which evaluated subjects with increased risk for MACE, enrolled a
majority of male subjects (approximately 70%). The majority of subjects in most trials were
White, although C-419 and C-448 enrolled approximately 50% and 39% of Asian subjects,
respectively. All trials predominantly enrolled non-Hispanic/Latino subjects.

Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

6.2.3.1.2. Disposition
Analysis populations and subject disposition from the efficacy trials is summarized in Table 12.
Screened subjects were eligible for randomization if study-specific inclusion/exclusion criteria
were met and if the subject successfully completed the run-in period. The most common reasons
subjects were not eligible for randomization varied by trial but included having screening HbA1c
<7% and not meeting the eligibility criteria for renal function.
Approximately 90 to 95% of the subjects completed each study (Table 12). The trials with the
least completers were C-419 and C-480, with about 90% of subjects in both trials completing
treatment in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively. While these trials had fewer
completers as compared to other trials in the clinical program, the completion rate is considered
acceptable, and it is reassuring that no imbalances in completers between treatment groups was
observed. While data are limited, there were no discernable trends in reasons for study
discontinuation between treatment arms and study. Imbalances in subjects lost to follow-up were
observed in some trials (e.g., seven and one subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups
were lost to follow up, respectively, in C-450); however, this finding was not observed
consistently, thus limiting interpretability. See Section 16.4 for additional information on
disposition for each trial.

6.2.3.2. Primary Efficacy Results, Sensitivity, and Subgroup
Analyses
6.2.3.2.1. Primary Efficacy Results
Table 13 summarizes FDA’s primary analysis results for the six pivotal efficacy studies in the
bexagliflozin clinical development program. Additional detailed information on the efficacy
analysis can be found in Section 16.
Superiority of bexagliflozin to placebo was demonstrated for the primary endpoint of change in
HbA1c from baseline to Week 24 for the four placebo-controlled trials (C-419, C-448, C-450,
and C-476). Reductions in HbA1c were both statistically and clinically significant. Bexagliflozin
was statistically superior to placebo as monotherapy, with a placebo-adjusted change from
baseline of -0.38% (-0.63%, -0.13%; p=0.002) in C-450, and as add-on to metformin, with a
placebo-adjusted change from baseline of -0.48% (-0.69%, -0.26%; p<0.0001). In addition,
bexagliflozin was statistically superior to placebo from baseline to Week 24 in subjects with
increased CV risk on standard of care therapy for glycemic control (placebo-adjusted change
from baseline of -0.44% [-0.53%, -0.35%; p<0.0001]). While statistically significant, glycemic
efficacy was more modest in C-448, enrolling patients with moderate renal impairment (i.e.,
eGFR ≥30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2), with a placebo-adjusted change from baseline of -0.27% (0.44%, -0.09%; p=0.002). Efficacy in this patient population is discussed in additional detail in
Section 6.3.1.
Noninferiority was achieved for the two active-controlled trials (C-423 and C-480). Trial C-423
compared bexagliflozin to sitagliptin at the recommended dose for patients without renal
impairment (100 mg). Trial C-480 compared bexagliflozin to glimepiride 2, 4, or 6 mg (see
Section 6.2 for additional details regarding how glimepiride was titrated in this study). Of note,
the maximal approved dose of glimepiride in the US is 8 mg, and 81% of subjects in the
glimepiride group received up-titration to 6 mg, suggesting that additional up-titration could
have occurred if an 8 mg dosage strength had been incorporated into this trial. The impact of
additional glimepiride titration (i.e., up to 8 mg) on the overall results of this trial is unclear.

Abbreviations: Bexa, bexagliflozin; CI, confidence interval; Ctrl, control; LS, least squares; Met, metformin; SE, standard error;
SOC, standard of care

To date, no trials have been conducted to directly compare the efficacy of bexagliflozin to
another approved SGLT2 inhibitor. Cross-trial comparison of data suggest that the efficacy of
bexagliflozin may be more modest than other approved SGLT2 inhibitors. For example, when
comparing data from monotherapy trials presented in Section 14 of the USPI, placebo-adjusted
change from baseline in HbA1c from baseline to 24 to 26 weeks was 0.7% for ertugliflozin 15
mg, 0.7% for dapagliflozin 10 mg, 1.2% for canagliflozin 300 mg, and 0.9% for empagliflozin
25 mg as compared to 0.4% for bexagliflozin 20 mg (Janssen 2020; AstraZeneca 2021; Merck
2021; Ingelheim 2022). However, cross-trial comparisons should be approached with caution as
differing trial designs and patient populations may influence the data observed in any given trial.
In addition, FDA’s approach to the preferred statistical analysis of trials to determine the most
appropriate estimate of the treatment effect closer to realistic effectiveness has evolved over
time, further limiting meaningful conclusions from cross-trial comparisons. (e.g., use of LOCF
versus return to baseline for missing data imputation based on the most relevant assumption of
missing data considering reasons of the missing data). Furthermore, other SGLT2 inhibitor
programs evaluated two dosage strengths in phase 3 as compared to bexagliflozin which only
evaluated a 20 mg dosage strength. It is unclear whether additional glycemic efficacy could be
achieved with a higher bexagliflozin dose without an unacceptable increase in tolerability events.
Ultimately, it is unclear at this time if, and to what extent, bexagliflozin may be less efficacious
than other approved products.
6.2.3.2.2. Sensitivity Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
For each of the superiority trials, tipping points were determined at the values of delta
combinations where the statistical significance of treatment effect is lost; none of these derived
delta combinations were clinically plausible (see result tables in Section 16.6). For each of the
noninferiority trials, penalizing the missing outcomes on the bexagliflozin arm by applying a
delta of 0.35% while keeping the MI-RTB imputed active-control arm missing outcomes
unchanged, did not change the conclusion of noninferiority. These sensitivity analyses and
results support the robustness of the conclusions based on primary analysis of HbA1c changes
across the six pivotal trials.
6.2.3.2.3. Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
Subgroup analyses of the primary endpoint were conducted for all trials and are presented
individually in Section 16.4. Subgroup analysis results were generally consistent across trials to
demonstrate homogeneity of the favorable result for bexagliflozin, although limited data in
44

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

certain subgroups impeded interpretability in some cases. Shrinkage estimates show narrower
credible intervals than confidence intervals of sample estimates. The point estimate of the
Shrinkage estimates are closer to the point estimate for the overall treatment difference compared
to the sample estimates. No notable differences were detected using shrinkage estimates in all
trials.
The subgroup analysis from C-476 is presented here as a representative analysis because C-476
was the largest trial (ITT N=1700) and thus, provided more data contributing to each subgroup.
As shown in Table 14, the treatment effect of bexagliflozin showed similar results to the overall
population in all subgroups evaluated with the exception of a subgroup of subjects with renal
impairment (i.e., baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2). Efficacy in subjects with moderate renal
impairment is discussed further in Section 6.3.1.

6.2.3.3.1. Change in HbA1c (%) From Baseline Over Time
As discussed above, most trials evaluated the primary endpoint of change in HbA1c from
baseline to 24 weeks, with active-controlled trial C-480 evaluating change from baseline in
HbA1c at 60 weeks. While the primary endpoint was evaluated at Week 60 in C-480 the total
trial duration was 96 weeks. Likewise, while the primary endpoint was evaluated at Week 24 in
C-476, the trial duration was driven by the accrual of major adverse cardiac events (MACE+),
and the average treatment duration was 2.4 years. These trials provide supportive information of
changes in HbA1c (%) from baseline to longer time points for bexagliflozin.
Figure 7 and Figure 8 are presented for exploratory purposes to show changes in HbA1c (%)
from baseline over time using observed data only without consideration of missing data
imputation. As shown in Figure 7, in C-480, maximal HbA1c reduction occurred at week 12
followed by decreases in effect over time for both drugs. C-476 provides additional long-term
data for changes in HbA1c from baseline up to 168 weeks, although data at later timepoints are
limited (Figure 8). As shown in Figure 8, reduction in HbA1c is maintained from baseline to
approximately week 36, with a decline in treatment effect over time. However, all timepoints
show a greater treatment effect in the bexagliflozin group as compared to the placebo group.

6.3. Key Review Issue Relevant to Evaluation of
Benefit
6.3.1. Efficacy of Bexagliflozin in Subjects With
Moderate Renal Impairment
Background/Issue
SGLT2 inhibitor products were not initially recommended for use in patients with moderate
renal impairment due to diminished glycemic efficacy and safety concerns (e.g., increases in
serum creatinine/decreases in eGFR upon treatment initiation, acute kidney injury) leading to an
unfavorable benefit-risk assessment in this patient population. However, additional information
has been obtained regarding the safety and efficacy of SGLT2 inhibitors in patients with
moderate renal impairment, including the evaluation of these products in dedicated renal
impairment studies and long-term cardiovascular outcome trials (CVOTs). These trials have
supported expanded labeling for the use of SGLT2 inhibitors to improve glycemic control in
patients with moderate renal impairment. Currently, dosing and administration recommendations
for patients with renal impairment are product-specific based on the available clinical data. At
this time of this NDA review, ertugliflozin and dapagliflozin are not recommended for use to
improve glycemic control in patients with T2DM and an eGFR <45 mL/min/1.73 m2, and
canagliflozin and empagliflozin are not recommended for use to improve glycemic control in
patients T2DM and an eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m2 because studies to support efficacy in patients
with this level of renal impairment were not conducted or did not show statistically robust
results.
50

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

In the bexagliflozin clinical program, C-448 was a dedicated renal impairment trial enrolling
patients with eGFR ≥30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2. In addition, subjects were eligible for
enrollment in C-476 with eGFR ≥45 mL/min/1.73 m2. Subjects with moderate and severe renal
impairment (eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2) were excluded from all other trials in the bexagliflozin
clinical program and therefore Trials C-448 and C-476 are the only trials with sufficient
information to inform safety and efficacy in this patient population. While glycemic efficacy was
observed in C-448, efficacy was not observed in the subset of subjects with moderate renal
impairment in C-476, raising uncertainties about the efficacy of bexagliflozin in this patient
population. The efficacy of bexagliflozin in patients with moderate renal impairment is therefore
a key review issue.

Assessment
Overview
The assessment of renal efficacy focused on two trials: C-448, a dedicated renal impairment trial
enrolling patients with eGFR between ≥30 and <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 (i.e., stage 3a and 3b CKD),
and C-476, a trial enrolling subjects with increased CV risk that enrolled subjects with eGFR
≥45 mL/min/1.73 m2 (i.e., stage 3a CKD). In C-476, treatment was stratified by eGFR category
(eGFR <60 or ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m2), and approximately 20% of subjects in each treatment arm
had eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2. Baseline demographics for C-448 and C-476 are presented in
Table 11. All other trials excluded subjects with a screening eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and
were therefore not considered due to limited data. Renal efficacy data from C-448 and C-476 are
discussed independently below.
Trial C-448 in Patients With Moderate Renal Impairment
C-448 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group
study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of bexagliflozin 20 mg versus placebo in subjects with
T2DM, moderate renal impairment, and inadequate glycemic control. The study population
included subjects with T2DM, an HbA1c of 7.0 to 10.5% (inclusive), and stage 3 CKD as
defined by an eGFR ≥30 and <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 at the screening visit and at one additional
time of measurement between 1 and 12 months prior to screening, which could be obtained from
available medical records. Subjects were excluded if there was a >20% change in eGFR between
the historical value and the screening visit. Subjects on any antihyperglycemic regimen were
eligible for enrollment (with the exception of taking an SGLT2 inhibitor). At the time of
screening, the doses and frequency of all antihyperglycemic medications were to have been
stable for at least 8 weeks. See
APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL

51

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Table 9 for additional information on inclusion/exclusion criteria for this trial.
All eligible subjects entered a one-week single-blind, placebo run-in period to allow for
optimization of compliance with diet and exercise recommendations. Subjects who were
compliant in taking run-in medication were eligible for randomization. Randomization was
stratified by HbA1c (7.0 to 8.5% or 8.6 to 10.5%), baseline insulin use, and eGFR (30 to
44 mL/min/1.73 m2 or 45 to 59 mL/min/1.73 m2) at the screening visit.
Out of 490 screened subjects, 312 were randomized to either the bexagliflozin arm (n=157) or
the placebo arm (n=155). All randomized subjects (ITT) took at least one dose of investigational
product (safety population), and thus the ITT and safety analysis populations were the same. As
shown in Table 11, subjects enrolled in C-448 had a mean age of 69.6 (39 to 88) years, a mean
diabetes duration of 15.9 (0 to 50.2) years, and a mean baseline HbA1c of 8.0% (SD =0.8). Mean
baseline eGFR was 45.1 mL/min/1.73 m2. The majority of subjects were male (63%), White
(55%), and non-Hispanic/Latino (92%). Approximately 56% of subjects used insulin as part of
their regimen for glycemic control.
The results of the primary efficacy analysis (i.e., placebo-subtracted change in HbA1c from
baseline to Week 24), are presented in Table 159 and reproduced in Table 16 below.
Bexagliflozin demonstrated a modest, but statistically and clinically significant reduction in
HbA1c as compared to placebo (LS mean difference: -0.27%; 95% CI: -0.44%, -0.09%). In
accordance with these results, fewer subjects received rescue treatment for uncontrolled
hyperglycemia in the bexagliflozin group (9 subjects (5.7%)) as compared to the placebo group
(17 subjects (11.0%)), and rescue medication was prescribed to subjects in the bexagliflozin
group at a later mean date in the treatment period as compared to subjects in the placebo group
(mean time-to-first rescue medication of 84.3 versus 72.3 days in the bexagliflozin and placebo
groups, respectively; Applicant’s analysis). As demonstrated in Figure 9, the bexagliflozin group
consistently had a larger mean change in HbA1c over time as compared to placebo.
More modest glycemic efficacy of SGLT2 inhibitor products in patients with moderate renal
impairment is expected based on the mechanism of action of the drug class and has been
characterized with other approved products. As discussed above (Section 6.2.3.2.1), it is difficult
to draw meaningful conclusions from cross-trial comparisons as differences in study designs and
analysis methods may impact trial results. However, the reduction in HbA1c with bexagliflozin
treatment in C-448 is generally consistent with data available from other members of class. For
example, in a double-blind, 26-week, placebo-controlled trial enrolling 269 subjects with T2DM
and moderate renal impairment (eGFR 30 to 50 mL/min/1.73 m2), canagliflozin 100 mg and 300
mg demonstrated placebo-adjusted changes in HbA1c of -0.30% (95% CI: -0.53%; -0.07%) and 0.40% (95% CI: -0.64%; -0.17%), respectively (Janssen 2020). In VERTIS CV enrolling
subjects with T2DM and CVD, ertugliflozin demonstrated a placebo-adjusted change in HbA1c
of -0.28% (95% CI: -0.4, -0.15) in a subgroup of subjects with baseline eGFR of 45 to 59
mL/min/1.73 m2 (see clinical review in DARRTS dated September 16, 2021).

Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

As displayed in Table 10 the secondary endpoints in C-448 included change in body weight in
subjects with baseline BMI ≥25 kg/m2, change in SBP in subjects with baseline SBP ≥130 mm
Hg, change in HbA1c over time in subjects with stage 3a CKD (eGFR 45 to 59 mL/min/1.73
m2), and change in HbA1c over time in subjects with stage 3b CKD (eGFR 30 to 44
mL/min/1.73 m2) (endpoints are listed in the hierarchical testing order). Statistical significance of
the effect of bexagliflozin compared to placebo on the effect of SBP was not met, and therefore
no additional testing of secondary endpoints could proceed. Therefore, change in HbA1c in
eGFR subgroups was evaluated only for descriptive purposes.

53

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Glycemic efficacy by CKD subgroup is shown in Table 17. A greater treatment effect in HbA1c
was observed in the stage 3b CKD subgroup (-0.33%; 95% CI: -0.59, -0.07) as compared to the
stage 3a CKD subgroup (-0.19; 95% CI: -0.43, 0.05). The treatment effect in the stage 3a
subgroup numerically favored bexagliflozin (-0.19%), but nominal statistical significance was
not achieved (p=0.06).
These findings are counterintuitive, as greater glycemic efficacy would be expected in subjects
with better renal function given the mechanism of action of the drug. Mean changes in HbA1c
from baseline in the placebo group between subgroups appears to contribute, at least in part, to
the findings noted above, with the stage 3a placebo group having a -0.42% mean decrease in
HbA1c as compared to the stage 3b placebo group which had a -0.18% decrease in HbA1c from
baseline. Importantly, C-448 was not powered to detect differences in glycemic control between
bexagliflozin and placebo in each subgroup of renal function; all of these descriptive findings are
for exploratory purposes only.

To further investigate glycemic efficacy in subjects with renal impairment, additional supportive
endpoints related to glycemic control were assessed in an exploratory manner by the review
team. As shown in Figure 10, greater decreases in median FPG were observed in both stage 3a
and 3b CKD bexagliflozin subgroups as compared to placebo subgroups over time. A larger
decrease compared to placebo was observed in the stage 3a CKD bexagliflozin subgroup as
compared to the stage 3b CKD subgroup at Week 24. Given reduction in median FPG in subjects
with stage 3a exposed to bexagliflozin, it is unclear why the effect of HbA1c was more modest
than in stage 3b.
A higher proportion of subjects with stage 3a CKD in the bexagliflozin group met the glycemic
control target (i.e., HbA1c <7%) as compared to subjects with stage 3b CKD at Week 24,
although differences in baseline HbA1c between subgroups limits the interpretability of these
data (e.g., 7.57% versus 7.37% in the placebo and bexagliflozin Stage 3a CKD subgroups,
respectively) (Figure 11). Overall, findings from these supportive measures support the efficacy
of bexagliflozin in patients with T2DM and moderate renal impairment in C-448.

Trial C-476 in Patients With Increased CV Risk
Subjects with eGFR ≥45 mL/min/1.73 m2 were eligible for enrollment in C-476, allowing for an
efficacy evaluation in a subgroup of subjects with stage 3a CKD in this trial. Unlike C-448, an
historical eGFR measurement was not utilized to confirm stability of renal function over time,
55

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

and some patients with baseline eGFR <45 mL/min/1.73 m2 were enrolled in C-476 due to
variability between screening and baseline eGFR measurements (this also occurred in C-448,
albeit to a lesser extent; Table 11). For the subgroup analysis presented in this review, a cutoff of
eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 was utilized to identify subjects with renal impairment in C-476. Of
the subjects in C-476 with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2, approximately 15% of subjects
in each treatment group had baseline eGFR <45 mL/min/1.73 m2. The mean (SD) eGFR value in
subjects with eGFR <45 mL/min/1.73 m2 was 41 (4) mL/min/1.73 m2.
Although efficacy in subjects with renal impairment was a prespecified subgroup, the Applicant
did not include this subgroup in their hierarchical testing plan, and therefore the analyses below
are presented descriptively. As shown in Table 18, glycemic efficacy was not observed in
subjects with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 in C-476, with bexagliflozin demonstrating a mean
treatment difference (vs. placebo) in HbA1c of 0.0% (95% CI: -0.23, 0.23). Change from
baseline in HbA1c over time by treatment group and baseline renal function is shown in Figure
12. In subjects with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 maximal glycemic reduction in the bexagliflozin
occurred at week 12, followed by a decline if efficacy at week 24.
The Applicant proposes several reasons that may account for this finding. The Applicant notes
that, although the mean change from baseline of HbA1c in the bexagliflozin arm of this subgroup
was more modest (-0.50%) compared to the mean change for bexagliflozin arm with normal
renal function or mild renal impairment (-0.87%), the mean change from baseline in the placebo
arm was larger in the subgroup with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 (-0.50%) as compared to the
subgroup with eGFR ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m2 (-0.32%). Furthermore, in subjects with eGFR <60
mL/min/1.73 m2, over twice as many subjects in the placebo arm required treatment
intensification as compared to the bexagliflozin group, and over twice as many subjects in the
bexagliflozin group had documented relaxation of concomitantly prescribed antihyperglycemic
agents as compared to the placebo group (Table 19). The Applicant states that these factors
indicate a degree of investigator intervention that did not occur in C-448, thereby explaining the
discrepant results between trials.
The rationales proposed by the Applicant do not provide compelling justification for the lack of
treatment effect in subjects with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 in C-476. For example, while more
subjects in the placebo group received treatment intensification as compared to the bexagliflozin
group, this occurred to approximately equal extents in both subgroups (Table 19). In addition,
these data are difficult to interpret since the degree of treatment intensification/relaxation is
unclear. The Applicant also posits that differences in placebo effect between subgroup accounts,
at least in part, for the lack of glycemic efficacy in subjects with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2.
This argument is not very compelling because in a randomized, controlled trial, the placebo arm
should reflect what would have happened in the drug arm absent the drug.

To further investigate glycemic efficacy in subjects with renal impairment in C-476, additional
glycemic control measures were assessed in an exploratory manner by the review team. As
shown in Figure 12, bexagliflozin was nominally statistically superior to placebo at weeks 6 and
12 in the subgroup of patients with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2, despite showing no treatment
effect at week 24. As shown in Figure 13, greater decreases in FPG were observed in the
bexagliflozin group as compared to placebo in subjects with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2
across Weeks 6, 12, and 24. In addition, as shown in Figure 14, a higher proportion of subjects in
the bexagliflozin group with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 met glycemic control targets as
compared to subjects on placebo at Weeks 6 and 12; however, a lower responder rate in the
bexagliflozin group was observed when compared to the placebo group at Week 24 in this
58

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

subpopulation. Notably, the responder rate in placebo group with baseline eGFR <60
mL/min/1.73 m2 was approximately twice that of the placebo group with baseline eGFR ≥60
mL/min/1.73 m2 (30% versus 15%, respectively). While efficacy appears more modest in
subjects with reduced renal function compared to patients with normal or moderately impaired
renal function in Trial C-476, overall, these supportive analyses generally appear to support the
efficacy of bexagliflozin in this patient population.

Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Conclusion
A dedicated renal impairment trial (C-448) demonstrated statistically significant and clinically
meaningful reductions in HbA1c in subjects with T2DM and moderate renal impairment. Despite
this, efficacy was not observed in a subgroup analysis of subjects with baseline eGFR <60
mL/min/1.73 m2 in C-476. While the Applicant has proposed explanations for these results, the
underlying reason for the data observed is ultimately unclear. Trial C-476 was not designed to
assess glycemic efficacy in subjects with renal impairment. Hence, all of the descriptive findings
presented herein are for exploratory purposes only. In contrast, C-448 was an adequate and wellcontrolled study designed to assess glycemic efficacy in subjects with renal impairment, thus
providing more conclusive evidence of the glycemic lowering effect of bexagliflozin in this
patient population. In addition, while modest, the efficacy observed in C-448 was generally in
accordance with the efficacy observed with other SGLT2 inhibitors, which also demonstrate
more modest efficacy in this patient population.
As discussed in detail in Section 7.7.2, the safety of bexagliflozin in subjects with moderate renal
impairment is generally similar to the overall population, and no safety findings were observed
that significantly impact the benefit-risk assessment. Subjects enrolled in C-448 (i.e., subjects
with T2DM failing standard of care therapy with renal impairment) represent a patient
population who could benefit from therapies that could further improve glycemic control. Taken
together, the data presented support the efficacy of bexagliflozin in this patient population.
Labeling in “8.6 Renal Impairment” and “14 Clinical Studies” is proposed to describe the results
obtained in the bexagliflozin clinical development program.

Dissent
Not applicable.

7. Risk and Risk Management
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Bexagliflozin was evaluated in a comprehensive program of nonclinical safety studies that
included secondary (off-target) pharmacology studies, in vitro and in vivo (rats and monkeys)
safety pharmacology studies, general toxicology studies (up to 6 months in rats and 9 months in
monkeys), genetic toxicology studies (Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and in vivo rat
micronucleus assays), a full battery of reproductive toxicity studies in rats and rabbits, a juvenile
animal study in rats, and 2-year (lifetime) carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats.
In the chronic (39-week) monkey study, minimal to slight kidney cortical tubular cell
vacuolation, characterized by cytoplasmic microvacuoles adjacent to the basement membrane,
was observed at all bexagliflozin dose levels. This finding is considered secondary to
bexagliflozin-induced glucosuria but was not considered toxicologically significant based on the
absence of significant adverse effects on renal function. In the chronic (26-week) rat study, a low
incidence of minimal to slight stomach erosion/ulcer was observed in all treated male groups,
without dose dependency, and in high dose females. These findings might be treatment related
60

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

because similar findings were also reported in rats with other SGLT2 inhibitors. However, the
clinical importance of stomach erosion is limited considering the low incidence and severity in
rats and absence of similar findings in nonrodent species as well as in humans based on the
extensive clinical experience with the drug class. In both monkeys and rats, the no observed
adverse effect level (NOAEL) was the highest dose tested, which corresponded to 78 and 89
times the clinical dose of 20 mg, respectively, based on AUC.
Bexagliflozin produced no adverse effects on fertility in rats, on embryofetal toxicity in rats or
rabbits, or on pre- and postnatal development in rats at clinically relevant exposures. In juvenile
rats administered bexagliflozin during a period of renal development corresponding to the late
second and third trimesters of human pregnancy, adverse renal pelvic and tubular dilatation was
observed at the end of dosing and throughout the 4-week recovery period at exposures > 11 times
the MRHD. These findings are secondary to bexagliflozin’s pharmacodynamic activity (see
details in Section 5.1) and have been seen with all the drugs in the class. Based on the juvenile
animal data, bexagliflozin is not recommended for use during the second and third trimester of
pregnancy.
Bexagliflozin produced no evidence of carcinogenic potential in mice or rats when administered
for a lifetime. The duration of the male rat study was inadequate to allow rigorous statistical
evaluation of carcinogenic potential, due to early mortality that resulted in premature study
termination at Week 88. However, the available information, including the absence of
imbalances for preneoplastic findings and tumors in male rats, suggests that repeating the
carcinogenicity study in male rats at lower doses would be unlikely to provide additional useful
information. See details in Sections 13.1.1.4.2 and 13.1.1.4.3.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
There are currently four SGLT2 inhibitor drugs approved in the US: canagliflozin (approved
2013, NDA 204042), empagliflozin (approved 2014, NDA 204629), dapagliflozin (approved
2014, NDA 202293), and ertugliflozin (approved 2017, NDA 209803). Selected safety
information as represented in the US prescribing information for each drug is shown in Table 20.
