Not applicable; bexagliflozin is not currently marketed in any country.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
7.4.1. Data Pooling
The FDA’s safety evaluation for this application was based on the review of pooled safety data
from three placebo-controlled trials enrolling subjects, who were either treatment naïve or
inadequately controlled on metformin (C-419, C-449, and C-450), termed the placebo-controlled
pool, and the review of individual phase 3 trial data from active-controlled trials (C-423 and C480) and trials enrolling specific populations (C-448 and C-476). The Applicant’s approach to
the safety review is discussed in Section 17.2.
The placebo-controlled pool was utilized to evaluate the safety and tolerability of 20 mg
bexagliflozin relative to placebo across multiple trials in a similar patient population. This pool
excluded the uncontrolled, high-glycemic group (i.e., baseline HbA1c >10.5%) from C-419, as
well as the 5 and 10 mg bexagliflozin dosage strengths evaluated in C-449 that are not proposed
for marketing. This pool was used to inform common adverse reaction information presented in
Section 6 of the labeling.
Not all of the placebo-controlled trials were included in the placebo-controlled pool, as C-448
(enrolling subjects with moderate renal impairment) and C-476 (enrolling subjects with
increased CV risk) evaluated distinct patient populations as compared to trials C-419, C-449, and
C-450. In addition, trial C-476 was by far the largest (1,699 subjects in the safety population)
and longest (median duration of 2.4 years) trial in the safety program, and therefore pooling of
this trial with other trials in the clinical development program would have skewed the results of
the safety analysis towards this trial. Additional reasons to exclude C-476 from pooled analyses
also include higher background rates of AEs in C-476 compared to other trials that may dilute
imbalances observed in other trials, potential attenuation of imbalances due to the long
observation period, and potential introduction of bias such as ascertainment bias due to
differential dropout rates between treatment groups over time. For these reasons, safety data
from C-448 and C-476 are presented independently where appropriate. Active-controlled trials
were also reviewed and presented where appropriate, but not pooled with other placebo62

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

controlled trials to inform product labeling. Lastly, a “broad pool,” which pooled all phase 2b/3
trials (C-418, C-419, C-423, C-449, C-450, C-476, C-480, C-603A), was used to assess more
infrequent adverse events and inform overall exposure. As the broad pool consisted of trials of
significantly different study designs, patient populations, and drug formulation, safety analyses
with this pool were used for exploratory purposes only. See Section 17.2 for additional
information regarding this pool, including data handling considerations.
Safety data from phase 1/2a trials were excluded from the safety review conducted to inform
product labeling as trials were generally short (e.g., 1 to 14 day) PK/PD studies that did not
utilize the to-be-marketed formulation of bexagliflozin and/or did not enroll subjects with
T2DM. Relevant phase 1 trials to this safety review, including C-428 conducted to assess the
impact of bexagliflozin on cardiac repolarization, are discussed where relevant.

7.4.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
This section summarizes relevant information and methods utilized for the definition,
categorization, assessment, and adjudication of safety data.
•

•

•

•

•

Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) coding and mapping: All
adverse events were coded according to MedDRA using the current version at the time of
trial reporting. For the purposes of the integrated safety analysis, the Applicant mapped
adverse events (AEs) from all trials to MedDRA version 22.0. The CoreDF Report was
utilized to ensure adequate mapping of verbatim terms to preferred terms (PTs).
AE: The Applicant defined an AE as any untoward medical occurrence in a subject
administered a pharmaceutical product that does not necessarily have a causal
relationship with the treatment. An AE could therefore be any unfavorable and
unintended sign (including an abnormal laboratory finding), symptom, or disease
temporally associated with the use of a medicinal product, whether or not it is considered
related to the medicinal product use.
Serious adverse event (SAE): A serious AE was defined as any untoward medical
occurrence that at any dose resulted in the following: death, a life-threatening experience,
in-patient hospitalization, or prolongation of existing hospitalization, persistent or
significant disability/incapacity, or a congenital anomaly. Important medical events
would also be considered an SAE when (based on appropriate medical judgement) the
event may jeopardize the subject and may require medical or surgical intervention to
prevent one of the outcomes listed in the definition of SAE.
Safety population and treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs): The safety
population was defined as all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of
investigational product. A TEAE was defined as an AE that begins after the first dose
date or an existing AE that worsens after the first dose date. The Applicant’s TEAE
definition was deemed adequate, and only TEAEs were considered in this review.2
MACE and fatal events: An independent clinical endpoint committee (CEC), based out
of the Clinical Endpoint Center at Brigham and Women’s Hospital (Boston, MA),

The Applicant’s ISS SAP defines a TEAE as an event occurring within 28 days of the last done taken; however, a
28-day window was not applied in the Applicant’s analysis. The Applicant confirmed (SD0018) that this was an
error in their ISS SAP, and that their analysis did not utilize a 28-day window.
2

63

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•

performed blinded adjudication of CV events that contributed to the MACE+ metaanalysis. The CEC members included medical experts in the field of cardiovascular
disease. The CEC received and reviewed the following events for adjudication: all deaths,
suspected nonfatal myocardial infarction (MI), suspected hospitalization for unstable
angina, suspected transient ischemic attack and stroke, suspected hospitalization for heart
failure, and coronary revascularization procedures. The CEC also reviewed a listing of
SAEs on a monthly basis to identify MACE events potentially not identified/reported by
the investigator. The CEC’s manual of operations (i.e., adjudication charter) was
reviewed and deemed adequate to classify events appropriately, consistently, and in an
unbiased manner. Deaths were classified as CV death, non-CV death, or death of
unknown cause. CV deaths were subclassified as either fatal MI, pump failure, sudden
death, presumed sudden death, presumed CV death, fatal stroke, fatal pulmonary
embolism (PE), death related to CV procedure, or other CV death. There was no
classification or judgement by adjudicators on the investigator-reported PT for any event
type.
Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA): DKA events were adjudicated by an endocrinologist
according to the Applicant’s DKA adjudication charter. See Section 17.3.6 for additional
information on the DKA monitoring plan and case definitions used in the clinical
program.

7.4.3. Data Querying
Several methods to query the data were employed in this safety review, including the use of FDA
Medical Queries (FMQs), custom MedDRA queries (CMQs) developed by the Applicant, and
grouped queries (GQs) developed by the clinical reviewer. All FMQ analyses utilized the
“narrow” queries from FMQ release Version 2.0. Additional information on the PTs considered
for each GQ is provided in the caption of the associated table.

7.5. Adequacy of Clinical Safety Database
7.5.1. Exposure
When pooling all phase 2b/3 trials conducted in the clinical development program of
bexagliflozin for improved glycemic control and the placebo-controlled portion of a trial for the
treatment of essential hypertension (i.e., the broad pool; see Section 17.2 for details), 2,578
subjects were exposed to bexagliflozin for 3,482 patient-years (on-treatment) (Table 21). There
were 1,294 subjects exposed to bexagliflozin for ≥1 year, and 791 patients exposed for ≥2 years,
with longer duration exposure primarily occurring in C-476. Given that all trials included a
follow-up period, on-trial exposure was longer, with 3,824 patient-years of on-trial exposure in
the broad pool. See Section 7.5.3 for a discussion on how the total exposure met current
divisional guidance recommendations.

7.5.2. Characteristics of the Safety Population
Baseline characteristics of subjects from trials that comprise the placebo-controlled pool, C-448,
and C-476, are presented in Table 23. The placebo-controlled pool consisted of 672 subjects with
T2DM. The mean age of the population was 56 years and 5% were older than 75 years of age.
65

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Fifty-seven percent (57%) of the population was male and 45% were Caucasian, 38% were
Asian, and 15% were Black or African American. Baseline renal function was normal or mildly
impaired in 98% of subjects and moderately impaired in 2% of subjects (mean eGFR of 92.2
mL/min/1.73 m2). Baseline characteristics of active-controlled trials C-423 and C-480 were
generally similar to the placebo-controlled pool (data not shown).
C-448 (renal impairment trial) and C-476 (increased CV risk trial) enrolled distinct populations,
with subjects in these trials being generally older, having a longer duration of diabetes, and
worse renal function as compared to subjects in the placebo-controlled pool and active-controlled
trials (Table 23). For example, the mean age of subjects enrolled in C-448 and C-476 was 64 and
69 years old, respectively, compared to a mean of 56 years in the placebo-controlled pool. Over
half of subjects in C-476 and C-448 used insulin as a part of their diabetes management at
baseline, indicating more advanced disease, as compared to no subject using insulin at baseline
in the placebo-controlled pool.

7.5.3. Exposure per FDA’s Draft Guidance
Recommendations
The extent to which the clinical program meets the recommendations set forth in the draft
guidance document Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus: Evaluating the Safety of New Drugs for Improving
67

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Glycemic Control Guidance for Industry (March 2020) is shown in Table 24 and discussed
below:
Total Exposure and Exposure Duration: There were 3,482 patient-years of on-treatment
exposure and 3,824 patient-years of on-trial exposure in the broad pool. On-trial exposure was
longer due to follow-up periods in each trial (AEs that occurred during the follow-up period were
considered treatment-emergent and thus considered in this safety review). Therefore, the
guidance recommendation to have at least 4,000 patient-years of exposure was not met. In
addition, while the program did not meet the guidance recommendations for 1-year exposure
duration, it exceeded the recommendations for 2-year exposure durations.
Older adults: The clinical program exceeded the recommendation to evaluate at least 600
subjects >65 years of age.
Established CV Disease: The clinical program exceeded the recommendation to evaluate at
least 600 subjects with established CV disease. In C-476, there were 703 subjects assigned to
bexagliflozin in group 1 (consisting of subjects with a history of atherosclerotic vascular disease)
and 166 patients assigned to bexagliflozin in group 2 (consisting of subjects with a history New
York Heart Association [NYHA] class II/III HF), for a total of 869 subjects with established CV
disease exposed to bexagliflozin. As exposure in C-476 exceeded the guidance recommendation,
the Applicant did not undertake a program-wide evaluation of exposure for this category.
Renal Impairment: The clinical program did not meet the recommendation to evaluate at least
500 subjects with stage 3/4 CKD exposed to drug. There were 375 subjects in C-448 and C-476
with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 exposed to bexagliflozin (Table 11). While all other
trials excluded subjects with eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2, a small number of subjects in these
trials were enrolled with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 due to variability in measurements
between screening and baseline. Given that baseline eGFR was variable between screening and
baseline, these subjects were not considered in the evaluation of exposure for this population.
With the new guidance, the Division has moved away “from a narrow focus on evaluating
cardiovascular safety” and toward an approach that would yield a robust evaluation for a broad
range of safety concerns (Chong et al. 2020). In lieu of a requisite minimum number of events
and a degree of risk to be excluded, adequate enrollment of a broad population with enough
exposure to generate a robust safety database was sought. The bexagliflozin clinical program was
completed prior to issuance of the draft guidance in March 2020. Furthermore, at the time the
clinical program was under development, a prior draft guidance document (Guidance for
Industry: Diabetes Mellitus – Evaluating Cardiovascular Risk in New Antidiabetic Therapies to
Treat Type 2 Diabetes dated December 2008; now withdrawn (December 2008)) was in effect,
and the Applicant’s clinical program was completed in accordance with the recommendations in
the 2008 guidance. Despite not meeting all criteria recommended in the 2020 draft guidance for
each category, the total exposure and exposure durations observed in the clinical program for
bexagliflozin are adequate given that 1) they are sufficient to characterize commonly occurring
AEs, 2) the additional exposure needed to meet the guidance recommendations is not of
sufficient quantity such that it is likely to characterize safety signals that may occur at a low
frequency, and 3) the safety profile of SGLT2 inhibitors is generally well established, and the
safety review supports a similar safety profile to other SGLT2 inhibitor products. On balance,
given that the program enrolled higher numbers for older adults and those with established CV

68

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

disease (see below) and exceeded the recommended number of patients with exposure greater
than 2 years, the total exposure was deemed adequate.
The amount of exposure in subjects with renal impairment is considered adequate as
bexagliflozin will not be recommended for use in patients with eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m2. In
addition, the amount of exposure in C-448 is generally comparable to renal impairment trials that
have been used to inform the safety and efficacy of other SGLT2 inhibitors in this patient
population (e.g., dapagliflozin/DERIVE N=160 exposed subjects, empagliflozin/1245.36 N=188
exposed subjects, canagliflozin/DIA3004 N=179 exposed subjects).

7.6. Safety Findings and Concerns Based on
Review of Clinical Safety Database
7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns
7.6.1.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event
Summary
There were no clinically meaningful imbalances in the overall incidence of subjects experiencing
AEs in the placebo-controlled pool, C-476, or C-448 (Table 25), nor in active-controlled trials
(data not shown). While some event categories shown in Table 25 demonstrated imbalances
unfavorable to bexagliflozin (e.g., AEs leading to discontinuation in the placebo-controlled pool,
SAEs in Trial C-448), limited data in these categories impedes interpretability. No meaningful
imbalances were observed for AEs leading to discontinuation, AEs leading to drug interruption,
or SAEs in C-476, which had the most data to support meaningful conclusions. Overall, there do
not appear to be concerning trends.

7.6.1.2. Deaths
There were 69 deaths across all phase 2b/3 trials (broad pool). The majority of deaths in the
bexagliflozin clinical program occurred in C-476 (65 deaths), which enrolled patients with either
established cardiovascular disease or multiple risk factors for cardiovascular morbidity (Table 26
and Table 27). Deaths were limited in other phase 2/3 trials, with no deaths occurring in C-419,
C-448, C-449, or C-603. Single events of fatal cardiac arrest and sigmoid adenocarcinoma
occurred in the bexagliflozin groups of C-418 and C-423, respectively, and events of fatal
cardiomyopathy and small cell undifferentiated lung carcinoma occurred in the placebo and
glimepiride groups of C-450 and C-480, respectively.
In C-476 there were proportionally fewer deaths in the bexagliflozin group (3.4%) as compared
to the placebo group (4.6%) (Table 26). As expected, the majority of deaths were related to
cardiac events. As discussed in Section 7.4.2, an independent CEC reviewed all deaths that
occurred in the clinical program regardless of whether they were indicated as CV-related or not
by the investigator, and classified the event as either a CV death, non-CV death, or death of
unknown cause. Deaths of unknown cause were considered CV deaths for the purpose of the
MACE analysis and are presented in accordance with this practice in Table 26. In line with
conclusions from the MACE+ meta-analysis, there were proportionally fewer adjudicated CV
deaths in the bexagliflozin group (26 events, 2.3%) as compared to the placebo group (18 events,
3.2%). In addition, there were no meaningful imbalances in subcategories of CV death (e.g.,
sudden deaths, deaths of unknown cause, pump failure, etc.); although events in each category
were limited.
An imbalance unfavorable to bexagliflozin in the PT of “cardiac arrest” was observed (1.0%
versus 0.2% in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively), as shown in Table 26.
Narratives for the cardiac arrest events were reviewed and no apparent trend was observed,
although some events contained incomplete information limiting interpretability. The majority of
adjudicated CV deaths were classified as sudden deaths in both treatment groups, with more
70

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

sudden deaths occurring in the placebo group (Table 26). The CEC did not comment on the PT
associated with the event, and several events with the PT of “cardiac arrest” were adjudicated not
to be CV related by the CEC. Overall, the reason for the noted imbalance in investigatorassigned PT is unclear; however, the overall favorable drug effect on CV death in C-476 and the
MACE+ meta-analysis is reassuring.
There were proportionally fewer deaths that were adjudicated to be non-CV deaths in the
bexagliflozin group (13 events (1.1%)) versus the placebo groups (8 events (1.4%)) (Table 26).
There were also no meaningful imbalances in any PTs for noncardiac deaths or concerning
trends at the system organ class (SOC) level (Table 27), although data were limited.
Lastly, to assess deaths that may have occurred after subjects were lost to follow-up or withdrew
consent, the Applicant attempted to determine the vital status (i.e., living or deceased) for all
subjects randomized in C-476. Vital status was obtained for all but four subjects. Results from
this analysis show that there were 82 deaths in C-476, 48 (4.2%) occurring in subjects
randomized to receive bexagliflozin and 34 (6.0%) occurring in subjects randomized to receive
placebo.
Overall, death data from the bexagliflozin clinical program indicate trends favorable to
bexagliflozin; however, it is acknowledged that death events, and particularly noncardiac death
events, were limited.

7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events
The incidence of SAEs was similar between treatment arms in the placebo-controlled pool,
occurring in six (1.7%) and five (1.6%) subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups,
respectively (Table 25). No events by PT occurred in more than one subject and there were no
apparent trends between treatment groups by SOC (data not shown). Notable SAEs in
bexagliflozin-exposed subjects in this pool include single events of urinary tract infection (UTI)
and DKA, which are discussed in additional detail in Sections 17.3.6 and 17.3.9.
Similarly, limited SAE data were obtained in C-448 and active-controlled trials (C-423, C-480),
with no meaningful imbalances in the incidence of SAEs or events by SOC between treatment
groups (data not shown). Significant SAEs in bexagliflozin-exposed subjects in these trials
include single events of pyelonephritis and phimosis with subsequent radial circumcision in C480 and a single event of gangrene in C-423. The event of gangrene occurred on Day 16
subsequent to a trauma event (subject stepping on a nail), and therefore the event may not be
related to bexagliflozin use.
Of note, UTI, including serious events of urosepsis/pyelonephritis, DKA, and genital mycotic
infection (GMI) are considered class effects proposed for labeling. SAE data from these trials
therefore do not raise new safety concerns.
Most SAEs were observed in C-476. In this trial, there was a lower incidence of SAEs in the
bexagliflozin group compared to the placebo group (33% versus 37%, respectively) (Table 25).
SAEs reported in at least two subjects in the bexagliflozin group with risk difference (RD) ≥0.1
are shown in Table 28. In line with the enrolled patient population, various cardiac SAEs were
observed. Imbalances unfavorable to drug were observed for various PTs including coronary
artery disease, cardiac failure, and myocardial ischemia.
As this trial enrolled patients with increased CV risk, cardiac events are expected in the enrolled
population and some imbalances in individual PTs due to chance may be expected. The MACE+
meta-analysis did not identify a risk for MACE with bexagliflozin and provided reassuring
evidence of cardiac safety.
Several imbalances unfavorable to bexagliflozin involving infection-related events were
observed in C-476, including sepsis/septic shock, osteomyelitis, localized infection, and
cellulitis. These are discussed individually below.

Sepsis/Septic Shock
Twelve subjects (1.1%) in the bexagliflozin group and one subject (0.2%) in the placebo group
had an SAE of sepsis and/or septic shock in C-476. The imbalance persisted upon evaluation of
all events (i.e., SAEs and nonserious AEs), with 14 (1.2%) and 2 (0.4%) subjects experiencing
an event of sepsis/septic shock in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively. Four
subjects in the bexagliflozin group with serious events of sepsis died during the trial. Subject
(b) (6) died during hospitalization for infective endocarditis with the primary causes of
476death listed on the death certificate attributed to acute respiratory failure, septic shock, and
(b) (6) died after hospitalization for sepsis, gangrene, and severe
endocarditis. Subject 476end-stage peripheral artery disease (PAD). The subject refused below-the-knee (BTK)
(b) (6) died of acute
amputation, and the reported cause of death was gangrene. Subject 476respiratory failure in the setting of metabolic acidosis secondary to septic shock that was
73

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
(b) (6) was
presumed to have been caused by a nonhealing sacral ulcer. Subject 476hospitalized with an event of sepsis on Day 495 and later died on Day 518 due to acute left
ventricular failure. Of the remaining nonfatal SAEs, three events in the bexagliflozin group and
the single sepsis event in the placebo group appeared secondary to UTI. Urosepsis will be
included as a Warning and Precaution in the labeling.

The potential drug-relatedness of the sepsis events is unclear. While some events appeared to be
urosepsis events, which could be drug-related, other sepsis events appeared associated with
endocarditis, pneumonia, appendicitis, and cellulitis events. In cases where death occurred, there
were multiple contributing factors that lead to the event of death. Laboratory parameter
assessments in C-476 did not indicate clinically meaningful changes in relevant hematology
parameters (e.g., decreases in WBCs with bexagliflozin treatment) between treatment groups,
nor were there significant imbalances in the SOC for “Infections and Infestations” (data not
shown). As shown in Table 29, there was no significant imbalance between groups in serious
bacterial infections as identified by the “Bacterial Infections” FMQ (4.1% versus 3.9% in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). Associations between drug exposure and
increased risk of sepsis have not been characterized with approved SGLT2 inhibitors. Despite
unclear drug relatedness, labeling is recommended to inform providers on the potential increased
risk of sepsis events with bexagliflozin treatment as they are serious and potentially lifethreatening. Based on the available information, standard postmarket surveillance appears
reasonable to monitor the risk of serious sepsis events. Postmarketing reports related to serious
infections should be monitored.

Osteomyelitis
Serious osteomyelitis events occurred in 10 (0.9%) subjects in the bexagliflozin group versus 1
(0.2%) subject in the placebo group. The imbalance persisted when evaluating events of all
severity, with 12 (1.1%) and 3 (0.5%) subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups
experiencing an event of osteomyelitis, respectively. Seven subjects (six in the bexagliflozin
group and one in the placebo group) underwent amputations prompted, at least in part, by
osteomyelitis. See Section 7.7.1 for additional discussion regarding increased risk of amputation
with bexagliflozin exposure, which is proposed for labeling.

Localized Infection
Serious events of localized infection occurred in five subjects assigned to the bexagliflozin group
as compared to none in the placebo group. This imbalance persisted when evaluating events of
all severity (12 (1.1%) and 2 (0.4%) subjects with an event in the bexagliflozin and placebo
groups, respectively) Narrative review of the serious events revealed that four of the five subjects
had toe/foot infections, of which one subject had a subsequent amputation event.

Cellulitis
Serious events of cellulitis occurred in nine (0.8%) and two (0.4%) subject in the bexagliflozin
and placebo groups, respectively. However, an imbalance in these events was not observed when
assessing cellulitis events of all severity, with 34 (3.0%) and 16 (2.8%) of subjects in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups having an event. Five of the nine subjects with a cellulitis event
had concurrent osteomyelitis and/or sepsis events. Eight of the nine serious cellulitis events were
located on a lower limb.
74

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Amputation is a serious and clinically meaningful consequence of osteomyelitis and skin
infections, including cellulitis. As discussed in Section 7.7.1, the labeling for bexagliflozin
should advise prescribers to consider risk factors that may increase a patient’s risk of amputation
before initiating treatment and monitor for infections or ulcers of the lower limbs and discontinue
treatment should these events occur. Labeling that describes predisposing factors (e.g.,
osteomyelitis, diabetic foot ulcers, etc.) is also recommended.

Regarding AEs relevant to the SGLT2 inhibitor class, there were no meaningful imbalances for
serious events of pyelonephritis (0.3% and 0.2% in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups,
respectively) or acute kidney injury (AKI) (0.8% and 0.7% in the bexagliflozin and placebo
groups, respectively) in C-476. Various PTs representing fracture events occurred at a RD ≥0.1,
which are discussed in additional detail in Section 17.3.1.
To further explore serious events in C-476 for potential safety signals, FMQs were utilized.
Table 29 presents SAEs with RD ≥0.2% by FMQ. As discussed further in Section 17.3.1, there
were more events of serious fracture in the bexagliflozin group compared to placebo. Events in
the FMQs for “hypotension” and “dizziness” could indicate volume depletion events, which are
known to be associated with the class. Other FMQs had relatively few events per treatment arm
and/or did not demonstrate noteworthy differences between bexagliflozin and placebo groups.

7.6.1.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse
Events
Data to characterize AEs leading to discontinuation were limited in all trials except C-476.
Discontinuations due to AEs in the placebo-controlled pool are shown in Table 30 and include
isolated events of frequent urination (polyurea, pollakiuria), DKA, and potential UTI (dysuria).
In active-controlled Trials C-480 and C-423, there was a higher incidence of GMI events leading
to discontinuation of treatment in the bexagliflozin group as compared to the active comparator
groups (data not shown).

In C-476, AEs leading to discontinuation were balanced between treatment groups (8.4% and
8.5% of subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). FMQ analysis was
utilized to explore the data. Table 31 presents AEs leading to treatment discontinuation by FMQ
with an RD >0.1%. The only FMQ wherein there was a significant difference in events between
77

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

treatment groups (e.g., a CI lower bound >0) was the query for “Fungal Infection.” PTs driving
this imbalance included “genital fungal infection,” “vulvovaginal candidiasis,” and
“vulvovaginal mycotic infection.” While not captured by FMQ, there was also an imbalance
unfavorable to bexagliflozin for events of balanoposthitis leading to discontinuation (6 (0.5%)
versus 0 (0%) subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). In addition, a
higher incidence of events leading to treatment discontinuation in the query for “Renal and
Urinary Tract Infections” also was observed, including imbalances unfavorable to bexagliflozin
for the PTs of UTI and cystitis (please refer to Section 17.3.9, Table 218). All other queries had
few events and/or did not show a meaningful difference between treatment groups.
As discussed further in Section 7.6.1.5, GMI, UTI, and frequent urination are AEs commonly
occurring with SGLT2 inhibitor products, including bexagliflozin, as demonstrated in this safety
review. The data presented herein supports that commonly occurring AEs may lead to treatment
discontinuation. Information regarding these important tolerability related events will be
included in labeling.

7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
AEs reported in ≥2% of subjects treated with bexagliflozin and greater than placebo in the
placebo-controlled pool are shown in Table 32 and include female GMI, increased urination,
78

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

vaginal pruritus, UTI, thirst, hypoglycemia, and male GMI. Grouped queries (GQs) were utilized
to identify events that represented a similar medical concept (e.g., terms of polyuria, pollakiuria,
micturition urgency, and nocturia were grouped for increased urination events). The events
resultant from this analysis are considered class effects that are described in the labeling of
currently marketed SGLT2 inhibitor products. Aside from the events utilized in the GQ analysis
shown in Table 32, there were no other PTs that occurred in ≥2% of subjects and more
frequently with drug than placebo.

FMQs were used to investigate potential safety signals in the placebo-controlled pool (Table 33).
Similar to the GQ analysis presented above, FMQs for fungal infection, renal and urinary tract
infection, and hypoglycemia demonstrated RDs >0.5%. The FMQ for “Pruritus” contained both
events of genital pruritus identified in the above noted grouped queries, as well as additional
nonspecific/generalized pruritus events. FMQs for hepatic injury, study abuse potential, and
palpitations also demonstrated a risk difference of 0.5; however, the low number of events limits
79

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

interpretability. Events of rash and hepatic injury are further discussed in Sections 17.3.4 and
17.3.5, respectively.
There was a higher incidence in the FMQ for “Tendinopathy” with exposure to drug (1.3%
versus 0% of subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). PTs included
tenosynovitis and trigger finger, and a review of the verbatim terms for the tenosynovitis events
indicated events generally involved fingers. Imbalances unfavorable to drug in tenosynovitis
events were also observed in FMQ analyses completed with C-448 (3 (1.9%) and 1 (0.6%)
subject in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively), and C-476 (35 (3.1%) and 13
(2.3%) subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively; Table 34). These events
were generally not serious, nor did they lead to treatment discontinuation. In C-476, the majority
of events identified by the FMQ in both treatment groups were events of rotator cuff syndrome
(1.0% versus 0.9%, bexagliflozin versus placebo), tendonitis (0.8% v 0.5%, bexagliflozin versus
placebo), and trigger finger (0.6% versus 0.2%, bexagliflozin versus placebo) (Table 34). Events
of tendon rupture were balanced between treatment groups (0.2% in each treatment group).
The clinical meaningfulness of the imbalance in events captured by the tendinopathy FMQ is
unclear. Drug-induced tendinitis and tendon rupture is associated with quinolones, statins,
aromatase inhibitors, and glucocorticoids, but has not been previously characterized with SGLT2
inhibitors (Kirchgesner et al. 2014; Knobloch 2016; Bolon 2017). Drug-induced tendinitis
typically affects lower limb tendons, most commonly the Achilles tendon. The pathophysiology
of drug-induced tendinopathy is incompletely understood but proposed mechanisms include
apoptosis and structural disintegration of tenoblasts and tenocytes in the absence of
inflammation. Time to onset varies from days to years and any tendon can be affected. The
anatomical location of tendinopathy events in the clinical program is unclear as this information
was not systematically collected; however, where information exists, the events appear to occur
in the upper extremities (i.e., rotator cuff, trigger finger, etc.). Overall, the clinical
meaningfulness of this imbalance in unclear and events were not generally serious or severe.
Based on these considerations, specific product labeling is not warranted.

To further investigate for important imbalances in AEs, additional analyses were completed with
C-476. Table 35 shows the results from an FMQ analysis of FMQs with RD ≥0.5 and Table 36
shows AEs by PT with an RD ≥0.5. The results were generally in accordance with the analysis of
the placebo-controlled pool utilizing GQs. PTs for hypotension, dizziness and AKI were
captured in these analyses, potentially due to the older enrolled population with prior history of
cardiovascular disease and heart failure who are more volume sensitive to the diuretic effects of
bexagliflozin. There were significantly more events of balanoposthitis in the bexagliflozin group;
an imbalance that is more pronounced when the analysis is restricted to males (2.9% versus 0%
of male subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). Imbalances unfavorable
to drug for the FMQ’s hemorrhage and thrombocytopenia were observed, which could
potentially signal increased bleeds due to decreased platelet count. In addition, there were more
subjects with level 3 (<10,000) platelet counts in the bexagliflozin group as compared to the
placebo group (2.5% versus 1.3% of subjects in the bexagliflozin and placebo group with at least
one level 3 platelet count). However, it is reassuring that there were fewer serious hemorrhage
events (as identified by FMQ) in the bexagliflozin group of C-476 (1.2% versus 1.6% of subjects
in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). In addition, no differences in mean
platelet count over time were observed in the placebo-controlled pool (Section 17.4, Figure 76),
nor are changes in platelet count labeled for other approved SGLT2 inhibitor products.
Rash, AKI, and hypersensitivity events are further discussed in Section 17.3.4.

Lastly, an analysis utilizing Applicant-derived custom MedDRA query (CMQs) for the
evaluation of AESIs was performed for C-476 (Table 37). The results were generally in
accordance with results from FMQ analyses, with imbalances unfavorable to drug in GMI, UTI,
and diuretic effects. AESIs are discussed in detail in Section 17.3.
FMQ and CMQ analyses differed with regards to their narrowness/broadness, with CMQs being
generally more tailored for the detection of class effects. For example, the FMQ for “Fungal
Infection” is not specific to genital infections. In addition, the “Fungal Infection” FMQ does not
contain all PTs that are typically assessed in SGLT2 inhibitor programs (e.g., PTs for phimosis
and balanoposthitis events are not included in the query). To provide another example, the
“Volume Depletion” FMQ does not include events that have previously been utilized to identify
potential volume depletion events such as events of dizziness and hypotension, which have
dedicated FMQs. Therefore, the Applicant’s CMQs are informative as they are more tailored to
SGLT2 inhibitor programs. CMQs were reviewed and they appeared appropriate to identify
events for each AESI category. Labeling of results from Applicant-derived CMQs appears
appropriate when informative.

7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings
The following changes in laboratory parameters are proposed for labeling: increases in serum
creatinine and decreases in eGFR, increases in hemoglobin/hematocrit, and increase in lowdensity lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). See Section 17.4 for a detailed review of laboratory
findings.

7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
7.7.1. Increased Risk of Nontraumatic Amputation
With Bexagliflozin Treatment
Issue
An increased incidence of nontraumatic amputation was identified in the bexagliflozin group
compared to the placebo group in C-476.

Background
Lower limb amputation is a recognized complication of diabetes mellitus. An excess risk of
lower limb amputation beyond background risk has been previously identified with canagliflozin
and ertugliflozin, but not dapagliflozin and empagliflozin (Janssen 2020; AstraZeneca 2021;
Merck 2021; Ingelheim 2022). The risk of amputation with SGLT2 inhibitor treatment has been
assessed in the postmarketing CVOTs for SGLT2 inhibitors, enrolling patients with T2DM and
increased CV risk, which were conducted to rule out 30% excess CV risk per the 2008 diabetes
CV safety guidance (now withdrawn) (December 2008). In the CANVAS Program, which was
84

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

comprised of two nearly identically designed trials (CANVAS and CANVAS-R), an increased
risk of lower limb amputations associated with canagliflozin use versus placebo was observed in
CANVAS (5.9 versus 2.8 events per 1,000 patient-years) and CANVAS-R (7.5 versus 4.2 events
per 1,000 patient-years) (Janssen 2020). Amputations of the toe and midfoot (99 out of 140
patients with amputations receiving canagliflozin in the two trials) were the most frequent;
however, amputations involving the leg, below and above the knee, were also observed (41 out
of 140 patients with amputations receiving canagliflozin in the two trials). Some patients had
multiple amputations, some involving both lower limbs. Lower limb infections, gangrene, and
diabetic foot ulcers were the most common precipitating medical events leading to the need for
an amputation. The risk of amputation was highest in patients with a baseline history of prior
amputation, peripheral vascular disease, and neuropathy. Increased risk of amputation was also
observed in a CVOT conducted with ertugliflozin (VERTIS CV), where the occurrence of
nontraumatic lower limb amputation was 4.7, 5.7, and 6.0 events per 1,000 patient-years in the
placebo, ertugliflozin 5 mg, and ertugliflozin 15 mg treatment arms, respectively (Merck 2021).
Similar to the CANVAS Program, amputations of the toe and midfoot were most frequent (81
out of 109 patients) with the most common precipitating events being infection, gangrene, and
diabetic foot ulcers.
An underlying mechanism for an increased risk of lower limb amputation has not been clearly
identified. It has been hypothesized that volume depletion with SGLT2 inhibitors may lead to
hypoperfusion of extremities in patients at risk for an amputation event, which can slow wound
healing, make any existing infection become irreparable, and cause tissue damage or necrosis.
However, it is unclear how this hypothesis can support a different degree of risk of amputation
among SGLT2 inhibitors because volume depletion events can occur with all SGLT2 inhibitors.
Overall, given conflicting data from several large, postmarketing clinical programs, it is unclear
at this time whether SGLT2 inhibitor treatment increases amputation risk.

Assessment
All nontraumatic amputations that occurred in the bexagliflozin clinical development program
were observed in C-476 (one traumatic amputation event occurred in C-448). The potential
association with SGLT2 inhibitor treatment and amputation risk became known while the
clinical development program, including C-476, was ongoing. Amputation and revascularization
procedures were included as AESIs via protocol amendment (protocol Version 8, dated October
28, 2016). In addition, a dedicated case report form (CRF) was introduced in protocol Version 8
to capture additional information regarding amputation and revascularization events including
the anatomical location of amputation and the condition leading to the amputation. Eighty-two
percent of total study follow-up time occurred under a consent or reconsent to protocol Version
8.
Despite inclusion of a dedicated CRF and specification of amputation as an AESI in protocol
Version 8, the Applicant states that failures to record procedures occurred that were not detected
until after database lock (integrated summary of safety [ISS], page 165). While the reason for
underreporting of these procedures is unclear, the Applicant notes that investigators may have
considered amputation events planned treatments/responses to medical conditions as opposed to
AEs. To address this, the Applicant performed a review of CRF and SAE narratives for
unreported procedures. The information submitted by the Applicant in the ADaM adverse events

85

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

analysis (ADAE) and ADaM procedures analysis (ADPR) datasets incorporates their thorough
review of the available safety data.
Given that underreporting of amputation events was a concern in this program, exploratory
analyses were conducted to identify potentially missed amputation events. A list3 of amputationrelated PTs and PTs for events that may precede an amputation event was generated. A search of
the terms was conducted in the ADAE and ADPR datasets to identify potential missed
amputations. A review of the findings of this search indicated that all additional events identified
through this process were not amputation events. Therefore, the Applicant’s search of procedures
and AEs appears to have adequately captured amputation events in C-476.
Time-to-event data were derived for time-to-first nontraumatic amputation based on the
procedure and AE datasets. Post hoc analyses were conducted using the derived data. The results
of an on-study analysis are presented in Table 38, while the results of an on-treatment +14-day
analysis are presented in Table 39. Both analyses used a Cox proportional hazards model with
treatment as the only fixed effect. Estimated 95% confidence intervals for the hazard ratios for
both analyses include the null value 1. However, the time-to-event analyses showed a numerical
increase in the incidence of amputations associated with bexagliflozin and the point estimates of
the hazard ratios (HRs) are both greater than 1.6 (on-study: 1.64; on-treatment: 1.71).

As noted above, the dedicated CRF was also designed to capture information on
revascularization procedures. There was a minor imbalance unfavorable to bexagliflozin in the
number of revascularization procedures for PAD (2.5% and 2.1% of subjects having a procedure
in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). Other revascularization procedures (e.g.,
coronary artery bypass grafting [CABG], percutaneous coronary intervention [PCI], etc.) are
discussed in additional detail in Section 17.3.14.
88

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Conclusion
As detailed above, data from C-476 indicated an increased risk of amputation with bexagliflozin
treatment as compared to placebo (HR: 1.64 (0.70, 3.82)). Although the findings above on
amputations do not necessarily raise new concerns about the risk of amputation due to treatment
with SGLT2 inhibitors, the concern about the potential for excess risk of amputations on the
bexagliflozin arm has not been alleviated, especially given the existing concern about increased
risk of amputations for this class of drug. Labeling should state that before initiating treatment
with bexagliflozin, providers should consider factors that may increase the risk of amputation,
such as a history of prior amputation, peripheral vascular disease, neuropathy, and diabetic foot
ulcers. In addition, labeling will advise providers to monitor patients receiving bexagliflozin for
infection, new pain or tenderness, sores or ulcers involving the lower limbs, and discontinue
treatment if these complications occur. Labeling is appropriate to inform providers about
increased risk of amputation associated with bexagliflozin treatment.

Dissent
Not applicable.

7.7.2. Safety of Bexagliflozin in Subjects With
Moderate Renal Impairment
Issue
The safety of bexagliflozin in patients with stage 3 CKD.

Background
The efficacy of bexagliflozin in subjects with moderate renal impairment was a key efficacy
review issue. As detailed in Section 6.3.1, the efficacy of bexagliflozin in subjects with stage 3
CKD is considered acceptable for approval in the population in the absence of safety findings
that could impact the overall benefit-risk assessment in this population.

Assessment
Approach to Review
This key risk review issue was assessed using safety data from the dedicated renal impairment
trial (C-448) in addition to supportive information from the subpopulation of subjects with renal
impairment in C-476. The subpopulation assessed from C-476 was based on baseline eGFR
values (i.e., having a baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2). Protocol synopses for these trials are
provided in Section 15 and key trial design features, including inclusion/exclusion criteria, are
presented in Section 6.2. In C-476, treatment was stratified by eGFR category (eGFR <60 or ≥60
mL/min/1.73 m2), and approximately 20% of subjects in each treatment arm had eGFR <60
mL/min/1.73 m2. Baseline demographics for C-448 and C-476 are presented in Table 11.

89

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Overview of AEs
The frequency of AEs between treatment arms in C-448 were generally balanced, with 69.4%
and 67.7% of subjects experiencing at least one AE in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups,
respectively (Table 42). Events by severity were also generally balanced between treatment
arms. There were few events of AEs leading to permanent discontinuation or dose interruption of
the study drug, limiting interpretability of the data. No deaths occurred in this trial.

severity were also generally balanced between treatment groups as well as AEs leading to dose
interruption or treatment discontinuation (Table 43).

There were fewer AEs leading to treatment discontinuation in C-448 in the bexagliflozin group

were classified as stage 1 AKI (defined by the Applicant as serum creatinine 1.5 to 1.9 × or
increase ≥0.3 mg/dL from baseline).

Commonly Occurring AEs
AEs reported in ≥2% of subjects treated with bexagliflozin and greater than placebo in C-448 are
shown in Table 48. GQs were utilized to group PTs of similar medical concept. Commonly
occurring AEs were generally similar in C-448 as compared to the placebo-controlled pool, with
more subjects experiencing events of female GMI, increased urination, UTI, and thirst in the
bexagliflozin group compared to the placebo group. Approximately 25% of subjects in this trial
experienced at least one hypoglycemia event, which is likely attributable to over 50% of subjects
using insulin at baseline in this trial (as compared to no subjects using baseline insulin in the
placebo-controlled pool; Table 11). Other AEs reported in ≥2% of subjects treated with
bexagliflozin and greater than placebo in C-448 that were not observed in the placebo-controlled
pool include AKI, blood creatinine increased, bronchitis, hypertension, hypotension, sinusitis,
back pain, fall, and arthralgia. Higher incidence of these events may be, at least in part, due to
differing patient populations between C-448 and the placebo-controlled pool (e.g., an older
patient population with more comorbidities, more advanced disease, and poorer renal function in
C-448 as compared to the placebo-controlled pool).

Significant AEs
To investigate potential safety signals in C-448, a FMQ analysis was performed, and queries
occurring at an RD >0% are shown in Table 49. As anticipated, “Renal and Urinary Tract
Infection” and “Fungal Infection” queries had a higher incidence with drug than placebo,
reflecting higher rates of UTI and GMI with bexagliflozin treatment. A higher incidence of
hypertension (as a reported AE) was observed in the bexagliflozin group (3.2%) compared to the
94

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

placebo (0%) group. Analysis of mean changes in SBP demonstrated a mean decrease in SBP
over time, with a mean decrease of 8 mm Hg versus 4 mm Hg in the bexagliflozin and placebo
groups at Week 24, respectively. There were also more events of hypotension in the
bexagliflozin group (3.8%) versus the placebo group (3.2%). The meaningfulness of these
findings is unclear, although it is noteworthy that events were not serious and did not lead to
treatment discontinuation. Moreover, adverse event reporting for hypotension/hypertension is
subjective and investigator dependent, limiting interpretation of reported events. In addition,
objective systolic and diastolic blood pressure measurements revealed slight decreases in mean
values in the placebo-controlled pool (see Section 17.5, Figure 80).
An imbalance unfavorable to drug occurred for “Dizziness” (4.5% versus 1.3%). While the
events of dizziness are nonspecific, these events could potentially indicate mild volume depletion
events. There was no imbalance in fracture events as identified by FMQ, with three patients
experiencing a nonserious fracture event in each treatment group, although the incidence of falls
was slightly higher in the bexagliflozin group (3.2%) compared to the placebo group (1.9%).
Regarding AKI events, the protocol-specified definitions specified changes of serum creatinine
from baseline levels as opposed to acute serum creatinine changes as recommended in the
Kidney Disease Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) Guidelines (Khwaja 2012). It is possible
that AKI events may be overreported in this trial, as decreases in renal function over time are
expected in this patient population; however, this would not be expected to bias the data (i.e.,
potential AKI overreporting should occur equally in both treatment arms). As shown in Table 49,
there was no meaningful imbalance in AKI events in C-448.
Approved SGLT2 inhibitors report volume depletion events in subjects with renal impairment in
Section 6 of the labeling, with events reported including events dehydration, dizziness postural,
presyncope, syncope, hypotension, and orthostatic hypotension. The FMQ analysis completed in
this review differs from previous SGLT2 inhibitor reviews. For example, a single event of
“dehydration” was captured by the “Volume Depletion” FMQ, and events considered potential
volume depletion events in other SGLT2 inhibitor reviews (e.g., hypotension, dizziness,
syncope) were not identified by this query. For the purposes of Section 6 of the labeling, the
FMQs for “Volume Depletion,” “Syncope,” “Hypotension,” and “Dizziness” are pooled to
characterize potential volume depletion events. There were 14 (8.9%) and 6 (3.9%) subjects
having at least one event of volume depletion as assessed by the noted queries in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively.

An FMQ analysis of the subpopulation of subjects with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 in
C-476 was performed, and FMQs with a RD ≥0.5 are shown are Table 50. An imbalance in the
“Lipid Disorder” FMQ was observed. PTs that occurred in the bexagliflozin group identified by
the FMQ included the following: dyslipidemia, hypertriglyceridemia, hyperlipidemia, blood
cholesterol increased, low density lipoprotein increased, blood triglyceride increased, and
hypercholesterolemia. As discussed in Section 17.4, SGLT2 inhibitor treatment is associated
with modest increases in LDL-C which may, at least in part, support the results observed here.
Important clinical consequences of elevated lipid parameters include increased risk of MACE.
As presented in Section 17.1, the subgroup analysis by renal function group did not show an
increased risk for MACE+ events in subjects with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 (HR:
0.56 (0.29, 1.1)) as compared to subjects with baseline eGFR >60 mL/min/1.73 m2 (HR: 0.88
(0.61, 1.26)). Changes in lipid parameters from laboratory assessments in subjects with renal
impairment are further discussed in the next section.

Laboratory Parameters
Changes in laboratory parameters over time in C-448 were generally in line with data observed
in other trials, including modest mean increases in serum magnesium, serum phosphate,
hemoglobin/hematocrit, and transient decreases in serum bicarbonate (see Section 17.4 for
detailed analyses of changes in laboratory parameters over time). Transient increases in serum
creatinine and decreases in eGFR are known class effects. The magnitude of change in these
parameters was greater in C-448 as compared to the placebo-controlled pool, with a mean
increase in serum creatine of 0.14 mg/dL (10.8%) and a mean decrease in eGFR of -4.6
mL/min/1.73m2 (9.0%) from baseline to Week 6 (Figure 16). In contrast, in the placebocontrolled pool, serum creatinine increased 0.03 mg/dL (3.8%) and eGFR decreased -2.3
mL/min/1.73 m2 in the bexagliflozin group from baseline to Week 6. These results appear in line
with data from other SGLT2 inhibitor programs.

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

There were no significant mean changes over time in lipid parameters between the bexagliflozin
and placebo groups in C-448 (data not shown); however, there were more subjects with level 3
elevations in serum cholesterol (>225 mg/dL), LDL-C (>190 mg/dL), and triglycerides
(500 mg/dL) in the bexagliflozin group as compared to the placebo group (Table 51). Supportive
data from the subpopulation of subjects with baseline eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 also indicated
a higher incidence of lipid disorders in this population. As discussed above, subgroup analyses
98

from the MACE+ meta-analyses provide reassurance that the noted changes in lipid parameters
do not increase the risk of MACE; however, labeling is proposed in Section 6 to inform
providers on potential changes in lipid parameters in this patient population.

Conclusion
Overall, considering the safety data from C-448 and C-476, no new safety issues were identified
in subjects with stage 3 CKD, although data to characterize certain events (e.g., SAEs, events
leading to discontinuation, etc.) were limited. Labeling to inform prescribers regarding changes
in certain laboratory parameters (i.e., eGFR/creatinine and lipid parameters) is warranted. The
available safety data do not raise new safety concerns and support labeling of bexagliflozin in
patients with eGFR >30 mL/min/1.72 m2.

Dissent
Not applicable.

7.7.3. Safety Database Quality Concerns
Issue
Safety database quality concerns.

99

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Background
Concerns regarding the quality of the safety database were raised when multiple events of falls
and AKI were found in subject narratives that did not have a corresponding PT in the ISS ADAE
dataset. For illustrative purposes, the following excerpts have been reproduced from subject
narratives where there was no PT of “fall” or “AKI” in the ISS ADAE dataset:
Fall:
•
•
•

(b) (6) : “On 1
(b) (6) (Day 247), the subject had a mechanical fall at
Subject 476home, suffered abrasion of the left shin and subsequently developed cellulitis.”
(b) (6) : “On
(b) (6) (Day 577), the subject sustained left hip fracture
Subject 476due to accidental fall at home.”
(b) (6) : “On
(b) (6) (Day 639), the subject sustained a right hip
Subject: 476fracture and complained of neuropathic symptoms on the right sole after he had a fall.”

AKI:
•
•

(b) (6) : “Hypotension was likely due to over diuresis, which also caused
Subject 476prerenal acute kidney injury.”
(b) (6) : “The site reported that elevated lipase was due to acute kidney
Subject: 476injury secondary to bilateral obstructing ureteral stones.”

In addition, as previously discussed in Section 7.7.1, the Applicant acknowledged in their
application that amputation events were underreported by investigators, and that additional
querying of the safety database was needed to adequately identify all amputation events.
Falls, AKI, and amputation were AESIs in all phase 3 protocols, and robust methods for
identifying, reporting, and analyzing these events are expected. As underreporting of AEs,
including AESIs, could ultimately impact labeling and the overall benefit-risk assessment, data
integrity and quality were examined.

Assessment
Several approaches were taken to investigate the quality of the safety database. First, FDA
requested that the Applicant perform additional analyses to investigate whether AESIs may have
been underreported in the bexagliflozin clinical program. Second, FDA asked the Applicant to
provide detailed information on the processes and procedures used for the collection of clinical
safety data, starting from the clinical encounter, and extending to the preparation of the safety
database, to provide assurance that the submitted data were of sufficient quality for review.
Third, Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) inspection information was reviewed to inform
potential data integrity issues. These approaches are discussed individually below.
Natural Language Processing-Based Data Querying
To investigate whether potential under-coding of safety data occurred in the bexagliflozin
clinical program, the Applicant utilized a natural language processing (NLP)-based approach to
query available free-text safety data (e.g., subject narratives, verbatim terms, the Argus safety
database (the database used to track, review and process individual safety events, including
SAEs), etc.). In accordance with the findings observed by the review team, a significant number
of additional fall events were identified by the Applicant utilizing the NLP-based approach. For
100

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

example, the Applicant states that 69 fall events occurring in 64 subjects were considered to
potentially represent distinct fall or fall-like occurrences based on free-text search of narrative
sources, 57 of which affected subjects without recorded events of fall in the ADAE database, 6
of which were fall events present in the ADAE database, and 6 of which were additional events
in subjects with existing events in the ISS ADAE database (SD0031). In other words,
approximately 90% of subjects who experienced a fall event were not included in the ISS ADAE
dataset submitted with this application.
The Applicant submitted a modified ISS ADAE dataset that included AEs identified by the NLPbased search for additional events. FMQ analyses were used to assess the original ISS ADAE
dataset (adae.xpt) and the NLP-modified ADAE dataset (tadae.xpt). As the majority of additional
adverse events found were identified from hospitalization records and/or narrative information
for SAEs, C-476 was utilized for this analysis as it contained the majority of SAEs observed in
the bexagliflozin clinical development program. In accordance with observations of the review
team, there were an additional 40 and 19 subjects who experienced a fall event in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively, reported in the NLP-modified dataset as
compared to the original ISS dataset. A number of additional AKI events were also reported in
the NLP-modified dataset. However, the identification of these events appeared primarily based
on elevations in creatinine that did not appear to represent events that necessitated coding based
on a random review of corresponding subject narratives. There was no significant difference in
the FMQs for “Fungal Infection,” “Dizziness,” “Rash,” “Renal and Urinary Tract Infections,”
“Hypersensitivity,” “Malignancy,” or “Fracture” between datasets (Table 52).
For FMQs where additional events were coded in the NLP-modified dataset, there was no
evidence of systemic bias towards underreporting in the bexagliflozin treatment group. For
example, in the original ISS dataset, there were 2.7% and 3.0% of subjects who had at least one
fall (as identified by the FMQ for “Fall”) in the bexagliflozin and placebo group of C-476,
respectively. In the NLP-modified dataset, there were 6.3% and 6.3% of events in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively.
Table 52. Comparison of FMQ Analyses From ISS ADAE Versus NLP-Modified ISS ADAE Datasets,
Safety Population, Trial C-476
NLP-Modified ISS±

ISS ADAE*

A root cause analysis for why fall events were underreported was requested by the Agency. The
Applicant states that there was no single explanation for the underreporting of fall events but
explained that contributing factors could include lack of guidance to investigators regarding the
collection of falls as AEs and the ability for a fall event to be viewed as a symptom of event by
investigators as opposed to a stand-alone event requiring independent reporting. In addition, the
Applicant also clarified that although “Falls and Fractures” were identified as AESIs in study
protocols, beginning with Version 3 of their AESI definitions (March 09, 2018), the PT of fall
was no longer included as an AESI. The Applicant provided the following response to FDA’s
information request:
There were several reasons for discontinuing fall as a component of the “Falls and
Fractures”AESI. Falls in of themselves do not contribute to an understanding of
potential adverse effects of bexagliflozin on bone, except as a kind of denominator over
which traumatic events leading to fracture could be assessed (e.g., to calculate fractures
per fall). Because few falls were expected to lead to fractures, (as was the case),
inclusion of falls could reduce the discriminating power of the event category. In
addition, fractures could originate from a variety of forms of trauma, not simply falls.
Enumerating traumatic events in general is one possible response, but subcategories of
trauma have poor support in MedDRA and their inclusion would also likely be dilutive.
Fall and related events associated with loss of postural control can have diverse
etiologies, such as hypoglycemia, hypotension, seizure, syncope, trauma or vestibular
events.
Thus, falls were not considered AESIs for the purposes of the integrated summary of safety.
Note, we do not agree with the Applicant’s rationale for removing falls from the AESI category
and all AEs related to falls should have been included.
Processes-Based Quality Review
In addition to efforts to uncover evidence of potential under-coding and differential coding (i.e.,
the same adverse event coded differently) of adverse events, the review team also examined
whether the bexagliflozin development program was designed with important data quality and
processes in place. We reviewed the Applicant’s data collection, management, and data quality
processes and procedures employed during the trial with reference to International Conference
on Harmonisation (ICH) E6(R2) Guidance entitled Good Clinical Practice: Integrated
Addendum to ICH E6(R1) (March 2018), as summarized below.

OSI Review
As detailed in OSI’s review (see DARRTS consult dated July 13, 2022), eight domestic clinical
investigators were inspected. Investigators/study sites were selected based on the ability of data
from their site to potentially impact the overall benefit-risk assessment. Amongst other activities,
site inspections reviewed subject source documents including AE and SAE reports, and source
records were compared to the Applicant’s data line listings in the NDA submission. OSI
concludes that no significant regulatory violations were found upon inspection and that, based on
the results of inspections and regulatory assessments, Trials C-419, C-423, C-448, C-450, C-476
and C-480 appear to have been conducted adequately.

Conclusion
Results from analyses conducted with the Applicant’s NLP-modified dataset (tadae.xpt) were in
line with the review teams observations that certain events (i.e., falls, AKI, amputation),
103

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

appeared under-coded in the safety database; however, a systematic under-coding of other
important events (e.g., fungal infections, fractures, malignancies) does not appear to have
occurred. In addition, no evidence of biased underreporting was observed (i.e., under coded
events impacted both treatment groups generally equally). The Applicant provided information
to support that the trials were designed with appropriate measures to ensure the collection of
high-quality data. Lastly, as detailed in OSI’s review, pivotal studies appear to have been
conducted adequately based on inspectional findings. Overall, the data generated and submitted
by the Applicant appear of acceptable quality for review.

Dissent
Not applicable.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Renal Impairment
In patients with mild, moderate, and severe renal impairment, bexagliflozin AUC increased by
6.8%, 33.8% and 53.9% compared to patients with normal renal function. The maximum plasma
concentration (Cmax) was not affected. Consistent with the mechanism of action, the 24-hour
UGE declined with decreased eGFR, from 100 g without impairment to 83, 40, and 17 g in the
setting of mild, moderate, and severe impairment, respectively.
The effects of moderate renal impairment (stage 3 CKD, 30≤eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2) were
evaluated in two phase 3 studies (C-448 and C-476). Based on the efficacy and safety observed
in these studies, bexagliflozin can be used in patients with mild and moderate renal impairment
without dose adjustment (see Section 6.3 for detailed information).
The safety and efficacy of bexagliflozin have not been established in patients with severe renal
impairment, with end stage renal disease, or receiving dialysis. However, bexagliflozin is not
expected to be effective in these patient populations based on its mechanism of action.

Hepatic Impairment
The use of bexagliflozin has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment. In
subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh class B), bexagliflozin AUC increased
by 28.3%, and Cmax increased by 6.2% compared to subjects with normal hepatic function. The
extent of exposure increase is less than that from moderate renal impairment and is not
considered clinically relevant and therefore dose adjustment is not needed in patients with mild
and moderate hepatic impairment.

UGT1A9 Polymorphism
Bexagliflozin is mainly metabolized by UGT1A9, which is polymorphic. Although the Applicant
didn’t collect information on UGT1A9 allelic variants in clinical studies, the impact of UGT1A9
genotype on the PK of bexagliflozin may be less than or similar to that observed in the clinical
104

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

study with UGT1A9 inhibition. Based on a <40% increase in bexagliflozin AUC in the presence
of UGT1A9 inhibitor probenecid, UGT1A9 polymorphism is unlikely to contribute a sufficiently
substantial effect to warrant a change in the dosing regimen.

Other Intrinsic Factors
The impact of intrinsic factors on bexagliflozin PK exposure was evaluated. Intrinsic factors
included in the population PK analysis (i.e., creatinine clearance, body weight, Asian race,
healthy status) were not considered to have a clinically meaningful effect on bexagliflozin PK.
Refer to the covariate effect assessment in the Pharmacometrics Review Appendix (Section
14.3.4).

8.2. Food Effect
Administration of bexagliflozin after consumption of a high-fat, high-caloric meal delayed
bexagliflozin time to maximum concentration (Tmax) by about 1.5 hours and increased its Cmax
and AUC values by 31% (90% CI: 17%, 48%) and 10% (90% CI: 3.9%, 17%), respectively,
compared to the values observed following dosing in the fasted state (Trial C-481). The observed
exposure increases are not considered clinically meaningful and bexagliflozin can be
administered with or without food.

8.3. Drug Interactions
In vitro studies indicated that bexagliflozin is mainly metabolized by UGT1A9 with minor
contribution from the cytochrome (CYP)3A enzyme. In addition, bexagliflozin is a substrate and
inhibitor of P-gp at clinically relevant concentrations. Based on the in vitro findings, the
Applicant conducted several clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies to further evaluate the
effect of UGT1A9 inhibition (probenecid), CYP3A/uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyltransferase
(UGT) induction (rifampin), and p-gp inhibition (verapamil) on the PK of bexagliflozin, as well
as the effect of bexagliflozin on the PK of a sensitive P-gp substrate (digoxin). The Applicant
also evaluated the potential interactions between bexagliflozin and commonly prescribed
antidiabetic agents including exenatide, glimepiride, sitagliptin, and metformin.
Based on the results of these studies (Figure 17 and Figure 18), clinically meaningful DDI is not
expected when bexagliflozin is taken with 1) antihyperglycemic agents including exenatide,
glimepiride, sitagliptin, metformin, 2) drugs that are substrates of P-gp, and 3) drugs that inhibit
P-gp or UGT1A9, (see Section 8.3.3 for further details). UGT enzyme inducers may significantly
reduce bexagliflozin exposure and decrease its efficacy. Therefore, adding another
antihyperglycemic agent may be considered in patients who require additional glycemic control.
