Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

8.3.1. DDI Potential as a Victim
Effect of UGT Inhibition on the PK/PD of Bexagliflozin
Effect of UGT inhibition on bexagliflozin PK/PD was assessed in 16 healthy subjects using a
nonspecific UGT inhibitor probenecid. During the study, subjects took a single dose of
bexagliflozin 20 mg on Day 1 and Day 5. Probenecid 500 mg twice a day was administered from
Day 3 to Day 6. Coadministration with probenecid increased bexagliflozin AUC0-t and Cmax by
38.8% [29.5%, 48.8%] and 18.7% [11.1%, 26.7%], respectively, compared to bexagliflozin
alone. Bexagliflozin Tmax and t1/2 were similar between treatments. Postdose UGE for each 12hour collection interval was similar for both treatments, indicating no impact on UGE. Based on
the safety profile of bexagliflozin, the magnitude of exposure increase observed in this study was
not considered clinically significant. Therefore, bexagliflozin can be taken with drugs that inhibit
UGT.

Effect of CYP3A/UGT Induction on the PK/PD of Bexagliflozin
Effect of CYP3A/UGT induction on bexagliflozin PK/PD was assessed in 16 healthy subjects
using a strong CYP3A/UGT inducer (rifampin). During the study, subjects took a single dose of
bexagliflozin 20 mg on Day 1 and Day 6. Rifampin 600 mg once daily was administered from
Day 3 to Day 7. The point estimate of geometric mean ratio (GMR) (90% CI) for treatments
bexagliflozin + rifampin vs. bexagliflozin alone was 86.1% (70.2%,105.6%) for AUC0-t and
119.6% (94.4%, 151.6%) for Cmax. The half-life of bexagliflozin was decreased from 12.9 hours
to 5.9 hours when c-administered with rifampin. Postdose UGE for each 12-hour collection
interval was similar for both treatments.
The review team is concerned that a 3-day pretreatment with rifampin is too short to achieve the
full potential of CYP3A/UGT induction. The induction effect-time profile of rifampin on
CYP3A has been demonstrated by several publications. In general, at least 5 days of treatment
with rifampin (600 mg per day) is needed to approach the plateau of CYP3A induction. Based on
the shared activation pathway of Pregnane X Receptor between CYP3A and UGT, it’s
anticipated that a 3-day pretreatment duration may not fully reach the peak of UGT1A9
induction as well.
Overall, the current study may not capture the worst-case scenario on the impact of
CYP3A/UGT induction on bexagliflozin PK. This speculation is further supported by the smaller
extent of exposure decrease with bexagliflozin compared to other SGLT2 inhibitors, all of which
are also mainly metabolized by UGT1A9. Specifically, DDI studies with rifampin showed AUC
reductions of 51%, 22%, and 39%, respectively, for canagliflozin, dapagliflozin, and
ertugliflozin.
Overall, the current study result may not reflect the maximum effect of CYP3A/UGT induction
on the PK of bexagliflozin. It is expected that strong CYP/UGT inducers may lead to
significantly decreased bexagliflozin exposures and may potentially result in compromised
efficacy in glycemic control. Therefore, adding another antihyperglycemic agent may be
considered in patients who require additional glycemic control. The impact of UGT enzyme
inducers on bexagliflozin and the potential requirement for additional antihyperglycemic agents
will be included in the prescribing information.
107

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Effect of P-gp Inhibition the PK of Bexagliflozin
When bexagliflozin (20 mg) was administered one hour after the intake of a P-gp inhibitor
verapamil (120 mg), the mean GMR (90% CI) was 96.6% (88.1%, 105.9%) and 106.0% (88.1%,
126.7%) for bexagliflozin AUC0-t and Cmax, respectively. Therefore, concomitant drugs that are
P-gp inhibitors are unlikely to affect bexagliflozin PK.

8.3.2. DDI Potential as a Perpetrator
In vitro studies indicate that bexagliflozin may be a P-gp inhibitor at the clinically relevant dose.
Therefore, a clinical DDI study with a sensitive P-gp substrate digoxin was conducted. The
results show no significant changes in the systemic exposures of digoxin when coadministered
with bexagliflozin (20 mg one daily for 8 days). Therefore, bexagliflozin administered as 20 mg
daily is unlikely to affect the PK of concomitant drugs that are substrates of P-gp.

8.3.3. DDI Potential With Commonly Prescribed
Antidiabetic Agents
DDIs With Exenatide
Following injection of 10 μg of exenatide, the GMR (90% CI) for AUC0-t, and Cmax of
bexagliflozin with exenatide compared to bexagliflozin alone were 1.47 (1.30, 1.67), and 1.25
(1.04, 1.50), respectively, and the median Tmax increased from 2 hours to 5 hours. Per the
Applicant, the effects of exenatide, a glucagon-like peptide (GLP)-1 receptor agonist, were
consistent with the known delay of gastric emptying by GLP-1 receptor agonists and mimicked
the effects of prior consumption of food (Trial C-481). However, it should be noted that the
impact on the PK of bexagliflozin was much larger with exenatide compared to the food effect
(47% versus 10% increase in AUC). The exact mechanism of interaction is not clear. The PD
response of 24-hour UGE was lower in the bexagliflozin plus exenatide group (~55 g) compared
to the bexagliflozin alone group (~65 g). Nonetheless, the decreased UGE is unlikely to translate
to diminished efficacy, given the 24-hour UGE value of 55 g is still on the plateau of the
bexagliflozin dose-UGE response curve (Figure 5). In addition, the observed 47% exposure
increase in bexagliflozin is not considered clinically relevant as no major safety concerns were
identified when bexagliflozin was given to patients with T2DM for 28 days at the dose of 50 mg.
Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when bexagliflozin is taken with a GLP-1 receptor
agonist.

DDIs With Metformin, Glimepiride and Sitagliptin
DDI potential of bexagliflozin with commonly prescribed antidiabetic agents including
metformin, glimepiride, and sitagliptin was assessed in 54 healthy subjects (N=18 per group).
For each group, subjects received a single dose of bexagliflozin 20 mg alone, a single dose of an
oral hypoglycemic agent (1000 mg metformin, 2 mg glimepiride, or 100 mg sitagliptin) alone,
and the combination of both (bexagliflozin and antidiabetic agents) alternately in a crossover
fashion, with three treatment periods separated by a washout period of at least 7 days.

108

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

The results show that bexagliflozin had no significant effect on the PK of metformin,
glimepiride, and sitagliptin. Similarly, there was no clinically relevant impact on the PK of
bexagliflozin. UGE values of bexagliflozin were slightly lower when administered with other
antidiabetic agents but the differences were not statistically significant. Overall, no dose
adjustment is needed for bexagliflozin when administered with metformin, glimepiride, and
sitagliptin.

Other Drug Interactions
A few drug interaction warnings have been added to the product labeling, but they were not
reviewed in the current submission. These drug interactions are related to the class effects of
SGLT2 inhibitors including increased risk of hypoglycemia when used combination with insulin
and/or insulin secretagogues, interference with the 1,5-anhydroglucitol (1,5-AG) assay, a
positive urine glucose test and potential reduction in serum lithium levels during the treatment
with SGLT2 inhibitors.

8.4. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The initial approval of bexagliflozin will be limited to adults as the trials included in this
application did not enroll pediatric subjects. The Division agrees to waive the pediatric study
requirement for ages 0 through 9 years (inclusive) because necessary studies are impossible or
highly impracticable. This is because there are too few children in this age range with T2DM to
study. Furthermore, the Division agrees to defer submission of the Applicant’s pediatric study
for ages 10 to 17 years (inclusive) for this application because this product is ready for approval
for use in adults and the pediatric studies have not been completed.
The Applicant’s deferred pediatric study is required under section 505B(a) of the FDCA as a
required postmarketing study. The status of this postmarketing study must be reported annually
according to 21 CFR 314.81 and section 505B(a)(3)(C) of the FDCA. The required pediatric
study is listed below:
1) A 24-week, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group study of the
safety, efficacy, pharmacokinetics (PK), and pharmacodynamics (PD) of bexagliflozin
for the treatment of type 2 diabetes mellitus in pediatric patients ages 10 to 17 years
(inclusive), followed by a 28-week double-blind, controlled extension. The bexagliflozin
dose will be determined based on the initial PK/PD lead-in portion of the study.
Draft Protocol Submission: January 2023
Final Protocol Submission: July 2023
Study Completion: May 2031
Final Report Submission: October 2031

8.5. Pregnancy and Lactation
There are no available data on bexagliflozin use in pregnant women to evaluate a drug-associated
risk of major birth defects, miscarriage, or adverse maternal or fetal outcomes, or information
regarding the presence of bexagliflozin in human milk. A standard battery of reproductive
109

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

toxicology studies and a juvenile toxicology study with relevance to pregnancy were conducted
with bexagliflozin. No treatment-related adverse effects on fertility, embryo-fetal development,
or pre- and postnatal development were observed at clinically relevant exposures. Based on
adverse renal effects (adverse renal pelvic and tubular dilatation) in juvenile rats administered
bexagliflozin during periods of renal development corresponding to the late second and third
trimester of human renal development, bexagliflozin is not recommended during pregnancy.
Detailed summaries of the results of these studies are contained in Section 13 of this review.
Table 53. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling
Labeling Section
Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy
Bexagliflozin dosed directly to juvenile rats at 0.3, 3, 30 mg/kg by oral gavage
from postnatal day (PND) 21 to 90 caused a dose dependent increase in the
incidence and severity of renal pelvic and tubular dilatation at > 3 mg/kg (11
times the MRHD). These effects occurred with drug exposure during periods
of renal development in rats that correspond to the late second and third
trimester of human renal development.
In embryofetal development studies, bexagliflozin was administered by oral
gavage to rats at 0, 7, 40 or 200 mg/kg/day and to rabbits at 0, 5, 25 or
150 mg/kg/day during the period of organogenesis. No adverse
developmental effects were observed in rats at doses up to 200 mg/kg/day
(550 times the clinical dose of 20 mg based on AUC). Reduced maternal body
weight, embryo lethality, and fetal malformations were observed in rabbits at
150 mg/kg/day (370 times the clinical dose of 20 mg based on AUC).

8.2 Lactation

In a pre- and postnatal development study, bexagliflozin was administered to
maternal rats by oral gavage from gestation Day 6 throughout lactation Day
20 at doses of 7, 40 or 200 mg/kg/day. Maternal mortality occurred at
>40 mg/kg (79 times the clinical dose of 20 mg based on AUC), primarily
following parturition. Reduced gestational body weight, increased
postimplantation loss, and smaller litter size were noted at 200 mg/kg (361
times the clinical dose of 20 mg based on AUC). In the offspring, lower body
weight gain and decreased survival were noted at 200 mg/kg, which occurred
in the presence of significant maternal toxicity.
Bexagliflozin was present in rat milk at a milk: plasma ratio of approximately 2.
If a drug is present in animal milk, it is likely the drug will be present in human
milk. Juvenile rats directly administered dapagliflozin showed risk to the
developing kidney (renal pelvic and tubular dilatations) during the period of
renal development in rats corresponding to the late second and third trimester
of human renal development.

13.1 Carcinogenesis,
Carcinogenesis: The carcinogenic potential of bexagliflozin was evaluated in
mutagenesis, impairment 2-year studies in Sprague-Dawley (SD) rats and CD-1 mice. There were no
of fertility
neoplastic findings in mice and rats up to the highest doses tested
(150 mg/kg/day for mice and 30 mg/kg/day for rats, representing
approximately 135 and 54 times the clinical dose of 20 mg based on AUC,
respectively). The male rat study was terminated prematurely at Weeks 8288, due to excessive mortality.
Mutagenesis: Bexagliflozin was not mutagenic or clastogenic in the in vitro
Ames bacterial mutagenicity assay, the in vitro mammalian chromosomal
aberration assay, an in vivo micronucleus test in rats, and in vivo unscheduled
hepatic DNA synthesis in rats.
Impairment of fertility: Bexagliflozin had no effects on mating, fertility, or early
embryonic development in male or female rats at any dose up to the highest
dose of 200 mg/kg/day, which resulted in exposures 280 times (males) and
439 times (females) the clinical dose 20 mg based on AUC.

9. Product Quality
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed NDA 214373 with
respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all
applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such,
OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
Bexagliflozin is a new chemical entity. The chemical name for bexagliflozin is
(2S,3R,4R,5S,6R)-2-(4-chloro-3-(4-(2-cyclopropoxyethoxy)benzyl)phenyl)-6(hydroxymethyl)tetrahydro-2H-pyran-3,4,5-triol. Bexagliflozin tablets are blue film-coated tablet
debossed with “2” and inverted “2” on one side and no markings on the other side. Tablets
contain 20 mg of bexagliflozin along with colloidal silicon dioxide, glyceryl dibehenate, lactose
monohydrate, magnesium stearate, microcrystalline cellulose, polyethylene oxide, and
poloxamer 188. The film coat contains FD&C Blue #1/Brilliant Blue FCF aluminum lake,
FD&C Blue #2/Indigo carmine aluminum lake, macrogol 3350, polyvinyl alcohol, talc, and
titanium dioxide. Bexagliflozin tablets are packaged in a 30 or 90 count HDPE bottle.

9.1. Device or Combination Product

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical
Site and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure
The Applicant states that all clinical trials were conducted in compliance with Good Clinical
Practice, applicable laws, and regulations, and in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki
(1996). Eight domestic clinical investigators were selected for inspection based on their
participation in pivotal efficacy studies conducted to support this application. No significant
regulatory violations were reported by OSI. Based on the results of the inspections and
regulatory assessments, the studies appear to have been conducted adequately, and the data
generated by the Applicant appear acceptable. Review of the financial disclosures did not raise
any concerns about the validity or reliability of the data. Please see Section 20 for a summary of
inspection findings and Section 23 for financial disclosures.

11. Advisory Committee Summary
There was no advisory committee meeting conducted for this application. Although bexagliflozin
is an NME, the efficacy endpoint (HbA1c) was straightforward and well accepted, and there
were no controversial efficacy or safety issues with this fifth-in-class product that would have
benefitted from an advisory committee meeting.

III. Appendices
12. Summary of Regulatory History
The following two sections provide a summary of the regulatory history. The first section
provides a general summary of the regulatory history, while the subsequent section is focused on
regulatory history related to statistical aspects of this application.

12.1. General Regulatory History
Opening IND 103822
The Division received the opening IND 103822 for bexagliflozin on January 7, 2009. Non-hold
issues were identified during the IND safety review regarding aspects of the proposed
nonclinical program, and feedback was provided to the Sponsor on February 13, 2009.
Additional nonclinical and clinical pharmacology feedback was provided on April 8, 2009, June
16, 2009, and September 11, 2009, to which the Sponsor provided responses on June 26, 2009,
and October 19, 2009.

Carcinogenicity SPAs
A request for a carcinogenicity special protocol assessment (SPA) for the protocol “104-Week
Oral Carcinogenicity Study of bexagliflozin in CD-1 Mice,” was received on March 17, 2010,
and denied. It was resubmitted with the requested full report of the mouse toxicity study and
received by the Agency on April 14, 2010. Another carcinogenicity SPA request for the protocol,
“26-Week Oral Gavage Chronic Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study with bexagliflozin in Rats
with a 4-Week Recovery Phase,” was received on June 17, 2010. FDA recommended doses of 0
(distilled water), 0 (vehicle: 1% medium viscosity carboxymethylcellulose), 15, 50, and 150
mg/kg/day in male and female CD1 mice, 0 (distilled water), 0 (vehicle: 30% PEG400 vehicle),
2, 7, and 20 mg/kg/day in male rats, and concurred with the Sponsor’s proposed doses of 0
(distilled water), 0 (30% PEG400 vehicle), 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day in female rats.
On February 1, 2012, the Agency received a carcinogenicity SPA amendment containing dosing
and vehicle changes with which the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC)
did not concur. The Agency responded with advice on February 3, 2012. Further carcinogenicity
protocol amendments and advice requests were received on February 17, 2012, and April 6,
2012. The Agency responded on February 29, and April 5, 2012, respectively. An additional
amendment was received on August 14, 2012, with the Agency again responding on September
5, 2012, with ECAC reiterating their lack of concurrence with dosing and vehicle changes.
On May 10, 2013, the Agency received nonclinical study reports for 104-Week Oral
Carcinogenicity Studies THR-1442-T-405 and THR-1442-T-406, along with a request for
comments on the carcinogenicity studies. The Agency’s reply on July 18, 2013, again reiterated
ECAC’s lack of concurrence with doses and vehicle change, along with a need for a complete
study review. A response from the Sponsor containing the requested study data was received on
November 12, 2013.
113

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

On May 20, 2017, the Agency issued feedback stating that the Division agrees that no additional
assessments of the carcinogenic potential of bexagliflozin are required. However, the Division
reiterated that the assessment of the carcinogenic potential of bexagliflozin in male rats was not
adequate given the limited duration of exposure, due to excessive early mortality. Based on the
absence of preneoplastic findings and tumors in males exposed for up to 88 weeks at 3 times the
MRHD and in females exposed for 100 weeks at doses up to 68 times the MRHD, repeating the
carcinogenicity study in male rats was not considered warranted.

Thorough QT Study
Protocol C-428 for a thorough QT (TQT) study was received on May 19, 2011. The
Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies (IRT-CS) reviewed the protocol (see
memo in DARRTS dated July 12, 2011) and feedback was provided to the Sponsor on July 26,
2011. The Sponsor adequately incorporated feedback from the IRT-CS (see IRT-CS memo in
DARRTS dated March 19, 2013). The clinical study report (CSR) was reviewed by the IRT-CS
on November 4, 2013. The memo concludes that no significant QTc prolongation effect was
detected in C-428.
(b) (4)

End-of-Phase 2 and Follow-Up Meetings
An End-of-Phase 2 (EOP2) Meeting was held with the Sponsor on January 15, 2015, with final
meeting minutes issued on February 12, 2015. The following topics were discussed:
•

•

•

Clinical Dose Selection: The Agency did not agree with the Sponsor’s proposal to select
phase 3 doses based on data from phase 1/2 studies conducted with the capsule
formulation of bexagliflozin and recommended that the Sponsor perform a dose-finding
study with the modified formulation prior to initiating their phase 3 program. The Agency
noted that, given the lack of established bioequivalence between the capsule and tablet
formulations, it is unlikely that the findings from phase 2 trials C-402 and C-418 will be
relevant to the formulation they intend to evaluate in their phase 3 program.
Phase 3 Program: The Sponsor stated that the efficacy and safety of bexagliflozin in the
context of combination therapy will be evaluated in: Trials C-419, C-423, and C-480, in
which metformin will be coadministered; Trial C-420, in which sulfonylureas will be
coadministered; Trial C-470, in which insulin will be coadministered; and Trials C-448
and C-476, in which all approved antidiabetic therapies will be candidates for
coadministration. In addition, Trials C-423 and C-480 will be designed to demonstrate
that bexagliflozin will be as effective as sitagliptin or glimepiride. FDA indicated
concurrence on the proposed phase 3 development program.
Adverse events of special interest (AESIs): In addition to the AESIs proposed by the
Sponsor (hypoglycemia, hypovolemia, falls, fractures, urosepsis, pyelonephritis, urinary
tract infections, genital infections, hepatic adverse events, and malignancies), the Agency
114

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•
•

recommended that the Sponsor also prospectively collect and evaluate hypersensitivity
reactions, hypotension episodes, acid-base disorders, and renal failure events as AESIs.
Studies in Older Adults: The Sponsor proposed not to perform dedicated studies in
older adults. The Agency agreed with this proposal.
Cardiovascular (CV) Risk Assessment: FDA confirmed that the Sponsor’s proposed
major adverse cardiac event (MACE+) primary composite endpoint is acceptable for the
premarketing assessment of CV safety to exclude an excess risk of over 80%. Additional
feedback on the proposed inclusion criteria for C-476 and the statistical analysis plan for
the proposed MACE meta-analysis was also provided.

On July 8, 2016, the Agency received a request for a Type C guidance meeting to follow-up on
the clinical discussions that occurred during the End-of-Phase 2 meeting. Agency feedback was
provided on September 21, 2016. Feedback included the following:
•
•

•

•
•

Dose Selection: The Sponsor requesting feedback regarding dose selection for their
phase 3 program. The Agency encouraged the Sponsor to evaluate two bexagliflozin
dosage strengths.
Insulin Trial: The Sponsor requested FDA feedback on their proposal not to conduct a
dedicated trial evaluating the effectiveness of bexagliflozin as an add-on to insulin
therapy. The Sponsor stated that approximately 50% of the population enrolled in C-476
to date used background insulin and proposed that a substudy of C-476 could be used to
inform the safety and efficacy of bexagliflozin in this population. The Agency noted that
this approach is reasonable provided that the design and conduct of the glycemic efficacy
portion of the trial is consistent with what would be expected if the study were conducted
as a separate study.
Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA): The Sponsor provided their DKA monitoring and analysis
plan for the bexagliflozin clinical program and requested FDA feedback on the DKA case
definitions. FDA stated that the proposed case definitions and DKA monitoring and
analysis plan appear acceptable.
C-476: Several aspects of C-476 were discussed. See section below (C-476 Protocol
Review) for additional information
Amputations: FDA recommended that amputations be included as an AESI. (Additional
FDA recommendations on how amputation events should be monitored, evaluated, and
analyzed was provided on October 25, 2016).

FDA Reviewer Comment: Overall, the Sponsor incorporated key FDA recommendations from
this EOP2 meeting and written response only feedback dated September 21, 2016 into their
clinical development program. The Sponsor did not take the Agency’s recommendation to
evaluate two doses of bexagliflozin in the phase 3 program.

Initial Pediatric Study Plan
On March 12, 2015, the Sponsor submitted an initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP), which received
a written response from the Agency on June 5, 2015. Additional communications between the
Sponsor and Agency resulted in the submission of an Agreed iPSP dated September 28, 2015, to
which the Agency responded with an initial agreement letter on October 27, 2015. The iPSP
states the Sponsor’s intention to request a waiver for pediatric assessments for bexagliflozin for

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

patients aged 0 to 9 years because studies would be impossible or highly impracticable, and to
defer studies in patients aged 10 to 17 years.

C-476 Protocol Review
The Sponsor submitted protocol C-476, entitled “A double blind placebo-controlled study to
evaluate the effects of bexagliflozin on hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) in patients with type 2 diabetes
and increased risk of cardiovascular adverse events” on May 15, 2015. A letter containing
statistical and clinical comments and requests was issued by the Agency on July 30, 2015. A
copy of the clinical endpoint committee (CEC) Charter was also requested for review. The
Sponsor provided responses to FDA’s comments/requests on October 7, 2015. In addition, the
CEC Manual of Operations (i.e., adjudication charter for MACE events), DSMB Charter, and
meta-analysis plan was provided for FDA review and comment. Additional feedback from the
Divisions of Biometrics II and VII was provided January 8, 2016, to which the Sponsor provided
responses on February 23, 2016.
On July 8, 2016, the Agency received a request for a Type C guidance meeting to follow-up on
the clinical discussions that occurred during the EOP2 meeting. The Sponsor requested FDA
agreement regarding updated inclusion/exclusion criteria for C-476 (e.g., change HbA1c criteria
from >7.5%-<11% to >7%-<11%; allowance of enrollment of subjects with a diagnosis of heart
failure (HF) with preserved left ventricular ejection fraction [LVEF]). FDA stated that these
changes to the protocol are generally acceptable given that heart failure is an exploratory
endpoint in C-476.
(b) (4)

PreNDA Meeting
On October 1, 2019, FDA received a request for a preNDA meeting. Final meeting minutes were
issued December 17, 2019. The meeting focused on the proposed NDA package and its
sufficiency to support a marking application for bexagliflozin for the treatment of T2DM.

12.2. Statistical Regulatory History
The Division of Metabolism and Endocrinology Products (DMEP)4 received Theracos’ IND
103822 for bexagliflozin for treatment of T2DM on January 7, 2009. Since then, the FDA
statistical team has been providing review comments/statistical advice to the Sponsor regarding
study design and analysis methodologies.

After Center for Drug Evaluation and Research (CDER)’s 2019 reorganization, Division of Diabetes, Lipid
Disorders, and Obesity (DDLO).

The statistical advice has been focused on aspects of missing data related to trial design and
analysis methods: missing data prevention, estimand, and missing data handling strategies.
Among them, advice common to all pivotal trials are summarized below.
•
•
•
•
•

FDA considers the treatment policy estimand critical to support effectiveness.
Provided advice on strategies for missing data prevention
Reiterated the importance of “off treatment” data collection
Analysis population should include all randomized subjects regardless of treatment
adherence or availability of follow-up data.
For the primary analysis model, at different stages, the following advice has been given:
— Advice against the last observation carried forward (LOCF) approach in missing data
handling;
— Advice against the mixed model repeated measures (MMRM) approach in missing
data handling;
— Recommended addressing missing data by having the missing data from subjects who
do not adhere to therapy represented by the data from those subjects on the same arm
that also did not adhere to therapy but had the measurement;
— Recommended retrieved drop-out based pattern mixture modeling approach; in case
the retrieved drop-out is not enough to support this analysis, recommended a returnto-baseline approach in missing data handling.

•

Recommended to use sensitivity analyses to study the limitation of the missing data
assumptions made in the primary analysis.
— Recommended the tipping point sensitivity analysis

•
•
•
•

Implemented actions in all phase 2/3 controlled studies to minimize missing data
The Sponsor still proposed MMRM as primary analysis for all studies, even though the
Sponsor agrees that MMRM cannot be used to account for missing data entirely.
The primary analysis will include the post-rescue data.
Proposed a list of sensitivity analysis including the tipping point analysis method.

Absorption Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion (ADME)/Pharmacokinetics
Absorption
Following oral administration, bexagliflozin was rapidly absorbed with a time to maximum
concentration (Tmax) ranging between 0.4 to 2.5 hours in rats, dogs, and monkeys. Exposure
(maximum plasma concentration [Cmax] and area under the concentration-time curve [AUC0-t])
generally increased in a dose-proportional manner. Oral bioavailability in rats, dogs, and
monkeys ranged from 46% to 82%.

Abbreviations: AUC00-∞, area under the curve from zero to infinity; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; F, bioavailability; MRT,
mean residence time; T1/2, half life; Tmax, time to maximum plasma concentration.

Distribution
Bexagliflozin was 93 to 97% bound to plasma proteins across all species tested (human, rhesus
monkeys, beagle dog, SD rat, and CD-1 mouse).
In a distribution study in rats, the kidney medulla and cortex, liver, adrenal cortex and medulla,
pancreas, harderian gland, and preputial gland showed the highest bexagliflozin concentration
between 4 to 12 hours postdose.
In lactating female rats, bexagliflozin distributed into maternal milk with a concentration higher
than maternal plasma (1.4 to 1.8 times), which resulted in detectable plasma levels of
bexagliflozin in nursing pups at levels 20 to 50 times lower than in milk. Levels in rat milk and
nursing pups are not predictive of drug levels in human milk or nursing infants.
Metabolism
Metabolism studies were conducted in vitro using liver microsomes or recombinant cytochrome
(CYP) P450 enzymes, and in vivo using radiolabeled test article. The metabolic profile was
similar across the species tested and there were no unique or disproportionate human
metabolites. Parent drug was the predominant drug-related component in the circulation in rats,
monkeys, and humans. In humans, the majority of biotransformation appears to be by phase II
metabolism and the most abundant circulating metabolite was glucose 3’-O-gluconide (32.8% in
humans versus 22.8% in monkeys), which was considered qualified given the high clinical
exposure multiples achieved in monkeys. An additional oxidative metabolite, EGT0001494, was
also observed in monkeys. Oxidative metabolites (EGT0001301, EGT0001663, EGT0001494)
were identified in incubations with animal and human liver microsomes, as well as in animal
studies, with EGT0001301 (O-dealkylated metabolite) being the predominant metabolite. The
metabolic enzymes CYP3A4 and UGT1A9 were responsible for the metabolism. After oral or
intravenous dosing, the terminal plasma half-life of bexagliflozin in rats, dogs, and monkeys
ranged between 3 to 5 hours.

No induction/inhibition of drug metabolizing enzymes and/or drug-drug interactions were
observed (Section 5).
Excretion
Following an oral dose of radio-labeled bexagliflozin, fecal excretion was the primary route of
elimination, accounting for 86.6% and 54.1% in rats and monkeys, respectively. Urinary
excretion was the secondary route, accounting for 14.4% and 29.2% in rats and monkeys,
respectively. The radiolabel in excreta consisted mainly of parent drug.

Toxicokinetic Data
Systemic exposures in rats and monkeys were generally dose proportional. No accumulation
occurred with repeated dosing. Safety margins were calculated based on average AUC for males
and females unless otherwise specified, using a clinical exposure of 1259 ng.h/mL at the
maximum recommended human dosage (MRHD) of 20 mg/day.

13.1.1.4. Toxicology
13.1.1.4.1. General Toxicology

26-Week Oral Gavage Chronic Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study With
Bexagliflozin in Rats With a 4-Week Recovery Phase
Key Study Findings
•
•
•

•

•

Rats were administered bexagliflozin by oral gavage at doses of 1, 3, 10, or 30 mg/kg/day
for 26 weeks
Consistent with the intended pharmacology, marked glucosuria and slightly decreased
plasma glucose levels were observed in all bexagliflozin treatment groups.
Chronic progressive nephropathy (CPN), particularly in the male treatment groups, was
observed at ≥3 mg/kg. The severity of this finding remained increased in the high dose
recovery males (slight/moderate). CPN is a common background finding in aging male
Sprague Dawley rats and is generally not considered to be relevant to human risk.
A low incidence and severity (minimal to slight) of glandular stomach erosion/ulcer was
observed in all male treatment groups, not dose-related, at Week 26 or after 4 weeks of
recovery, and in high dose females at Week 26. Similar stomach findings have been
observed with other sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitors. The clinical
relevance of stomach erosion is likely limited considering the low incidence and severity
and due to the absence of similar findings in other species (monkeys with bexagliflozin
and dogs with other drugs in this class), as well as in humans with other drugs in this
class.
The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was considered 30 mg/kg (54031 and
112292 ng.h/mL, 43- and 89-fold MRHD, respectively).

Study in Rats
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
None
Clinical signs
Rough hair coat was observed for individual males in the 10 and 30 mg/kg/day
male groups and for most groups including the control in the female treated
animals.
Body weights
No statistically significant body weight changes
Ophthalmoscopy
No remarkable findings
ECG
Not performed
Hematology
No remarkable findings
Clinical chemistry Relative to control, decreased serum glucose (up to -25%), increased urea nitrogen
(up to 2-fold) and ALT (up to 1.6-fold) were observed in bexagliflozin treated
groups. All these findings resolved after 4 weeks of recovery period.
Urinalysis
Consistent with the test article pharmacology, dose-related increases in urinary
glucose concentration (up to 445-fold), glucose excretion (>1000-fold), urine
volume (up to 2-fold) and specific gravity (up to 1.5-fold) were observed. All findings
resolved after 4 weeks of recovery period.
Gross pathology
Bexagliflozin treatment groups showed discolored (black, ran, red or dark red) foci
in the mucosa of the glandular stomach (males at ≥3 mg/kg/day; females at
30 mg/kg/day) which correlated with microscopic findings of minimal to slight
erosion/ulcer.
Organ weights
Increased organ weights in the kidney (up to 30.5%), adrenal gland (up to 20%),
and liver (up to 14%), and decreased prostate gland weight (up to -28%) were
observed in bexagliflozin treatment groups, in a dose-related manner. Organ weight
changes in the liver, adrenal and prostate did not correlate with microscopic
changes.

129

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Parameters
Histopathology
Adequate battery:
Yes

Major Findings
Bexagliflozin treatment exacerbated chronic progressive nephropathy in males and
caused glandular stomach erosion/ulcer in both males and females. These findings
were not resolved or only partially resolved after 4 weeks of recovery period.
Chronic progressive nephropathy (CPN) represents a common rodent-specific
finding that typically increases in incidence and severity with age in rats, with males
more greatly affected than females. The CPN was characterized by dilated tubules
filled with proteinaceous fluid in the inner cortical region and basophilic
regenerating tubules in the cortex with aggregates of pigment-laden macrophages
within the interstitium around regenerating tubules.
Minimal to slight glandular stomach erosion/ulcer, characterized by minimal to
moderate degeneration of gastric epithelium associated with submucosal edema
and/or submucosal inflammation in more extensive lesions, was observed in all
treated male groups, not dose related, and in high dose females. This finding was
still present in high dose males after the recovery period.

Kidney tubular vacuolation (minimal to slight in severity) was observed in all
bexagliflozin treatment groups at Week 39 and in one recovery male at 20 mg/kg after 4

toxicologically significant.

The liver weight was increased in all bexagliflozin treated groups (up to 1.2-fold in

gastric mucosa from these animals. Given the low incidence and absence of doseresponse, this finding is considered incidental.

recovery male at 20 mg/kg.

13.1.1.4.2. Genetic Toxicology
Bexagliflozin was not mutagenic or clastogenic in the Ames mutagenicity assay, in vitro
mammalian chromosomal aberration assay, in vivo micronucleus test in rats, and in vivo
unscheduled hepatic DNA synthesis in rats.

13.1.1.4.3. Carcinogenicity

104-Week Oral Carcinogenicity Study of Bexagliflozin in Sprague-Dawley
Rats
Male rats were administered bexagliflozin by oral gavage in 1% medium viscosity
carboxymethylcellulose at 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg for 507 days (72.4 weeks) and then at 1, 3, and
10 mg/kg for additional 12, 5, and 2 weeks for the low dose, mid dose, and high dose group,
respectively. In the male study, dosing was stopped at Week 75, 78, and 85 and the groups were
terminated at Week 82, 85, and 88 at the high, mid, and low dose, respectively. Female rats were
administered bexagliflozin at 3, 10 and 30 mg/kg for 100 weeks. Treatment-related findings
included higher mortality in males, lower body weight (up to -14%) and higher food
consumption (up to 26%) in both males and females. Treatment-related exacerbation of CPN and
associated secondary findings (widespread vascular mineralization and diffuse hyperplasia of
parathyroid gland), observed primarily in males, were considered to be the cause of death. There
was no increase in the incidence of preneoplastic findings or tumors in any treatment group at
doses up to 30 mg/kg/day.
The study in males was conducted at doses that exceeded those recommended by the Executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC: 2, 7, and 20 mg/kg/day), which were selected
based on adverse kidney findings (increased kidney weight and chronic progressive
nephropathy) in a 6-month dose ranging study. ECAC and the Division considered the study in
males inadequate to assess the risk of carcinogenicity because the duration of exposure was
limited. However, based on the absence of preneoplastic findings and tumors in males exposed
for up to 88 weeks at 3 times the MRHD and in females exposed for 100 weeks at doses up to 68
times the MRHD, repeating the carcinogenicity study in male rats was not considered warranted.

104-Week Oral Carcinogenicity Study of Bexagliflozin in CD-1 Mice
Mice were administered bexagliflozin by oral gavage at 0 (1% medium viscosity
carboxymethylcellulose in water), 15, 50, or 150 mg/kg/day for approximately 2 years (males,
104 weeks; females, 97 weeks). The high dose of 150 mg/kg was selected based on AUC ratio
and maximum tolerated dose (MTD) in males based on mortality at 120 to 160 times the clinical
AUC. ECAC concurred with the Applicant’s dose selection. No treatment-related non-neoplastic

134

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

or neoplastic findings were observed at doses up to 150 mg/kg, corresponding to an AUC of
170550 ng.h/mL (135 times the MRHD of 20 mg/day).

Oral Gavage Study of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to
Implantation With Bexagliflozin in Rats
Key Findings
There were no bexagliflozin-related adverse effects on male and female fertility or early
embryonic development up to the highest dose of 200 mg/kg (237-fold MRHDAUC, based on an
AUC of 299,000 ng.h/mL derived from a 28-day rat study).

Cesarean section data:
Implantation sites, pre-implantation loss, and postimplantation loss were not

THR-1442-T-394: Developmental Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Oral Gavage
Study With Bexagliflozin in Rabbits
Key Findings
Maternal:
•

Abortions or moribund sacrifice with no viable fetuses at 150 mg/kg

Fetal:
•
•

•

Significantly decreased gestational weight gain (-65%) at 150 mg/kg
Malformations in two fetuses from two dams administered bexagliflozin 150 mg/kg:
pulmonary trunk stenosis in one fetus and adipose tissue abnormal distribution,
protruding tongue and abnormal position of the digits in the second fetus were noted.
Although occurred at low incidence (litter incidence: 6.2% for each malformation, 12.4%
for total malformations), these malformations are rare and possibly treatment-related, but
they are not considered clinically relevant as they occurred at high exposure multiples
(282-fold the MRHD based on AUC).
NOAEL was 25 mg/kg (AUC0-24h, 42,905 ng.h/mL, 34 times the MRHD based on AUC))
for both maternal and fetal toxicity.

Table 71. Observations and Results for Oral Gavage Study for Effects on Pre- and Postnatal
Development, Including Maternal Function, With Bexagliflozin in Rats, F0-Generation Dams
Parameters
Major findings
Mortality
Moribund sacrifices occurred in three mid dose animals (one at LD2 and two at LD0)
and two high dose animals (one at GD9 and one another at LD1). Clinical signs of poor
physical conditions were observed at mid gestation phase (HD), late gestation phase
(all doses) and lactation phase (MD and HD). It seems that bexagliflozin treatment
weakened animal’s tolerance to the stress related to late gestation and delivery
process. An additional high dose female was sacrificed on lactation Day 0 due to
stillbirths of most pups.
Clinical signs Dose- and duration-related clinical signs, including rough hair coat, nonformed/liquid
feces, red or cloudy genital discharge, thin appearance, hunched appearance,
squinting, irregular respiration and cold to touch, were observed at 40 mg/kg
throughout lactation, and at 200 mg/kg in mid to late gestation. Similar findings except
irregular respiration and cold to touch were also seen in animals at 7 and 40 mg/kg in
the late gestation phase

Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

THR-1442-T-482: A 10-Week Toxicity Study of Bexagliflozin by Oral
Administration (Gavage) in Juvenile Rats With a 4-Week Recovery Period
Key Findings
•
•

•

•

Bexagliflozin doses: 0, 0.3, 3, 30 mg/kg/day.
Increased kidney weight in all treated female groups (up to 45%) and in males at
≥3 mg/kg/day (up to 35%) correlated with increased incidence of minimal to mild renal
dilatation in males at ≥3 mg/kg/day (11 times the MRHD) and in females at
30 mg/kg/day (139 times the MRHD). There were no toxicologically significant changes
in kidney injury molecule-1 (KIM-I), a biomarker of tubular injury. A longer treatment
duration (~26 weeks) in adult rats (e.g., 6 weeks old) did not alter renal structures,
suggesting a particularly susceptible window for renal toxicity if exposed to bexagliflozin
during postnatal weeks 3 to 6. This susceptible window in the young rats is characterized
by active morphological and functional development of the kidneys and is equivalent to
the events in the second and third trimester of human gestation and continuing until
approximately 2 years of age.
Increased trabecular bone (femur) in the high dose groups (127 times the MRHD),
associated with increased trabecular bone mineral content (BMC) and bone mineral
density (BMD) at the distal metaphysis in males. No changes were observed in recovery
animals. Similar findings have been observed with other approved SGLT2 inhibitors and
are likely related to calorie-wasting and/or off-target inhibition of SGLT1, which is not
expected at the clinical exposure.
NOAEL was 0.3 mg/kg/day in males (1,370 ng.h/mL, at the MRHD) and 3 mg/kg/day in
females (14,400 ng.h/mL, 11 times the MRHD) based on renal dilatation.

(Gavage) in Juvenile Rats With a 4-Week Recovery Period

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Parameters
Clinical
Chemistry

Urine
Chemistry

Necropsy
findings

Major findings
No toxicological significant findings. On PND 91, there were mild increases (up to 1.8fold) in urea nitrogen in high dose males and in females at >3 mg/kg/day. No changes
in creatinine were observed. Additionally, slight decreases in total protein and albumin
in high dose females (6% and 7%, respectively) and slight increase in potassium levels
in high dose males and females (7% and 8%, respectively) were noted. All these
findings were reversible after 4 weeks of recovery period.
Consistent with the intended pharmacology, moderate to marked increases in urine
glucose excretion in all treatment groups and mild increases in renal calcium (up to 5.9
times the control group) and/or phosphorus excretion (up to 1.9 times the control
group) in all treatment groups, except 0.3 mg/kg/day males, were observed.
There were no toxicologically relevant bexagliflozin-related effects in urinary kidney
injury molecule-1 (KIM-1), a marker of renal tubule injury.
Increased incidence of renal pelvic dilatation in males at ≥3 mg/kg/day.

Source: Applicant’s submission

Organ weight

Dose-related increases in the kidney weights and liver weights were observed. These
findings were reversible.

Source: Applicant’s submission

151

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Parameters
Histopathology

Major findings
Renal pelvic dilatation and renal tubular dilatation were noted in males at ≥3 mg/kg/day
and in females at 30 mg/kg/day.
Increased trabecular femoral bone (42% in both mineral content and mineral density)
was also observed microscopically at 30 mg/kg/day or 81 times the MRHDAUC.
The kidney findings were not fully reversible but trabecular bone findings were fully
reversible after 4 weeks of recovery period.

Source: Applicant’s submission

Biomarkers of
bone turnover

Procollagen Type I N-terminal propeptide (PINP) was slightly reduced (up to -44%) in
males at >3 mg/kg, without changes in C-telopeptides of Type I collagen (CTxI). This
effect on bone metabolism was associated with a minimal decrease in femur length (2%) and increase in trabecular bone mineral content (BMC) and bone mineral density
(BMD; +42% for both) in high dose males (127 times the MRHD). None of these
changes persisted into the recovery period. There were no significant findings on bone
measurements or pQCT parameters at the midfemur diaphysis in males or females.

14.1.2. Protein Binding (Study THR-1442-C-424)
Method: The effect of concentration on the plasma protein binding of bexagliflozin was
assessed by equilibrium dialysis at 37ºC for 7 hours, the experimentally determined equilibration
time, in pooled plasma from subjects with T2DM and normal renal function at concentrations of
25, 50, 100, 250, and 500 ng/mL. Subsequently, in vitro experiments to assess whether or not
bexagliflozin and its major metabolite EGT0002149 would interact with each other with respect
to their binding to plasma proteins were performed by equilibrium dialysis at 37ºC for 7 hours.
Finally, the plasma protein binding of bexagliflozin was determined by equilibrium dialysis at
37ºC for 7 hours in predose and 3-hour postdose plasma samples obtained from subjects enrolled
in Trial C-424.
Results:
•
•
•

•

The mean percentages of bexagliflozin bound to plasma proteins of donors with type 2
diabetes and normal renal function ranged from 90.9 to 93.0%, with no dependence on
concentration over the range of 25 to 500 ng/mL.
The mean percentages of bexagliflozin bound to plasma proteins when incubated with 0,
75, or 150 ng/mL EGT0002149 were 90.4, 90.3, and 89.3%, respectively, indicating that
EGT0002149 did not affect the extent of bexagliflozin bound to plasma proteins.
The mean or average percentages of bexagliflozin bound to plasma proteins when
incubated with 0, 75, or 150 ng/mL bexagliflozin were 90.6, 88.7, and 86.3%,
respectively, suggesting that bexagliflozin had a minor effect on the extent of
EGT0002149 bound to plasma proteins.
The mean or individual percentages of bexagliflozin bound to plasma proteins of 3-hour
postdose plasma samples from subjects enrolled in Trial C-424 analyzed ex vitro ranged
from 73.7 to 92.7%. However, with the exception of a single replicate sample at 73.7%
(b) (6) ), all other postdose samples ranged from 83.7 to 92.7%.

14.1.4. In Vitro Metabolism in Human Liver
Microsomes (Study THR-1474-N-341)
Methods: [14C] bexagliflozin (100 μM) was incubated in the presence of human liver
microsomes for 0, 15, 30, and 60 min at 37°C and a protein concentration of 1 mg/mL. Blank
incubations were conducted (60 min) in the absence of microsomes and presence of an NADPH
regenerating system and in the presence of microsomes and absence of an NADPH regenerating
system to assess chemical degradation. Samples were analyzed with high-performance liquid
chromatography (HPLC) using on-line ultraviolet (UV) and radiochemical detection.
Results:
•
•

At the end of the 60 min incubation, turnover was approximately 9%.
The predominant CYP450 mediated metabolite (based on retention time) in human liver
microsomes was EGT0001301. EGT0001663 and M6 were also detected as minor
metabolites.

14.1.5. In Vitro Metabolism by Recombinant CYP3A
(Study THR-1474-N-367)
Methods: Bexagliflozin (50 μM or 20 μM) was mixed with cell membrane preparations
containing human recombinant CYP3A4 (20 pmol/mL or 10 pmol/mL) in potassium phosphate
buffer (pH 7.4). An equal volume of NADPH regeneration system was added and incubated at
37°C for either 30 or 90 minutes. The reaction was terminated by the addition of acetonitrile to a
final concentration of 33.33% and centrifugation at 10,000 g for 3 minutes. High performance
liquid chromatography-photodiode-array mass spectrometry (HPLC-PDA/MS) was used to
identify the metabolites.
Results:
•

The incubation of bexagliflozin with human recombinant CYP3A4 resulted in the
identification of two metabolites, EGT0001301 and EGT0001663.

14.1.6. In Vitro Metabolism by Human Liver
Microsomes and Recombinant UGT (Study THR1474-N-377)
Methods: The human liver microsome incubations were conducted at a microsomal protein
concentration of 2 mg/mL and a single concentration of bexagliflozin (1mM) for 2 h; NADPH
(2.0mM when present), UDPGA (2.0mM when present), magnesium chloride (10mM), and
EDTA (2mM) in a total volume of 0.5 mL PBS buffer (100mM, pH 7.4) were also added to the
reaction system. Reactions were terminated by adding ice-cold acetonitrile to the reaction
mixtures.
Reaction mixtures containing 50mM Tris (pH 7.5), 10mM MgCl2, 5mM UDP-glucuronic acid,
0.025 mg/ml alamethicin, 0.35 µg/µl protein of different human recombinant UGT membrane
preparations (UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, UGT1A10, UGT2B7), and 100µM bexagliflozin
were incubated at 370C for 120 min in 200 µl volume. Reactions were terminated by the addition
of 100 µl of acetonitrile, centrifuged at 10,000 g for 3 min and the supernatants were transferred
to fresh tubes.
The incubation samples were analyzed by a developed HPLC-PDA/MS method.
Results:
•
•

In human liver microsomes incubations containing solely UDPGA, the only glucuronide
conjugate of bexagliflozin detected was metabolite M5 (EGT0002149).
Following incubation with human liver microsomes fortified with UDPGA and NADPH,
three additional metabolites M1 (EGT0001301), M2 (EGT0001494) and M4
(EGT0001663) were characterized in addition to M5. M5 was also identified following
incubation with UDPGA and recombinant UGT1A9.
156

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Conclusion: UGT1A9 is the major UGT isoform for glucuronidation in vitro.

14.1.7. In Vitro CYP and UGT Phenotyping by
Recombinant Human CYP and UGT Enzymes
(Study THR-1442-N-440)
Methods: For phase I metabolism, bexagliflozin (10 and 100 μM) was incubated for 30 minutes
with a panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes (rCYPs, namely rCYP1A2, rCYP2B6,
rCYP2C8, rCYP2C9, rCYP2C19, rCYP2D6 and rCYP3A4, 10 pmol/incubation) and two
negative controls (Bactosome control and reductase control). For phase II metabolism,
bexagliflozin (10 and 100 μM), was incubated with two recombinant human UGT enzymes
(rUGTs, namely rUGT1A1 and rUGT2B7) and negative control (insect cell control). Metabolite
production was monitored using an LC/MS/MS method that was transferred and modified for
concentrations of the metabolites ranging from 10 to 1000 ng/mL. Positive controls were
conducted in parallel using prototype substrates for each respective enzyme.
Results:
Recombinant Human CYP Enzymes
•

•

From an initial concentration of 10 μM bexagliflozin, CYP3A4 caused 16% depletion
and CYP2D6 approximately 12% depletion. From an initial concentration of 100 μM
bexagliflozin, depletion greater than 10% was only observed in incubations with
CYP3A4 (approximately 17%). Depletion by less than 10% was observed for the other
recombinant CYP enzymes evaluated (data not shown).
Bexagliflozin was metabolized in vitro by recombinant human CYP enzymes
(predominantly by CYP3A4, with minor contributions from CYP2C8 and 2D6) to
EGT0001301 and EGT0001685 (Table 80).

14.1.8. Identification of Additional Metabolites (Study
THR-1474-N-382)
Methods: Bioanalyses performed on plasma, urine, and fecal samples collected from subjects
after administration of a single oral dose of bexagliflozin (150 mg, cohort 6) in Trial C-328. The
metabolites of bexagliflozin in human urine, feces, and plasma were also identified and
characterized using an ultra-high performance liquid chromatography-quadrupole time-of-flight
mass spectrometry (UPLC/Q-TOF MS) via positive and negative scan modes in SCDMPK.
Results:
•

In addition to the six known metabolites (three oxidative metabolites: EGT0001301,
EGT0001494 and EGT0001663, and three bexagliflozin glucuronides), six additional
minor metabolites were identified and partially characterized (Figure 20).
— In plasma, two additional metabolites were found: UM4, the product of oxidation of
the bexagliflozin glucose 6′ carbon to a carboxylic acid (bexagliflozin glucuronic
acid) and UM6-2, a glucuronide of EGT0001301.
— In urine, six additional metabolites were found: UM4, described above, UM5-1 and 2, two glucuronides of the phenol resulting from abstraction of the bexagliflozin
ethyloxycyclopropyl moiety, UM6-1 and -2, two glucuronides of EGT0001301, and
UM7, a glucuronide of EGT0001494.
— In feces, UM4 was also found.

Figure 20. Minor Metabolites of Bexagliflozin

Source: Figure 2.7.2-7 Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies

FDA Reviewer Comment: None of the metabolites contribute to the pharmacological activity of
bexagliflozin.

14.1.11. Induction Effect of Bexagliflozin on CYP450
Activities (Study THR-1474-N-347)
Methods: Plated cryopreserved human hepatocytes were separately incubated with 0.05, 0.50,
and 5.0 μM solutions of EGT0001474 in supplemented William’s E media for 48 hours. The
selected concentrations were expected to approximate and significantly exceed the anticipated
clinical exposure conditions. Controls of 1 μM 3-methylcholanthrene, 10 μM rifampicin, and 25
μM rifampicin in the same media were used to establish positive induction responses for
CYP1A2, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4, respectively. The activities of CYP1A2, CYP2C19, and
CYP3A4 were determined by incubating the cells with solutions of phenacetin, S-mephenytoin,
and testosterone, respectively, in the same media. Samples of the media were removed,
extracted, and analyzed for the metabolites of the probe substrate using HPLC in conjunction
with LC/MS/MS.

14.1.12. Inhibitory Effect of Bexagliflozin on CYP2C8
Activity (Study THR-1442-N-439)
Methods: To evaluate bexagliflozin as a direct, time-dependent, and metabolism-dependent
inhibitor of CYP2C8 activity, human liver microsomes from a pool of sixteen individuals were
incubated with the marker substrate in the presence or absence of bexagliflozin To distinguish
between time-dependent and metabolism-dependent inhibition, bexagliflozin was preincubated
with human liver microsomes for 30 minutes without and with an NADPH-generating system,
respectively, prior to incubation with the marker substrate. Known direct and metabolismdependent inhibitors of CYP2C8 were included as positive controls, and negative controls
included the absence of bexagliflozin.
Results:
•

Under the experimental conditions examined, there was little or no evidence of direct,
time-dependent, or metabolism-dependent inhibition of CYP2C8 by bexagliflozin.
Bexagliflozin is unlikely to inhibit CYP2C8 at the clinically relevant dose.

14.1.14. P-gp Substrate and Inhibitory Potential
(Study THR-1442-N-432)
Methods: For bidirectional transport assays, Caco-2 cells were grown to confluence for 14 to 21
days on 1.0 µm BD Falcon filters in 24-well plates. Monolayer integrity was confirmed by
transepithelial electrical resistance values greater than 400 ohms-cm2 prior to assay initiation and
monitored during the studies by Lucifer yellow transfer. The bidirectional apparent permeability
coefficients (Papp) and efflux ratio were determined for 1 to 100µM bexagliflozin using 20minute sampling intervals over 2 hours. For P-gp inhibitor assays, transport of [3H] Digoxin
transport was assessed in the presence of 100µM bexagliflozin.
Results:
•

•

The efflux ratios were 13, 6.7, and 5.4 for 1, 10, and 100 μM bexagliflozin, respectively.
The inclusion of 20 μM cyclosporin A (a potent inhibitor of P-gp) with 10 μM
bexagliflozin reduced the efflux ratio to 1.9, indicating that bexagliflozin is a potential Pgp substrate.
The presence of 100 μM bexagliflozin reduced the efflux ratio for [³H]-digoxin from 10.3
to 3.5 suggesting that bexagliflozin at 100 μM is a potential inhibitor of P-gp but is not as
potent as 20 μM cyclosporin A, which reduced the efflux ratio for [³H]-digoxin to 1.5.
Calculation of the half-maximal inhibition constant (IC50) for this interaction based on
efflux ratio yielded an estimated value of 3.7 μM (2.3 to 6 μM range).

FDA Reviewer Comment: The determined IC50 is much higher than the Cmax of bexagliflozin
following the oral dose of 20 mg with calculated R1<1.02. R1 in the gut is estimated to be about
6 and is less than the cut-off value of 11 in the DDI Guidance (January 2020). Therefore,
bexagliflozin is unlikely to be a significant P-gp inhibitor in the clinical setting. Nonetheless, the
Applicant conducted a clinical DDI study and demonstrated the lack of significant effect of
bexagliflozin on the pharmacokinetics (PK) of the sensitive P-gp substrate digoxin.

14.1.15. Inhibitory Effect of Bexagliflozin on
Transporters (Study THR-1442-N-441)
Methods: Vesicles or cells transfected with control vectors were used as negative controls and
the validity of each assay was confirmed with a positive control inhibitor for each transporter.
Bidirectional apparent permeability coefficients, the resulting efflux ratios (for breast cancer
resistance protein [BCRP]), and the cleared volume (for BSEP, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, OCT1, and OCT2) were quantified using transporter specific substrates in the
presence and absence of bexagliflozin at 1 and 100 μmol L⁻¹. Each assay was conducted in
duplicate and mean values were reported for each assay. For BCRP, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3,
which were inhibited significantly by 100 μM bexagliflozin, IC50 values were determined.
Results:
•

At concentrations up to 100µM, bexagliflozin did not inhibit BSEP, OAT1, OAT3,
OCT1, and OCT2 (Table 87).

164

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•
•

The rank order for inhibition at 100 μM bexagliflozin was OATP1B1>OATP1B3>BCRP.
However, the IC50 values were high multiples of the expected Cmax produced by the
20 mg bexagliflozin tablet (0.38 μM).
Therefore, no meaningful inhibition on BCRP, BSEP, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, OCT1, and OCT2 is expected from the plasma levels produced by the
intended dosage strength.

and MATE2-K, are unlikely to affect bexagliflozin PK. This is based on the observation from the
mass balance study where only 2% of administered dose was found as the parent drug
(bexagliflozin) in urine.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Single and Multiple Dose in Healthy Subjects
(Trial C-328)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-escalation study conducted in 78
healthy male and female subjects to assess the safety, tolerability, PK, and pharmacodynamics
(PD) (24 hours urinary glucose excretion [UGE]) of rising single and multiple oral doses of
bexagliflozin. In the study, bexagliflozin was administered as an L-proline: bexagliflozin 2:1
cocrystal (EGT0001474) formulated in neat-filled hypromellose capsules. Therefore,
EGT0001474 doses of 2.5 mg to 150 mg listed below are cocrystal doses and are equivalent to
bexagliflozin doses of 1.67 to 100 mg.
•

In Part 1: subjects were randomized to receive either a single oral dose of placebo or
EGT0001474 at 2.5 mg, 5 mg, 10 mg, 25 mg, 75 mg, or 150 mg in the fasted state.
166

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•

Subjects in the food effect cohort received a second dose of placebo or 25 mg
EGT0001474 after a standard high fat breakfast.
In Part 2: subjects received single oral daily doses of placebo or EGT0001474 at 10 mg,
50 mg, or 150 mg in the fasted state for 14 days.

Serial PK, safety, and PD evaluations were performed at prespecified times throughout the study.

Results
•
•
•
•
•

Following the oral administration of bexagliflozin capsules, peak plasma concentrations
of bexagliflozin reached between 2 to 4 hours postdose (Figure 21).
Based on data from the single ascending dose (SAD) portion of the study, bexagliflozin
exhibits a linear PK (as assessed by Cmax and AUC) in EGT0001474 doses ranging from
2.5 mg to 150 mg (equivalent to 1.7 mg to 100 mg bexagliflozin, Table 90).
Food delayed Tmax by 1 to 2 hours and decreased the mean Cmax and AUC0-inf by
approximately 34% and 10%, respectively, when compared to the fasted state.
Based on data from the multiple ascending dose (MAD) portion of the study, there is no
significant accumulation after multiple doses of bexagliflozin capsules once daily for 14
days at 6.7 mg to 100 mg with accumulation ratios ranging from 1.10 to 1.17 (Table 90).
Bexagliflozin increased UGE in healthy subjects in a dose-dependent and saturable
manner with plateau between 16.7 mg and 50 mg of bexagliflozin (Figure 22).

(Right) Ascending Doses

Conclusion
•
•

Bexagliflozin was well tolerated up to 100 mg per day for 14 days.
Bexagliflozin, administered as oral capsules, was rapidly absorbed, and resulted in dosedependent increases in systemic exposure and dose-related saturable glucosuria.

FDA Reviewer Comment: Linear PK in the dose range of 1.7 mg to 100 mg is based on Cmax
and AUC values. The terminal t1/2 values appear to increase with increasing bexagliflozin doses.
Nonetheless, based on limited accumulation between 6.7 and 100 mg, the effective t1/2 may be
comparable among the doses. Based on data from Part 2, after 14 days of repeat dosing, UGE
declined by about 13% in the 100 mg bexagliflozin/day cohort and 26% in the 16.7 mg
bexagliflozin/day cohort when compared to the UGE values measured on Day 1 in the same
cohort.

14.2.2. SAD Study in Patients With Type 2 Diabetes
(Trial C-396)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, single-dose, double-blind, placebo-controlled study to assess the PK, PD
(UGE), and safety tolerability of bexagliflozin following the oral administration of 25 mg,
75 mg, and 150 mg EGT0001474 (capsules; equivalent to 16.7, 50, and 100 mg bexagliflozin) in
subjects with T2DM. During the study, 24 subjects (3 cohorts of 8 subjects per cohort) were
randomized in a ratio of 3:1 to bexagliflozin or placebo groups. Serial PK, safety, and PD
evaluations were performed at prespecified times throughout the study.

Results
•
•
•
•

Upon absorption, the maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) of bexagliflozin was reached
at 2 to 3 hours postdose (range 0.5 to 8 hours) (Figure 23).
Systemic exposures (Cmax and AUC) of bexagliflozin showed a linear dose
proportionality in the studied dose range of 16.7 mg and 100 mg (Table 91).
Although the amount of UGE increased significantly compared to that measured in
subjects taking placebo, the increase (24 hour UGE between 90 to 120 g) was comparable
at the studied doses of 16.7 mg and 100 mg (Table 92).
Single doses of bexagliflozin decreased the fasting plasma glucose (FPG) levels by
29 mg/dL, 27 mg/dL, and 26 mg/dL, respectively, 24 hours after bexagliflozin
administration at the doses of 16.7 mg, 50 mg, and 100 mg (Table 93).

14.2.3. SAD and MAD Study in Healthy Subjects and
Patients With T2DM (Trial C-445)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose escalation study to assess the safety,
tolerability, PK, and PD of bexagliflozin (EGT0002142 sustained release (SR) tablet) in
Japanese healthy subjects and patients with T2DM. The treatment plan is described below:

Results
Part 1: SAD in Healthy Japanese Subjects
•

•

•

•

Following a single dose oral administration of bexagliflozin tablets, a dose-proportional
increase in Cmax and AUC of bexagliflozin was observed in the studied dose range of
3 mg to 90 mg. The percentage of dose excreted in urine (Fe %dose) as bexagliflozin was
2.17 to 2.76% of the dose and thus, renal clearance (CLr) was a minor component of the
total clearance (Figure 24 and Table 95).
A dose-proportional increase in Cmax and AUC of metabolite EGT0002149 was observed.
The Fe %dose as metabolite EGT0002149 was 28.4 to 34.8% of the dose and thus, CLr
was a significant component of the total clearance of the metabolite (Figure 24 and Table
96).
For bexagliflozin, subjects dosed in the fed state exhibited a higher Cmax compared to
subjects dosed in the fasted state. The terminal elimination constant (λz) and Tmax were
unaffected by food consumption. There was no impact on the PK of the metabolite (data
not shown).
Consistent with the study results in American subjects, UGE in Japanese subjects
increased in a dose-dependent manner after administration of 3 mg through 30 mg. The
UGE was essentially the same in the 30 and 90-mg dose groups (Table 97)

14.2.4. Mass Balance Study (Trial C-410)
Study Design
This was an open-label, radiolabeled mass balance study conducted in six healthy male subjects
to determine absorption, routes of elimination, and circulatory and excretory metabolites of
bexagliflozin. During the study, subjects received a single oral dose of 50 mg of bexagliflozin
(with approximately 100 μCi of [14C]-bexagliflozin) in an aqueous solution. Plasma and whole
blood samples were collected for at least 48 hours after dosing. Urine and feces samples were
collected for up to 9 days or to when the total excreted radioactivity levels had exceeded 90% of
the administered radioactivity, whichever occurred first.

Results
Plasma:
•

•
•

Mean Cmax and AUCinf values for total radioactivity in plasma were approximately 2-fold
higher than those for total radioactivity in whole blood, indicating that most of the total
radioactivity resides in the plasma compartment of blood and is not highly associated
with blood cells.
The t1/2 of unchanged parent drug in plasma from humans was 5.6 hours after oral dosing.
In plasma, 55% of the circulating radioactivity was associated with unchanged
bexagliflozin. EGT0002149, an O-glucuronide, was the major circulating metabolite in
plasma contributing to 32% of parent AUC and 18% of total radioactivity AUC. Other
metabolites identified in plasma were the O-glucuronide conjugates EGT0002147 and
EGT0002148; and the phase 1 metabolites EGT0001301 (M1), EGT0001494 (M2), and
EGT0001684. All other metabolites were <10% of parent AUC and total radioactivity
AUC (Table 102). A metabolism diagram is provided in Figure 25.

178

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Table 102. PK Parameters for [14C] Bexagliflozin and Metabolites in Plasma

Source: Table 11-3 of Study Report THR-1442-C-410
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; AUC00-∞, area under the curve from zero to infinity; AUC0-t, area
under the concentration-time curve from 0 to time; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; PK, pharmacokinetics; t1/2, half life; tmax,
time to maximum plasma concentration

Figure 25. Proposed Biotransformation Pathway of [14C] Bexagliflozin in Humans

Source: Figure 11-1 of Study Report THR-1442-C-410

Urine and Feces:
•
•

Total recovery of radioactivity in urine and feces combined was approximately 91.6%
over the 192-hour postdose assessment period, representing 51.1% mean recovery in
feces and 40.5% mean recovery in urine.
Most of the radioactivity excreted in urine was associated with EGT0002149, accounting
for 30.1% of the administered dose through 96 hours post dosing, of which the majority
was excreted within the first 12 hours.
179

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•
•
•
•

For minor metabolites, 1.69% EGT0001301 (M1), 3.88% EGT0001494 (M2), 1.63%
EGT0002147, and 0.45% EGT0002148 were also identified in urine.
Unchanged parent drug in urine accounted for 1.50% of the dose through 96 hours
postdose.
Most of the radioactivity in feces was associated with the unchanged parent drug,
accounting for 28.7% of the administered dose through 144 hours postdose.
In feces, EGT0001301 (M1) and EGT0001494 (M2) were the major metabolites and
accounted for 8.03 and 10.1% of the dose, respectively, through 144 hours postdose.
EGT0001684 was also identified in feces.

Conclusions
•
•
•
•
•
•
•

Most of the total radioactivity was in plasma and was not highly associated with blood
cells.
Bexagliflozin was metabolized in humans via oxidation and glucuronidation with 55.3%
of the circulating radioactivity associated with the parent drug.
EGT0002149 (glucuronide metabolite) was the major circulating metabolite in plasma.
Mean total recovery of radioactivity was 91.6%, with a mean of 51.1% of the dose
excreted in feces and 40.5% excreted in urine through 192 hours postdose.
In urine, most of the radioactivity (30.1% of the dose) was associated with EGT0002149.
In feces, most of the radioactivity (28.7% of the dose) was associated with unchanged
parent drug.
Bexagliflozin was safe and well tolerated in this group of healthy male subjects.

14.2.5. Food Effect Study (Trial C-481)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label, two-treatment, two-period crossover study designed to assess the
effects of a high-fat meal on the PK of orally administered bexagliflozin tablets in 18 healthy
adults. Subjects were randomized 1:1 to receive bexagliflozin tablets with a high-fat meal on
Day 1 and without a meal on Day 8 after an overnight fast or to receive bexagliflozin tablets
without a meal on Day 1 after an overnight fast and with a high-fat meal on Day 8. Blood and
urine samples were collected at prespecified time points up to 48 hours postdose.

Results
•
•
•

High fat, high calorie meal did not affect the extent of bexagliflozin absorption with both
AUC parameters entirely contained within the 80% to 125% margins (Table 103).
Intake of a high-fat, high-calorie meal increased bexagliflozin Cmax by approximately
30% and delayed the median Tmax by 1.5 hours (5.0 and 3.5 hours for fed and fasted
states, respectively).
Intrasubject variability was low (<30%) for the primary PK parameters AUC0-t, AUC0-∞,
and Cmax.

180

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•
•

The elimination half-life is 11.7 h or 10.1 h following dosing in the fasted or fed states,
respectively.
Administration of bexagliflozin was well tolerated under both fasted and fed conditions.

Table 103. Bexagliflozin PK Parameters Following Oral Administration Bexagliflozin Tablets,
20 mg Under Fasted Versus Fed Condition

Source: Table 11 of Study Report THR-1442-c-481
Abbreviations: ANOVA, analysis of variance; CV%, coefficient of variation; PK, pharmacokinetics.

Conclusion
No significant food effect was observed for bexagliflozin 20-mg tablet.

14.2.6. Formulation Development (Trial CLR-002)
Study Design:
This was a phase 1, open-labeled, crossover (Part 1) and parallel (Part 2) study to assess the PK
and PD of multiple oral doses of bexagliflozin sustained release (SR) tablets in healthy male
subjects.
•
•

In Part 1, a crossover design was used. Twenty eligible subjects were dosed in a
randomized sequence with each of 4 formulations of bexagliflozin in 15-mg formulations
of bexagliflozin SR tablets (SR11, SR8, and SR5) and 20 mg bexagliflozin capsule.
In Part 2, three bexagliflozin formulations (SR5 and additional formulations of 10 and
30 mg) were administered orally under fasting condition on Day 1 and 2 and 30 minutes
after a standard meal on Day 3.

Serial PK, safety, and PD evaluations were performed at prespecified times up to 48 hours
postdose.

Results:
•

Median Tmax values were 1 hour for the capsule formulation and 3 hours for all SR tablet
formulations (Figure 26). Administration of the SR formulations in the fed state resulted

181

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•
•
•

in an increase in exposure of 17% to 45% and 71% to 97%, for AUC0-∞ and Cmax,
respectively, compared to administration in the fasted state (Table 104).
SR5 formulation showed the largest systemic exposures of the SR formulations and was
selected to be studied in Part 2.
In Part 2, bexagliflozin exposures (AUC0-24 and Cmax) generally increased in a doseproportional manner over the dose range of 10 to 30 mg of the SR5 formulation (Figure
27 and Table 105).
There was no apparent difference in UGE between formulations in Part 1 (data not
shown). In Part 2 (SR5 formulation), UGE0-24 values were comparable between 10 and
15-mg dose groups, both of which were slightly lower than that from the 30-mg dose
group (Table 106).

(b) (4) excipients that promote gastric retention. In
the SR5 formulation is mediated by
Part 1 of the study, PK exposure of bexagliflozin was compared between 20 mg prolonged
release tablet formulation (SR5) and 15 mg immediate release capsule formulation in a
crossover manner. Based on the demonstrated dose proportionality of bexagliflozin in the range
of 1.67 to 100 mg, bexagliflozin exposures were normalized by the studied doses to assess the
relative bioavailability between capsule and tablet (SR5) formulations. The results showed that
the prolonged release tablet formulation (SR5) had about 16% lower AUC and 55% lower Cmax
compared to the immediate release capsule formulation.

14.2.7. Effect of Hepatic Impairment (Trial C-455)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label, parallel-group study to assess the effect of moderate hepatic
impairment on the PK and PD of orally administered bexagliflozin tablets. A total of 16 subjects
comprising 8 with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh total score 7 to 9) and 8 healthy,
matched controls, were enrolled and received a single oral dose of bexagliflozin tablets, 20 mg,
after an overnight fast. Blood samples were collected prior to dosing, and at multiple time points
for up to 48 hours postdose. The unbound fraction of bexagliflozin at 24 hours postdose and at
the maximum plasma concentration for each subject was determined by equilibrium dialysis.
Urine samples for PD analysis were collected for the 12 hour interval preceding dosing and 48
hours following dosing. Plasma concentrations of bexagliflozin were determined by a validated
LC-MS/MS assay.

Results
•
•
•
•

Bexagliflozin AUC0-∞ and Cmax were about 28 to 32% and 6 to 10% higher, respectively,
in subjects with moderate hepatic dysfunction compared to subjects with normal function.
(Table 107 and Table 108).
Plasma protein binding were minimally affected by moderate hepatic impairment.
T1/2 values were comparable between subjects with moderate hepatic impairment
(11.2±4.16 hrs) and subjects with normal hepatic function (12.3±6.56 hrs).
Urinary glucose excretion was lower in the group with reduced hepatic function, with 24
hour collection values of 58.5 and 46.8 g for subjects with normal and moderately
diminished function, respectively. The corresponding values for the creatinine
normalized urinary glucose excretion over the 24 hours postdose was 67.38 (19.0) and
74.69 (35.2) g for subjects with normal or moderately diminished function, respectively,
and were consistent with the substantially lower creatinine excretion displayed by the
impaired cohort.

185

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

•

Bexagliflozin was well tolerated, treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were mild
in severity and no subject experienced a death, serious TEAE, or TEAE leading to
withdrawal from the study.
