15.1.5. Clinical Protocol Synopsis for C-476 (Version
8.0, Date: October 28, 2016)
Sponsor: TheracosBio, LLC.
Name of Finished Product: Bexagliflozin Tablets
Name of Active Ingredient: Bexagliflozin
Study Title: A Double-Blind Placebo-Controlled Study to Evaluate the Effects of Bexagliflozin
on Hemoglobin A1c in Patients With Type 2 Diabetes and Increased Risk of Cardiovascular
Adverse Events
Study Number: THR-1442-C-476
Study Phase: 3
Primary Efficacy Objective: The primary efficacy objective of this trial is to evaluate the
placebo-adjusted change in HbA1c from baseline after 24 weeks of exposure to bexagliflozin in
type 2 diabetic subjects at high risk of cardiovascular adverse events.
Secondary Efficacy Objectives:
The key secondary efficacy objectives are:
•
•
•

To evaluate the effect of bexagliflozin compared to placebo on the change in HbA1c
from baseline to Week 24 in randomized subjects who have been prescribed insulin to
control their diabetes
To evaluate the effect of bexagliflozin on the change in body weight from baseline to
Week 48 in randomized subjects with a BMI ≥25 kg/m2 compared to placebo
To evaluate the effect of bexagliflozin on the change in SBP from baseline to Week 24 in
subjects with baseline systolic blood pressure ≥140 mm Hg compared to placebo

Additional exploratory efficacy objectives are:
•
•
•
•
•

To assess the effect of bexagliflozin treatment on the change in HbA1c versus placebo
over time
To evaluate the effect of bexagliflozin treatment on the change in FPG versus placebo
over time
To measure the proportion of subjects requiring an intensification of antidiabetic regimen
versus placebo over time
To measure the proportion of subjects requiring a relaxation of their antidiabetic regimen
versus placebo over time
To measure the incidence of hospitalization for heart failure among all subjects and
among subjects with a history of heart failure at baseline.

Safety Objectives: The primary safety objective of this study is the contribution of at least 134
MACE+ to an eventual meta-analysis that is intended to exclude a hazard ratio of 1.8 or greater
for subjects exposed to bexagliflozin compared to subjects exposed to placebo. MACE+ is
defined as cardiovascular death, nonfatal myocardial infarction (MI), nonfatal stroke, or
hospitalization for unstable angina. An additional objective is the evaluation of the safety of
exposure to bexagliflozin for a minimum of 52 weeks in a treatment population that is at elevated
risk for major adverse cardiovascular events.
238

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Other Objectives:
•
•

Measurement of bexagliflozin plasma concentration as a function of time from dosing
(sparsely sampled) will be conducted at 30 sites and will include approximately 240
subjects
Measurement of cardiovascular biomarkers at baseline and Week 12 in an exploratory
study to increase the understanding of bexagliflozin treatment effect on the biomarkers
that are relevant in the CV disease diagnosis and prognosis

Study Design: C-476 is a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallelgroup study. Approximately 1650 subjects with poorly controlled T2DM and at high risk of CV
adverse events will be randomized to bexagliflozin tablets, 20 mg, or placebo in a ratio of 2:1 in
addition to the background antidiabetic medications.
Potential participants with suboptimal glycemic control (HbA1c between 7.0% and 11%) despite
treatment as recommended in local guidelines and at high risk of cardiovascular adverse events
will enter a screening period of up to three weeks. Qualified participants will enter a single-blind,
placebo run-in period to allow for diabetes education and optimization of compliance with diet
and exercise recommendations. Qualified subjects will continue their prescreening regimen for
glycemic control and will be instructed to take the run-in medication once daily for 13±2 days.
Adjustment of treatment for hypertension or dyslipidemia will not be permitted during the
screening and run-in periods although initiation of anticoagulant therapy (if clinically indicated)
will be permitted. If a change in treatment is required to improve management of hypertension or
dyslipidemia, the subject may re-enter the screening after the clinical condition and treatment
regimen have not changed for at least four weeks. Subjects who continue to qualify for the study
and who have demonstrated compliance by missing no more than one dose of the run-in doses as
instructed will be randomized to receive the investigational product.
Assignment to treatment groups will be stratified by baseline HbA1c (> or ≤9.5%), baseline
eGFR (eGFR ≥ or <60 mL/min/1.73 m2), baseline BMI (≥ or <25 kg/m2), and history of heart
failure. The study subjects will visit the clinic or complete phone interviews for evaluation at 2,
6, 12, 18, 24, 36, and 48 weeks postrandomization with subsequent visits scheduled every 24
weeks and a phone interview at 12 weeks after each clinic visit. The study duration will be
determined by event accumulation. The treatment period will end when all randomized subjects
have completed at least 52 weeks of treatment and a total of 134 subjects have experienced a
MACE+. A follow-up visit will take place four weeks after the conclusion of treatment.
During the first 24 weeks of the efficacy assessment period, hyperglycemia should be managed
with diet and exercise counseling. Changes in the medical therapy are allowed if the investigator
feels it is necessary for the well-being of the subject. After 24 weeks, changes to the medical
therapy for hyperglycemia should occur at the discretion of the investigator based on SMBG,
FPG, and HbA1c information using local standards of care for subjects with T2DM. During the
course of the study investigators will adjust other medications to affect changes in blood pressure
and serum lipid values according to national or local guidelines. At every visit participants will
be queried regarding adverse events and information on all events that potentially represent a
MACE endpoint will be forwarded to a cardiovascular endpoint committee (CEC) for blinded
adjudication. An investigator should also query the subject about symptoms that may be
associated with possible DKA at every visit. A DKA adjudication committee will review and
adjudicate all suspected DKA cases.
239

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

In this study, the incidence of MACE+ will be recorded for subsequent meta-analysis. MACE+
is defined as CV death, nonfatal MI, nonfatal stroke, or hospitalization for unstable angina. It is
estimated that the required total of 134 patients with a MACE+ will accrue over a treatment
period of 2 to 3 years. During the trial, a DSMB will review the unblinded data periodically and
may recommend an early termination of the trial for safety reasons. No interim analysis will be
performed for efficacy assessment and the study will not be stopped for futility or benefit of
bexagliflozin treatment.
Measurement of bexagliflozin plasma concentration as a function of time (sparsely sampled) will
also be conducted at 30 centers and will include approximately 240 study subjects as part of a
bexagliflozin population PK study. Samples will be drawn at Weeks 6 or 12. Cardiovascular
biomarkers will be measured at baseline before treatment is administered and after 12 weeks of
treatment in an exploratory study to evaluate the effect of bexagliflozin treatment on biomarkers
that are relevant in cardiovascular disease diagnosis and prognosis.
Study Population: The study population will consist of approximately 1650 subjects who have
been diagnosed with suboptimally controlled T2DM and who exhibit an elevated risk of
cardiovascular events. The study population will comprise:
(1) Male or female adult subjects (age ≥40 years)
(2) Subjects with a diagnosis of T2DM
(3) Subjects with HbA1c values of 7.0 to 11%, inclusive
(4) Subjects with FPG ≤300 mg/dL at screening
(5) Subjects who have a regimen for treatment of T2DM that has been stable for the past 3
months. A stable regimen is defined as: no changes in dose or frequency of oral
hypoglycemic agents or GLP-1 agonists, or <20% variability in total daily insulin dose.
(6) Subjects with either established cardiovascular disease or with multiple cardiovascular
risk factors (if no documented cardiovascular disease) having the following attributes:
a. Group 1: A history of atherosclerotic vascular disease as defined by one or more
of the following: a) MI or ischemic (nonhemorrhagic) stroke >3 months but ≤5
years prior to screening or b) documented history of coronary, carotid, or
peripheral arterial revascularization (coronary artery bypass grafting must have
occurred ≥5 years prior to screening)
b. Group 2: A history of New York Heart Association (NYHA) class II or class III
heart failure (Appendix 4) at the time of screening. A history of heart failure is
defined as:
i. documented left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) ≤40% and no
subsequent LVEF >40% within 6 months of screening, or
ii. an N-terminal probrain natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP) >300 pg/mL and
no evidence of atrial fibrillation/flutter (AF) at the time of the screening
ECG or an NT-proBNP >900 pg/mL and evidence of AF at the time of the
screening ECG and (ii) either (a) structural heart disease documented by
report of left atrial enlargement or left ventricular hypertrophy, or (b)
exhibiting symptom(s) of HF requiring treatment with diuretic(s) for at
least 30 days prior to screening

240

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

c. Group 3: Age ≥55 years with 2 or more of the following:
i. diabetes duration of ≥10 years,
ii. uncontrolled hypertension defined as SBP >140 mm Hg despite 3 or more
antihypertensive medications,
iii. current smoking,
iv. UACR >30 mg/g,
v. eGFR of 45 to 60 mL/min/1.73 m2, or
vi. high-density lipoprotein <1 mmol/L (38 mg/dL)
To ensure that subjects with various cardiovascular risks are adequately represented, a minimum
of 352 subjects (21%) from each of the three groups will be recruited and ≤400 subjects who
have a history of heart failure will be randomized in the study. The intended population is
presented in Figure 46 below.

Statistical Methods: The primary endpoint is the difference in the change in HbA1c from Week
0 (randomization visit) to Week 24 among subjects exposed to bexagliflozin, 20 mg, compared
to subjects exposed to placebo. Statistical significance will be declared at a two-sided 0.05 level
of significance. The ITT population that includes all randomized subjects regardless of treatment
adherence or availability of follow-up data will be analyzed. An MMRM ANCOVA)with
baseline HbA1c as a covariate will be performed on the available data, incorporating all visits
from each patient at which HbA1c was measured.
Three key secondary endpoints are:
(1) Change in HbA1c from baseline to Week 24 in randomized subjects who have been
prescribed insulin to control their diabetes
(2) Change in body weight from baseline to Week 48 in subjects with a BMI ≥25 kg/m2
(3) Change in SBP from baseline to Week 24 in subjects with baseline systolic blood
pressure ≥140 mm Hg
A hierarchical testing strategy in the following order will be undertaken, if the null hypothesis is
excluded for the primary efficacy endpoint:
(1) Effect of bexagliflozin on the change in HbA1c from baseline to Week 24 in subjects
who have been prescribed insulin to control diabetes at baseline, using MMRM
ANCOVA adjusting for baseline HbA1c at a two-sided 0.05 level of significance; if a
significant bexagliflozin effect is found, then:
(2) Comparison of treatments on the change in body weight at Week 48 in subjects with
baseline BMI ≥25 kg/m2, using MMRM ANCOVA adjusting for baseline body weight. at
a two-sided 0.05 level of significance; if a significant bexagliflozin effect is found, then:
(3) Comparison of treatments on the change in SBP at Week 24 in subjects with baseline
systolic blood pressure ≥140 mm Hg, using MMRM ANCOVA, adjusting for baseline
SBP, at a two-sided 0.05 level of significance.
Additional effects of bexagliflozin on FPG, changes in HbA1c over time, changes in body
weight over time, frequency of hospitalization for heart failure (in all subjects and in subjects
with a history of heart failure), and general safety of bexagliflozin in subjects with T2DM will be
analyzed as exploratory endpoint and will not be adjusted for multiplicity.
The sample size of 1650 patients is estimated based on the required number of events of MACE+
for the program-wide meta-analysis to assess bexagliflozin effect on cardiovascular safety and
provides adequate power to compare treatments on the primary endpoint of change in HbA1c
from baseline to 24 weeks.
Statistical analyses and summaries will be performed using SAS software (SAS Institute, Cary,
NC).

FDA Reviewer Comment: This trial was designed to assess the safety and effectiveness of
bexagliflozin for improved glycemic control in subjects with increased risk for MACE as
identified by the following three groups: 1) subjects with documented atherosclerotic CV
disease, 2) subjects with congestive heart failure, or 3) subjects exhibiting multiple CV risks for
atherosclerotic vascular disease. In addition, this trial was designed to accrue MACE events for
the planned MACE meta-analysis.
The protocol for this study was submitted to the Agency and feedback on the study design and
analysis plan was issued on July 30, 2015, and January 8, 2016 (see communications under IND
103822 in DARRTS). The Applicant appears to have incorporated FDA feedback into their study
protocol and analysis plan. Protocol Version 5, dated September 8, 2015, was the first version
under which subjects were consented. The protocol was amended to implement the following
changes:
•

Protocol Version 6, dated February 2, 2016, implemented DKA surveillance and
documentation procedures as a result of FDA communications regarding rare but
potentially serious ketoacidosis events occurring in the setting of SGLT2 inhibitor
treatment.

•

Protocol Version 7, dated October 12, 2016, contained several changes to enrollment
criteria and study design (discussed further in the next bullet point). However, no
subjects were consented under this protocol version as additional revisions were needed
to communicate emerging findings related to potential increased risk of SGLT2 inhibitor
treatment with lower limb amputation.

•

Protocol Version 8, dated October 28, 2016, was the final version for the study. It
contained the following modifications from Version 6:
o Based on preliminary data indicating that approximately half of the subjects
enrolled in the study were prescribed insulin at baseline, the Applicant proposed
to analyze the effects of bexagliflozin on HbA1c and other endpoints in the
corresponding subpopulations instead of conducting dedicated clinical studies
evaluating cohorts recruited for these characteristics. This proposal was
submitted to FDA. The Division issued feedback on September 21, 2016, noting
that this approach is reasonable provided that the design and conduct of the
glycemic efficacy portion of the trial is consistent with what would be expected if
the trial was conducted as a separate study. The protocol was amended to include
these analyses in the subpopulations.

NDA 214373

o As over 50% of the consented subjects exhibited HbA1c values lower than the
threshold of ≥7.5% at screening, the HbA1c inclusion criterion was widened to be
between 7.0% and 11%. The eligibility criteria were similarly relaxed to allow
entry of subjects with fasting plasma glucose concentrations ≤300 mg/dL as
opposed to ≤250 mg/dL. The Division issued feedback on September 21, 2016,
stating that this protocol update was acceptable.
o The initial study design anticipated that approximately 25% of the participants
would exhibit heart failure with reduced LVEF (<40%). The recruitment of
subjects with existing NYHA class II or III heart failure who had an LVEF <40%
was substantially slower than the recruitment of subjects without these

Regarding study conduct, each center was visited at regular intervals by a monitor to ensure
compliance with the study protocol, GCP guidelines, and applicable laws and regulations. This
included inspections of the CRFs for completeness and clarity, verification with source
documents, and clarification of administrative matters. Fourteen investigational sites, located in
Canada, Czech Republic, Republic of Korea, Mexico, the Netherlands, Poland, Russian
Federation, Taiwan, and the United States were audited during the trial. One critical finding
regarding missing documentation was observed at a site located in Russia enrolling a small
number of subjects (11 randomized subjects). This finding is unlikely to impact study results.
Audit certificates were submitted with the NDA. Protocol deviations were identified and
documented, and a list of all protocol deviations was provided. Review of the major protocol
deviations did not reveal any obvious trends or treatment differences across treatment groups.
Overall, the trial appears appropriately designed and conducted to meaningfully inform
glycemic efficacy in the studied subject population.

Study Title: A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-Blind, Active-Controlled Study to Evaluate the
Effects of Bexagliflozin versus Glimepiride in Subjects with Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus Who
Have Inadequate Glycemic Control by Metformin
Study Number: THR-1442-C-480
Study Phase: 3
Primary Objective: The primary efficacy objective is to demonstrate that bexagliflozin is
noninferior to glimepiride by evaluating the treatment effect on HbA1c reduction at Week 60 in
subjects whose T2DM is inadequately controlled by metformin.
Secondary Objectives: The key secondary objectives are:
•
•

body weight in subjects with baseline BMI ≥25 kg/m2 at Week 60
To evaluate the treatment effect of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride on the change in SBP
in subjects with baseline SBP ≥140 mm Hg at Week 60
To evaluate the treatment effect of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride on the difference in
proportion of subjects with ≥1 severe or documented symptomatic hypoglycemia events
over 96 weeks
To evaluate if bexagliflozin is superior to glimepiride on HbA1c reduction at Week 60

Exploratory Objectives:
•

To assess the treatment effect of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride on the change in the
proportion of subjects achieving HbA1c <7.0% over time

•

•

To compare the effects of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride on the incidence of adverse

hypovolemia, hypotension episodes, hypoglycemia, hepatotoxicity, MACE, falls and
fractures, malignancies, hypersensitivity reactions, acid-base disorders including diabetic

use of concomitant medications

To compare the effects of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride on health-related quality of
life using a validated instrument EQ-5D-3L.

Study Design: C-480 is a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, parallel-group study
to evaluate the efficacy and safety of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride in subjects with T2DM is
inadequately controlled by metformin. A total of 420 subjects are planned to be randomized in a
ratio of 1:1 to receive once daily treatment of active bexagliflozin with placebo glimepiride
(Group 1) or placebo bexagliflozin with active glimepiride (Group 2) for 96 weeks in an
outpatient setting. Active treatment in Group 1 will be bexagliflozin tablets, 20 mg. Active
treatment in Group 2 will be glimepiride capsules, 2, 4, or 6 mg. At the time of screening,
subjects will be taking metformin only (metformin-only) or metformin in addition to no more
than one additional oral hypoglycemic agent (metformin-and-OHA). Study subjects will
continue receiving open-labeled metformin background medication during the entire study.
The main eligibility criteria include 1) male or female subjects with T2DM and 2) screening
HbA1c between 7.0% and 10.5% (inclusive) for subjects who are metformin-only treated, or
screening HbA1c between 6.5% and 10.0% (inclusive) for subjects who are metformin-andOHA treated. All eligible subjects must have taken metformin at a stable dose of ≥1500 mg/day
for at least 8 weeks prior to screening. Subjects who are metformin-and-OHA treated must be
willing to undergo a 6-week wash-out of the non-metformin OHA prior to the start of a 2-week
run-in period. During the wash-out, subjects will discontinue non-metformin OHA and continue
taking metformin at the dose and frequency taken at screening.
247

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

All eligible subjects who are metformin-only treated or have completed the 6-week wash-out
period will start a 2-week single-blind run-in period. During the run-in, subjects will receive
placebo bexagliflozin and placebo glimepiride. Subjects who meet all inclusion criteria and no
exclusion criteria prior to randomization will be eligible for randomization and receive
investigational products.
During the treatment period, glimepiride will be provided in three dose levels: 2 mg, 4 mg, and
6 mg. Placebo glimepiride will also be provided in three dose levels: placebo for 2 mg, placebo
for 4 mg, and placebo for 6 mg. Subjects will receive the starting dose of glimepiride, 2 mg or
placebo. Subjects who meet protocol-specified glycemic criteria will undergo up-titration of the
glimepiride dose at Weeks 2, 4, and 6. The dose will be increased to the next dosing level if
during the preceding 2 weeks: (1) ≥50% of fasting SMBG measurements ˃110 mg/dL and (2) no
severe or documented symptomatic hypoglycemia events. Subjects assigned to receive placebo
glimepiride, will receive mock titrations of glimepiride at Weeks 2, 4, and 6 based on the same
glycemic criteria. No dose changes of glimepiride will occur after 6 weeks of treatment.
Each subject will be instructed to return to the clinic at Weeks 0, 2, 4, 6, 12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 72,
84 (off-site phone visit), and 96 for efficacy assessment and safety monitoring. Subjects will
return to the clinic for a follow-up exit visit at Week 98 or 2 weeks after last dose of study drug
if subjects early terminate prior to Week 96.
During the study, a DSMB will review the unblinded data periodically and may recommend
early termination of the trial for safety reasons. In the event of administrative changes or other
related reasons, the Sponsor reserves the rights to terminate the study early. There will be no
interim analysis; final statistical analysis will be performed after all subjects complete the study.
Study Population and Main Eligibility Criteria: A total of 420 eligible subjects are planned
for this study. The study population will comprise:

pregnancy test and agree to abstain from coitus or use contraception during the entire
study to avoid any possible pregnancy

a. Metformin-only treated: receive ≥1500 mg/day metformin only for at least 8
weeks prior to screening or
b. Metformin-and-OHA treated: receive ≥1500 mg/day metformin for at least 8
weeks prior to screening and ≥14 days of 1 additional OHA in the 8 weeks prior
to screening.
(4) Subjects with a diagnosis of T2DM with HbA1c levels between 7.0% and 10.5%
(inclusive) at screening if metformin-only treated or with HbA1c levels between 6.5%
and 10.0% (inclusive) at screening if metformin- and-OHA treated
(5) Subjects with a BMI ≤45 kg/m2 at screening
(6) If applicable, taking stable doses of treatment for dyslipidemia and/or hypertension for 30
days prior to screening. Subjects do not need to be treated for dyslipidemia or
hypertension to be eligible for the study
(7) Subjects who are willing and able to return for all clinic visits and to complete all studyrequired procedures, including SMBG measurements

Statistical Methods: The primary hypothesis is that bexagliflozin is noninferior to glimepiride
by evaluating the treatment effect on HbA1c reduction at Week 60 in combination with
metformin. The noninferiority margin for mean change from baseline at Week 60 in HbA1c
comparing bexagliflozin group with glimepiride group will be 0.35%. Superiority will be
declared without additional statistical testing when the upper bound of the 95% CI for difference
in change from baseline at Week 60 in HbA1c comparing bexagliflozin group with glimepiride
group is less than 0. The treatment difference will be examined by using an MMRM ANCOVA.
The model will include treatment, visit, treatment-by-visit interaction, and randomization
stratification factors as the fixed effects terms and the baseline HbA1c value as a fixed effect
covariate. Least squares mean treatment differences between the bexagliflozin group and the
comparator group at Week 60 in change from baseline will be estimated from the model. An
unstructured covariance will be used to model the within-subject correlation. If the model with
the unstructured covariance structure does not converge, an autoregressive (1) covariance
structure will be used. HbA1c values obtained after the start of rescue medication will not be
excluded from this primary analysis. Primary endpoint will be analyzed on both ITT and PP
analysis sets.
The MMRM model is one approach to obtain treatment effect estimates in the presence of
missing data. As sensitivity analyses for missing data, the following will be performed on both
ITT and PP analysis sets:
(1) Missing data will be imputed via multiple imputations, following which the MMRM will
be repeated on the complete datasets with results combined across complete datasets
using standard multiple imputation techniques; HbA1c values collected after the start of
rescue medication will not be excluded.
(2) Missing data will be imputed via LOCF, following which the MMRM will be repeated;
HbA1c values collected after the start of rescue medication will be considered missing.
(3) HbA1c values collected after the start of rescue medication will be considered missing,
and the MMRM analyses will be reperformed.
The effects of bexagliflozin on the changes from baseline at Week 60 in body weight and
systolic blood pressure, the difference in the proportion of subjects with ≥1 severe or
documented symptomatic hypoglycemia events over 96 weeks and the change in HbA1c from
baseline at Week 60 will be tested as key secondary endpoints and will be analyzed in a
hierarchical testing strategy and only if the primary objective is met. Similar to the primary
endpoint, ANCOVA models will be used for the continuous secondary endpoints and logistic
regression model will be used for the binary endpoint. A hierarchical testing strategy will be
followed to preserve experiment-wide alpha at 0.05:
(1) Superiority test of the change in body weight in subjects with baseline BMI ≥25 kg/m2 in
the bexagliflozin group versus the glimepiride group at Week 60
(2) Superiority test of the change in SBP in subjects with baseline SBP ≥140 mm Hg in the
bexagliflozin group versus the glimepiride group at Week 60
(3) Superiority test of the difference in proportion of subjects with ≥1 severe or documented
symptomatic hypoglycemia event in the bexagliflozin group versus the glimepiride group
over 96 weeks
250

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Additional effects of bexagliflozin versus glimepiride on the changes in HbA1c, FPG, SBP, body
weight, the proportion of subjects who reach an HbA1c <7%, and the proportion of subjects with
≥1 severe or documented symptomatic hypoglycemia events over time, as well as the general
safety in subjects with T2DM, will be analyzed as exploratory efficacy endpoints. These
endpoints are considered exploratory and will not be adjusted for multiplicity.
For the primary efficacy endpoint, a minimum of 172 subjects are needed in each treatment arm
to detect noninferiority of bexagliflozin to glimepiride with respect to the change in HbA1c from
baseline at Week 60 by a margin of 0.35%, assuming an estimated pooled standard deviation of
1.0%. This estimation is based on a one-sided t-test with 90% power at a 0.025 level of
significance. To allow for an estimated 18.0% dropout rate, 210 patients will be randomized in
each treatment arm, and the total sample size will be 420 subjects for this study.
Statistical analyses and summaries will be performed using SAS software (SAS Institute, Cary,
NC).
FDA Reviewer Comment: This trial aimed to demonstrate that bexagliflozin is noninferior to the
sulfonylurea glimepiride as add-on therapy in subjects with T2DM not adequately controlled by
metformin treatment. Sulfonylureas may be prescribed with metformin as a combination therapy
for treating T2DM. As such, clinical programs for other SGLT2 inhibitors have evaluated
noninferiority of the candidate drug to glimepiride. Glimepiride is an appropriate comparator
for the evaluation of noninferiority of bexagliflozin to another oral agent prescribed for the
treatment of T2DM.
The trial enrolled subjects with T2DM taking stable doses of metformin monotherapy, or subjects
taking metformin and one other antihyperglycemic agent willing to undergo a 6-week washout
period. The trial also included a two-week run-in period, such that subjects requiring washout
were on metformin monotherapy for a minimum of 8 weeks prior to randomization. In addition,
randomization was stratified by background treatment status (metformin only versus metformin
plus an additional oral agent), HbA1c (≤8.5% versus >8.5%), and eGFR (≥90 mL/min/1.73 m2
versus <90 mL/min/1.73 m2). These procedures appear reasonable to mitigate the possibility
that oral antihyperglycemic agents other than metformin taken prior to study enrollment
impacted study outcomes.
Sulfonylureas are known to increase the risk of hypoglycemia. To control hyperglycemia and
minimize hypoglycemia, subjects were initiated on glimepiride (or placebo glimepiride) at 2 mg
and underwent dose up-titration based on protocol-specified criteria during the first 6 weeks of
the study. Although the maximum approved dose in the US is 8 mg, titration of glimepiride only
occurred up to 6 mg, potentially due to differences among countries in maximum approved doses
in this global trial. The procedures for glimepiride titration in this trial appear generally
reasonable. Similar to active controlled Trial C-423, the NI margin was larger than what is has
been used in other SGLT2 inhibitor development programs (i.e., 0.35% as opposed to 0.3%). At
the IND stage, FDA reviewed agreed with the margin selection and accepted the Applicant’s
justification for the proposed margin.
Minor changes occurred from Protocol Version 1 to Version 3, including the addition of
amputation and revascularization procedures as AESIs. Protocol deviations were captured
during clinical monitoring visits and recorded in the CRF. Review of the above major protocol
deviations did not reveal any obvious/important trends or treatment differences across treatment
groups.

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

16. Efficacy: Additional Information and
Assessment
16.1. Bexagliflozin for T2DM Clinical Program
The Applicant, Theracos, submitted eight efficacy studies for supporting the efficacy of
bexagliflozin as an adjunct to diet and exercise to improve glycemic control in adults with type 2
diabetes mellitus. The bexagliflozin clinical program was designed in accordance with the
recommendations set forth in FDA’s draft guidance for industry Diabetes Mellitus: Developing
Drugs and Therapeutic Biologics for Treatment and Prevention (March 2008). The guidance
states, “Efficacy and safety of new products for the treatment of type 2 diabetes can be evaluated
in placebo-controlled monotherapy trials, placebo-controlled add-on therapy trials, and activecontrolled trials.”
Based on consultation with the medical team, the statistical efficacy review will be focused on
the six phase 3 HbA1c studies: Trial C-450, a placebo-controlled monotherapy study in T2DM
patients who are treatment-naïve or previously treated with one OHA; Trial C-419, a placebocontrolled add-on to metformin study in T2DM patients who are not adequately controlled by
metformin alone; Trials C-423 and C-480, two active-controlled add-on to metformin studies in
T2DM patients who have inadequate glycemic control by metformin with sitagliptin (C-423) or
glimepiride (C-480) as an active control; Trial C-448, a placebo-controlled study in T2DM
patients with moderate renal impairment (eGFR 30 to 60 mL/min/1.73 m2); and Trial C-476, a
placebo-controlled study in T2DM patients with increased risk of cardiovascular adverse events.
Based on consultation with the medical team, results from Trials C-450 and C-419 serve as the
key basis of the benefit evaluation.

16.2. Trial Design Overview
See Trial Design Overview in Section 6.2.1.

16.3. Statistical Analysis Plan Overview
16.3.1. Primary Efficacy Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint in all the six studies was change from baseline in HbA1c. Except
for the glimepiride-controlled study, which used a 60-week endpoint, the primary endpoint was
evaluated at 24 weeks. The primary endpoint point selection was also in accordance with the
above-mentioned FDA 2008 Draft Guidance. The draft guidance states “For purposes of drug
approval and labeling, final demonstration of efficacy should be based on reduction in HbA1c
(i.e., HbA1c is the primary endpoint of choice, albeit a surrogate), which will support an
indication of glycemic control”(March 2008).

252

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

16.3.2. Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
The key secondary efficacy endpoints (mainly, change from baseline in weight, FPG, and SBP)
varied little across the six trials. However, in each trial the endpoints were ranked in different
orders (see Table 136), as each order was reflected in the corresponding study specific
prespecified sequential testing procedures for multiplicity control. The study designs were
similar, but the study populations varied across trials; neither the individual study protocols nor
the NDA Clinical Overview offers reasons why the secondary endpoints should differ across
trials and be prioritized in different orders. Hence, in this review, the same set of secondary
efficacy endpoints were evaluated across trials when data were available.
Of note, in C-419, no formal hypothesis testing was planned for the secondary efficacy
endpoints.
In addition, the Applicant’s statistical analysis plan (SAP) prespecified that efficacy in terms of
weight and SBP were to be evaluated in subgroups of subjects:
•
•

In all six studies, efficacy on change from baseline in weight was to be evaluated in the
subgroup of subjects with baseline BMI ≥25 kg/m2
In C-448, efficacy on change from baseline in SBP was to be evaluated in the subgroup
of subjects with baseline SBP ≥130 mm Hg; and, in Trials C-476 and C-480, in the
subgroup of subjects with baseline SBP ≥140 mm Hg.

Of note, in C-476, the Applicant added HbA1c changes from baseline to Week 24 in subjects
who have been prescribed sulfonylurea as an exploratory endpoint in the late stage of the trial,
one day before the database lock (see Section 12). The Applicant proposed to include the results
for this exploratory endpoint in labeling.
16.3.2.1. Labeling Considerations on the Key Secondary
Efficacy Endpoints
From an individual study perspective, at the judgement of the review team, when the results for a
specific key secondary endpoint are statistically significant based on the prespecified sequential
testing procedure and also deemed clinically meaningful, these data could be included in the
labeling Clinical Studies section as it could be used to support distinct claims of effectiveness.
Since multiple studies are included in the bexagliflozin for T2DM development program, some
key secondary endpoints did not meet the criteria for statistical significance in all studies. In such
cases, the review team should determine whether the totality of evidence supports inclusion of
the endpoint in labeling. For body weight and SBP endpoints that were prespecified in the
subset, the results should be indicated clearly with the number of subjects used in the analysis
and described in the text instead of a table format in the Clinical Studies section of the labeling.
16.3.2.2. Other Secondary Efficacy Endpoint: Proportion of
Subject Reaching HbA1c <7% at Week 24/60
The primary efficacy endpoint of change from baseline in HbA1c (%) was established by
rigorous statistical methods. However, the binary measure of HbA1c (%) levels, proportion of
subjects reaching <7%, is an important endpoint for understanding the treatment effects in
253

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

HbA1c (%) and a measure useful to prescribers and patients. Since the binary version HbA1c
endpoint was not included in any of study-specific sequential multiplicity testing procedures for
multiplicity protection, a descriptive statistic, the responder proportional information, was
considered for inclusion in the labeling. The statistical reviewer has also verified the Applicant’s
analysis results for this endpoint.

16.3.3. Analysis Set
The screened analysis set included all subjects screened for eligibility prior to randomization.
The screened population will include screen failures. This population was used for subject
listings and for summaries of subject disposition.
All subjects who were randomized and had taken at least one dose of double-blind study
medication were included in the safety analysis set. Safety analyses were based on the first
medication kit that was dispensed to a subject. The safety analysis set is the primary analysis set
for safety evaluation.
All subjects who were randomized were included in the ITT analysis set. All analyses of the ITT
analysis set were based on the randomization schedule. The ITT analysis set was preplanned to
serve as the primary set for the efficacy analyses.

16.3.4. Analysis Method
16.3.4.1. Primary Analysis Method – Regulatory Interaction
History
During the IND stage, the Applicant first proposed an LOCF analysis, and then an MMRM
method for the primary analysis of the primary or secondary endpoints with missing data. We
advised against the LOCF first, and then advised that the MMRM approach likely does not
appropriately address missing data, as it treats the behavior of missing data for those subjects
who are off-treatment to be the same as that of observed data for those subjects who are ontreatment in the same treatment group. We clarified that the preferred estimand is the treatment
policy (de facto) estimand. To estimate the treatment policy estimand, efficacy data should be
collected and utilized in analyses on all randomized treated subjects regardless of treatment
adherence, rescue status, or other post baseline status (e.g., post bariatric surgery status). Missing
data should be imputed in a fashion consistent with what the measurement would have been, had
it been measured.
Disagreeing with the proposed MMRM approach in the Applicant’s preNDA meeting package
(meeting date December 5, 2019), we recommended that where there are a sufficient number of
retrieved dropouts (subjects who discontinued treatment before the final assessment window but
still had an efficacy measurement in the final assessment window), we prefers a multiple
imputation approach that models the missing final assessment of nonretrieved dropouts (subjects
who discontinued treatment before the final assessment window and did not have a measurement
in this assessment window) based on known measurements from the retrieved dropouts. We
suggested that, for the nonretrieved dropouts, the Sponsor should multiply impute the change
from the last measurement before treatment discontinuation to the final assessment, using this
254

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

information from the retrieved dropouts. Where there are not enough retrieved dropouts to do a
retrieved dropout analysis, we recommended a return-to-baseline analysis. In this analysis a
subject’s final assessment is multiply imputed based on the subject’s baseline value. Variance for
the multiple imputation can be based on residual variance from an ANCOVA model, using the
nonmissing final assessments as the response variable. Henceforth, we refer to this approach as
the return-to-baseline coupled ANCOVA.
In the Applicant’s final SAP, the primary efficacy endpoint was to be tested based on the ITT
analysis set using all observed data and the MMRM approach. The MMRM model included
terms for stratification factors as a fixed effect covariate. HbAlc values obtained after the start of
rescue medication were not excluded from the analysis.

the preplanned subgroup analyses of the primary endpoint. FDA requested these analyses via
information requests during the NDA review. These results have been validated and are
presented in this review.

can be located in Section 12 Table 54.
16.3.4.2. Primary Analysis Method – A Return-to-Baseline
Coupled ANCOVA
As lack of enough retrieved dropouts rendered the pattern mixture multiple imputation approach
analysis not feasible, we used the three-step return-to-baseline coupled ANCOVA in this NDA
review.

ANCOVA Model Analysis
Then, for continuous endpoints, for each iteration of the multiply imputed full dataset, the
primary and secondary endpoints were analyzed using an ANCOVA model with treatment and
stratification factors as fixed effects and the corresponding baseline value as a covariate. Based
on this model, the least squares (LS) mean differences between bexagliflozin 20 mg and control,
and the associated two-sided 95% CIs were estimated.
For the categorical secondary efficacy endpoint (proportion of subjects reaching HbA1c <7.0%),
the imputation results from the corresponding HbA1c (%) values were used to derive the
responder status. For descriptive analysis, the Applicant chose to present the crude by arm
proportion of subjects reaching HbA1c <7.0%. We also carried out a logistic model analysis with
treatment and stratification factors as fixed effects and baseline HbA1c as a covariate to estimate
the by arm responder rates.

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Rubin’s Rule to Combine the Results
Lastly, Rubin’s rule was applied to combine the analysis results from the multiply imputed
datasets for both the continuous endpoints and the binary HbA1c responder proportion endpoint.
16.3.4.3. Primary Endpoint – Subgroup Analysis Methods
16.3.4.3.1. Sample Estimates of Subgroup Treatment
Effects
Following the primary analysis method change that was finalized during our NDA review, the
analysis method for the SAP preplanned subgroup analyses of the primary endpoint was also
updated. For each subgrouping factor level, an LS mean difference versus placebo at Week 24/60
was assessed using the same return-to-baseline coupled ANCOVA model as described for the
primary endpoint. When carrying out subgroup analyses, the corresponding subgroup factor was
removed from the primary analysis model. Forest plots were used to display subgroup level LS
mean differences together with the associated 95% CI.
When a subgroup analysis interaction test was necessary, we took a three-step approach in
deriving the interaction test p-value. First, for each imputation, the difference (double-delta)
between the two subgroup-level placebo-adjusted treatment effects was derived by running a full
analysis model including the interaction term; second, we used Rubin’s Rule to summarize the
double-deltas across iterations; lastly, we output the t-test p-value associated with the iteration
mean double-delta as the treatment by subgroup interaction test p-value.
16.3.4.3.2. “Shrinkage” Estimates of Subgroup Treatment
Effects
There were likely some random highs and random lows in sample estimates of subgroup
treatment effects due to small sample size and large variability for some subgroups. Therefore,
we also derived shrinkage estimates of subgroup treatment effects using a Bayesian hierarchical
model based on summary sample estimates. The total variability in the sample estimates is the
sum of the within subgroup variability of the sample estimator and the across subgroups
variability in underlying/true parameter values. A shrinkage estimate of the subgroup treatment
effect, which borrows information from the other subgroups while estimating the treatment effect
for a specific subgroup, is a “weighted” average of the sample estimate and overall estimate. The
weights are based on the ratio of the between subgroup variability to the within subgroup
variability. The greater the ratio the smaller the weight on the overall estimate (the less the
shrinkage). We used the same flat prior to derive shrinkage estimates for all subgroups. The

257

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Bayesian hierarchical model assumptions for change in HbA1c (%) from baseline at Week 24/60
are:
For i =1, 2… Yi represents the observed sample estimate of treatment effect in a subgroup level i,
assume 𝑌𝑖 ~ 𝑁(𝜇𝑖 , 𝜎𝑖2 ) where
•
•
•

𝜎𝑖2 are the observed variance for sample estimates
𝜇𝑖 ~ 𝑁(𝜇, 𝜏 2 )
𝜇 ~ 𝑁(0, 9), 1/𝜏 2 ~𝐺𝑎𝑚𝑚𝑎 (0.001, 0.001)

A flat prior with mean 0 and variance of 9 was chosen. The results of the sample estimates and
the shrinkage estimates of treatment effects in the same subgroups, are presented in Forest plots
respectively. Some of the sample subgroup analyses are indicative of certain subgroups having
no treatment benefit. However, after shrinkage, the subgroup treatment effects are, in general,
consistent across subgroups and with the overall treatment effect.
16.3.4.4. Exploration of Potential Modulation Effect of
Baseline HbA1c Levels
To investigate the potential modulation effect of baseline HbA1c levels, aside from the
Applicant’s preplanned subgroup analysis on the dichotomized baseline HbA1c levels (<=8.5%,
or >8.5%), an additional “subgroup” analysis was carried out for the four placebo-controlled
studies by treating baseline HbA1c as a continuous variable. For each of the four studies, a
simple linear regression model based on the completers data was used for this exploratory
analysis. The response variable was still change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24, the
predictor variables include treatment, baseline HbA1c levels, and a treatment by baseline HbA1c
levels interaction. The size and significance level of the interaction term (difference in by-arm
baseline HbA1c slopes) from this model fitting is an indicator of the potential modulation effect.
16.3.4.5. Primary Endpoint – Tipping Point Sensitivity
Analysis Method
16.3.4.5.1. Placebo-Controlled Studies
To conduct analyses that systematically and comprehensively explore the space of plausible
missing data assumptions, at the IND stage, we recommended the inclusion of tipping point
analyses that vary assumptions about the missing outcomes on the two treatment arms. The
tipping point analyses should target the de facto estimand and therefore include all observed data
regardless of treatment adherence. The analyses should be two-dimensional, i.e., should allow
assumptions about the missing outcomes on the two arms to vary independently, and should
include scenarios where dropouts on bexagliflozin have worse outcomes than dropouts on
control. The goal is to explore the plausibility of missing data assumptions under which the
conclusions change, i.e., under which there is no longer evidence of a treatment effect.
The Applicant’s preplanned tipping point sensitivity analysis method consists of three steps:
(1) Nonmonotone (intermediate visits) missing data were to be imputed first using the Monte
Carlo Markov Chain (MCMC) method under the missing at random (MAR) assumption
258

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

in all treatment arms (using the MCMC statement in PROC MI). Multiple chains option
(CHAIN = MULTIPLE option in the MCMC statement of PROC MI) was to be used.
For the nonmonotone imputation of the HbA1c missing data, a multivariate normal
model was to be used including variables for the HbA1c at baseline and all postbaseline
visits within each treatment group. The Applicant preplanned to generate five hundred
imputed datasets.
(2) After the nonmonotone missing data have been imputed, the remaining monotone
missing data were to be imputed within each treatment arm and subgroup, using a
regression approach. The predictors for the regression imputation model at any time point
were the stratification factors, and HbA1c at all previous time points, including baseline.
Imputations were performed using a sequence of regression-based imputations (using
PROC MI statement MONOTONE REG) at each postbaseline time point.
(3) The first imputed values were to be penalized or rewarded based on the treatment the
subject received:
a. Subjects in the bexagliflozin group: missing value will be analyzed assuming the
treatment effect is worsened by δ1 (where  =0.1 to 0.5, in step of 0.1) compared
to the subjects who have no missing value in the reduction of HbA1c;
b. Subjects in the placebo group: missing value was to be analyzed assuming the
treatment effect is better by δ2 (where the range and step of change δ2 also
depends on the observed study effect size) compared to the subjects who have no
missing value in the reduction of HbA1c.
FDA Reviewer Comment: The Applicant’s preplanning only included a range of δ1 and by δ2
from 0.1 to 0.5 with a fixed step. In this review, depending on the observed study effect sizes, we
adjusted the range of the deltas by increasing the size of the steps (size tailored for each study) to
reach the tipping points.
16.3.4.5.2. Active-Controlled Studies
For each of the two active-controlled studies, we performed a one-dimensional tipping point
analysis: based on the return-to-baseline multiple imputation, we penalized the missing outcomes
on the bexagliflozin arm by the null hypothesis of the noninferiority test (i.e., the margin) while
multiply imputing the active-control arm missing outcomes with the return-to-baseline method.
The goal was to determine if the null margin penalty applied to the bexagliflozin changes the
primary analysis conclusion, and if not, under what level of penalty there is no longer evidence
of a treatment effect.
16.3.4.6. Secondary Endpoint – Primary Analysis Method
All the key secondary efficacy endpoints were continuous change from baseline endpoints. The
above-described return-to-baseline coupled ANCOVA approach was also used for the key
secondary endpoint analyses.

In each of the six pivotal studies, the study SAP prespecified a hierarchical testing sequence to
control for overall type I error due to multiplicity. For each endpoint in the sequence, a two-sided

Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

statistical test was to be conducted at the 5.0% significance level. Table 136 summarizes, for
each study, the ranks of the primary and key secondary endpoints within the study specific
testing sequence.

16.4. Primary and Key Secondary Efficacy Results
For each study, a brief description of study specific trial design and study analysis plan are
provided first before presenting the efficacy analysis results.

16.4.1. Trial C-450
16.4.1.1. Design, Trial C-450
Trial C-450 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallelgroup study. C-450 was designed to compare the efficacy and safety of once daily oral
administration of bexagliflozin tablets, 20 mg, to placebo in treatment-naïve T2DM subjects or
subjects previously treated with no more than one OHA.
260

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Run-In Procedures for OHA Treatment Naïve or Not
Potential subjects were screened within three weeks of the start of the wash-out or run-in period.
The study included both treatment-naïve T2DM subjects with inadequate control on diet and
exercise and subjects on an OHA, who underwent a washout of the OHA. Subjects not on an
OHA directly entered a 2-week, single-blind, placebo run-in period, while subjects on an OHA
underwent a 6-week OHA washout period followed by a 2-week single-blind placebo run-in
period. After the placebo run-in period, all subjects were randomized into a 24-week, doubleblind, placebo-controlled period, followed by a 2-week follow-up period.
Treatment assignment randomization was stratified by HbA1c level at the start of the run-in
period (≤8.5 or >8.5%), country (Canada or United States), and background antidiabetic
treatment status (treatment-naïve or not). The study design is depicted in Figure 47.
Figure 47. Study Plan, Trial C-450

Source: Study 450 Clinical Study Report, Figure 1.

16.4.1.3. Results of Analyses, Trial C-450
16.4.1.3.1. Baseline Demographic, Baseline Clinical
Characteristics, and Disposition
Demographic factors and baseline clinical characteristics were generally balanced between these
two groups, as summarized in Table 137. There was a slightly higher proportion of male subjects
(52%) than female subjects (48%) in C-450. The median age was 56 years ranging from 26 to 81
years of age (18% of all subjects were 65 years or older at trial enrollment). Slightly above 50%
of all subjects identified as Hispanic or Latino. The trial was conducted in North America, with
84% of the subjects from the United States and the rest from Canada. The bexagliflozin 20 mg
arm had slightly higher mean and median baseline BMI (mean 32.8, [SD 5.7] kg/m2; median
31.8) than the placebo arm (mean 30.5 [SD 4.6] kg/m2; median 29.8). Overall, 92% of the
subjects were either overweight or obese at baseline (BMI ≥25 kg/m2); median duration of
diabetes was 8 years.

Analysis population and subject disposition information for Trial C-450 are summarized in Table
139. Overall, about 90% of the subjects completed study treatment, the bexagliflozin group had a
numerically lower treatment completion rate (88%) than the placebo arm (93%). The most
frequent reasons for treatment discontinuation were “subjects withdrew consent” and “subjects
lost to follow-up” for the bexagliflozin arm.
This disposition table reveals two issues with trial disposition data collection and consolidation.
First, a “withdrawal of consent” is a broad umbrella term that covers potentially multiple specific
reasons why subjects opt to withdraw consent and participation in the trial. Withdrawal of
consent is the result of these potential reasons, the term itself gives no deliberate reason as to
which caused the subject to opt out (e.g., efficacy, safety, or administrative reasons). More
specific reasons such as adverse drug effects and tolerability issues, lack of efficacy, subject
relocation, and others could be handled differently in a missing data imputation algorithm.
Second, in analysis dataset derivation, the more than one entry in the form of “adverse event:
specific reason” should be consolidated under one category “adverse event” so unnecessary
details could be omitted from crowding a summary level table. The overall study completion rate
(90.3%) is very close to the treatment completion rate (89.4%).

16.4.1.3.2. Primary Endpoint, and Key Secondary Efficacy
Results

Primary Endpoint – Primary Analysis
Table 140 summarizes the primary analysis results for C-450. Twelve (8.7%) and 5 (7.2%)
subjects in the Bexagliflozin 20 mg group and the placebo group, respectively, had missing
HbA1c data at Week 24.
In the primary analysis based on the ITT analysis set, using the return-to-baseline coupled
ANCOVA analysis, at Week 24, bexagliflozin statistically significantly reduced HbA1c from
baseline compared with placebo. The adjusted mean difference was -0.38 (95%CI: -0.63, -0.13;
one-sided p-value, 0.0016).
Figure 48 displays the mean change from baseline in HbA1c (%) over 24 weeks by time and
treatment group. Data are observed values; vertical lines represent 95% confidence intervals. The
primary return-to-baseline analysis results at Week 24 is plotted separately.

265

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Figure 48. Change From Baseline in HbA1c (%) by Time and Treatment and Week 24 Return-toBaseline Analysis Results, ITT, Trial C-450

Source: FDA statistical reviewer
Abbreviation: ANCOVA, analysis of covariance; eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate; HbA1c, hemoglobin A1c; ITT, intentionto-treat; RTB, return to baseline.

In addition, tipping point sensitivity analysis results (Table 184) show that only under
implausible situations will the treatment effect demonstrated by the primary analysis lose
significance: after imputing both the two treatment arm missing data assuming MAR for the
nonmonotone missing data, then awarding the placebo arm and penalizing the bexagliflozin arm
for the first missing data point within each subject with a delta method, the analysis results for
the regression-based imputation of HbA1c value at Week 24 show that only when penalizing the
bexagliflozin arm by adding 1.6 (%) or more to the imputed data will the estimated treatment
effect lose significance.
Also based on the return-to-baseline multiply imputed HbA1c (%) values at Week 24, a
responder defined as subjects reaching the treatment goal of glycemic control (HbA1c [%] <7%)
was derived. The logistic regression model adjusted proportions are 26.8% and 14.4% for the
bexagliflozin 20 mg group and the placebo group, respectively.

16.4.1.3.3. Subgroup Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
Subgroup analyses showed that the HbA1c lowering effect was consistent across subgroup levels
(Figure 49). There are trends that HbA1c reductions with bexagliflozin were greater in males, in
the subgroup of subjects who had higher baseline HbA1c (>8.5%), and in the subgroup of
subjects who were on an OHA at screening.
To further investigate the potential modulation effect of baseline HbA1c level, a linear regression
“subgroup” analysis using the continuous baseline HbA1c level based on the completers data
was carried out. Figure 50 shows a scatter plot of change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24
versus baseline HbA1c data, overlayed with the estimated by-arm group regression lines. In
general, in both bexagliflozin-treated subjects and in placebo subjects, higher baseline HbA1c
level is associated with a bigger change from baseline HbA1c levels at Week 24. The two byarm regression lines are nearly parallel, the difference in slope (slope in bexagliflozin arm - slope
in placebo arm) is -0.042 (standard error [SE]=0.167, p-value=0.80). The small size of the
difference and the p-value of 0.80 suggest that baseline HbA1c level is less likely to have any
modulating effect on the action of this SGLT2 inhibitor drug in this study population.

16.4.2. Trial C-419
16.4.2.1. Design, Trial C-419
Trial C-419 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study conducted to
determine the effect of bexagliflozin as add-on to metformin on the placebo-corrected change in
HbA1c from baseline to Week 24 in adults with T2DM whose disease is inadequately controlled
by metformin alone.
The trial consisted of a double-blinded main part of the study and an open-label single-arm high
glycemic arm of the study. Figure 51 illustrates the schematic of the overall study plan. The high
glycemic arm recruited 37 subjects with HbA1c >10.5% and ≤12.0% into an open-label arm, to
receive bexagliflozin tablets, 20 mg, for 24 weeks. The endpoint for the open-label arm was the
270

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

change from baseline to Week 24 of the HbA1c (%). For this efficacy review, we focus on the
main part (placebo-controlled) of the study.
In the placebo-controlled part of the study, subjects who successfully completed a 1-week run-in
and who remained eligible were randomized 1:1 to receive bexagliflozin 20 mg or placebo, in
addition to their customary metformin dosage for 24 weeks in an outpatient setting. The
randomization was stratified by HbA1c (≤8.5% or ˃ 8.5%) and country (United States or Japan).
Study subjects were not to change their metformin dosage unless adjustment was medically
necessary to manage hypoglycemia.
Figure 51. Study Plan, Trial C-419

Source: Study 419 Clinical Study Report, Figure 1.
Abbreviations: FU, follow-up

16.4.2.2. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial C-419
16.4.2.2.1. Efficacy Endpoints

Primary Endpoint
•

Change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24

Trial C-419 study protocol and SAP prespecified that no formal hypothesis testing will be
performed for the secondary efficacy endpoints. For the secondary efficacy endpoint, nominal pvalues will be presented for exploratory purposes.

See Section 6.2.2 for description of the analysis methods.

Demographic factors and baseline clinical characteristics were generally balanced between these
two groups, as summarized in Table 142.

There was a higher proportion of male (61%) than female subjects (39%) in C-419. The median
age was 56 years ranging from 28 to 81 years of age (21% of all subjects were 65 years or older
at trial enrollment). The trial was conducted in Japan and the United States, with 52% of the
subjects from the United States and the rest from Japan. Overall, 77% of the subjects were either
overweight or obese at baseline (BMI ≥25 kg/m2); median duration of diabetes was 8.0 years.

16.4.2.3.2. Primary Endpoint, and Key Secondary Efficacy
Results

Primary Endpoint – Primary Analysis
Table 145 summarizes the primary analysis results for C-419. Sixteen (10.1%) and 15 (9.4%)
subjects in the bexagliflozin 20 mg group and placebo group, respectively, had missing HbA1c
data at Week 24.
At Week 24, bexagliflozin 20 mg was statistically significant in lowering HbA1c compared with
placebo in T2DM subjects who has inadequate glycemic control with metformin only with an
adjusted mean difference of -0.48% (95% CI (-0.69 to -0.26); one-sided p-value<0.0001).
Figure 52 displays the mean change from baseline in HbA1c (%) over 24 weeks by time and
treatment group. Data are observed values; vertical lines represent 95% confidence intervals. The
primary return-to-baseline analysis results at Week 24 is plotted separately.

Also based on the multiply imputed HbA1c (%) values at Week 24, a responder defined as

25.3% and 9.0% for the bexagliflozin 20 mg group and the placebo group, respectively.

Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
The study SAP prespecified that no formal hypothesis testing will be performed for the
secondary efficacy endpoints. We validated the Applicant’s analysis results of key secondary
efficacy endpoints including changes from baseline in fasting plasma glucose, systolic blood
pressure, and body mass (see Section 16.5.2). For the individual test results of the three
endpoints, all nominal p-values (all <0.05) were presented for exploratory purposes.
16.4.2.3.3. Subgroup Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
Preplanned subgroup analyses showed that the HbA1c lowering effect was consistent across
subgroup levels (Figure 53). There is a trend that HbA1c reductions with bexagliflozin were less
effective in subjects 65 years of age and older in this study population.
Figure 54 shows a scatter plot of change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24 versus baseline
HbA1c data, overlayed with the estimated by-arm group regression lines. In general, in both
bexagliflozin treated subjects and in placebo subjects, a higher baseline HbA1c level is
associated with bigger change from baseline HbA1c levels at Week 24. Compared with placebo,
bexagliflozin treatment is associated with a slightly flatter slope, the difference in slope (slope in
bexagliflozin arm - slope in placebo arm) is 0.074 (standard error [SE]=0.127, p-value=0.56).
The small size of the difference and the p-value of 0.56 suggest that baseline HbA1c level is less
likely to have any modulating effect on the action of this SGLT2 inhibitor drug in this study
population.

16.4.3. Trial C-423
16.4.3.1. Design, Trial C-423
Study 423 is a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, active-controlled study to demonstrate that
bexagliflozin is noninferior to sitagliptin by evaluating the treatment effect on HbA1c reduction
in subjects with T2DM who have inadequate glycemic control by metformin.

Noninferiority Margin Selection and Justification
The Applicant’s proposed noninferiority margin of 0.35% was based on a published clinical trial
that demonstrated the effectiveness of sitagliptin, 100 mg, on HbA1c reduction in subjects with
T2DM (Charbonnel et al. 2006). The Applicant further reasoned that: the sitagliptin study
included male and female adult subjects with T2DM diagnosis and inadequate glycemic control
278

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

defined by an HbAlc ≥7.0% and ≤10% while taking metformin monotherapy at a stable dose of
≥1500 mg per day. Trial C-423 recruited the same patient population except that the HbAlc
range was between 7.0% to 11%. Baseline HbAlc values in the sitagliptin study (Charbonnel et
al. 2006) were 8.03±0.82% in the placebo arm and 7.96±0.81% in the active arm, respectively.
This is close to the baseline mean HbAlc of 7.99±0.87% in the C-423 subject population. In the
published sitagliptin study, a placebo-adjusted least-squares mean (95% CI) treatment effect of
reduction from baseline in HbAlc was -0.65% (-0.77, - 0.53). The clinical meaningful margin is
selected to be approximately half of sitagliptin effect, i.e., 0.35% reduction.
At the IND stage, FDA reviewed agreed with the margin selection and accepted the justification.
16.4.3.2. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial C-423
16.4.3.2.1. Efficacy Endpoints
The primary and key secondary endpoints listed in this subsection are shown in the study
prespecified hierarchical testing sequence.

16.4.3.3.1. Baseline Demographic, Baseline Clinical
Characteristics, and Disposition

overweight or obese (89%). The median age was 61 years ranging from 23 to 83 years of age.
Baseline clinical characteristics were generally balanced between the bexagliflozin 20 mg and
sitagliptin 100 mg groups.

16.4.3.3.2. Primary Endpoint, and Key Secondary Efficacy
Results

Primary Endpoint – Primary Analysis
Table 149 summarizes the primary analysis results for C-423. At Week 24, 11 (5.8%) and 3
(1.6%) subjects in bexagliflozin 20 mg group and sitagliptin 100 mg group, respectively, had
missing data. A return-to-baseline approach multiple imputation analysis method was used for
missing data handling. For lowering of HbA1c at 24 weeks, bexagliflozin 20 mg was noninferior
to sitagliptin 100 mg (adjusted mean difference 0.08% [95% CI: -0.06, 0.22]).
Also based on the multiply imputed HbA1c (%) values at Week 24, a responder defined as
reaching the treatment goal of glycemic control (HbA1c (%) <7%) was derived. Seventy-seven
(40.3%) subjects reached the treatment goal of glycemic control in the bexagliflozin 20 mg
group versus 87 (45.1%) subjects in the sitagliptin 100 mg group. Logistic regression model
adjusted proportions of responders were 36.4% and 44.5% for bexagliflozin 20 mg group and the
sitagliptin 100 mg group, respectively.
Figure 55 displays the mean change from baseline in HbA1c (%) over 24 weeks by time and
treatment group. Data are observed values; vertical lines represent 95% confidence intervals. The
primary return-to-baseline analysis results at Week 24 is plotted separately.
In addition, the one-dimensional tipping point sensitivity analysis results (Table 186) show that
at the null hypothesis imputation (i.e., a penalty of size 0.35% applied to the bexagliflozin arm
missing data), the primary analysis conclusion is not changed; and only under the implausible
situation, a penalty of size 1.75 applied to the bexagliflozin arm missing data, is the tipping point
reached.

16.4.4. Trial C-480
16.4.4.1. Design, Trial C-480
Trial C-480 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, parallel-group study. It was
designed to evaluate the hypothesis that bexagliflozin would be noninferior to glimepiride as a
therapy added to metformin in subjects with disease inadequately controlled by metformin alone
or by metformin plus one other OHA (see Figure 57 for study design).
287

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)
Figure 57. Study Plan, Trial C-480

Source: Applicant’s Trial C-480 Clinical Study Report, Figure 1.
Abbreviations: met: metformin, OHA: oral hypoglycemic agent, Glim: glimepiride, FU: follow- up, SCN: screening, Rand:
randomization

Treatment Duration
Different from the other five pivotal trials, both the primary endpoint and key secondary efficacy
endpoints were assessed at Week 60.

Noninferiority Margin Selection
The Applicant provided the following justifications for this noninferiority margin selection:
According to FDA Guidance for Industry Non-Inferiority Clinical Trials to Establish
Effectiveness (November 2016), the noninferiority margin, M2, reflects the largest loss of effect
that would be clinical acceptable (Section III.C.). M2 is generally accepted to be 50% of M1, or
the entire effect of the active control versus placebo as seen in historical placebo-controlled trials
and is assumed to be present in the noninferiority study. A double-blind, double-dummy, parallel
group study with a similar study design as Trial C-480 showed subjects taking 6 mg glimepiride
as an add-on to metformin for 8 weeks after a 12 week up-titration period had HbAlc values 0.81% compared to placebo (Charpentier et al. 2001). Fifty percent of the total effect size of the
placebo-controlled study is 0.41%. Based on these historical data, a noninferiority margin of 0.41
could be justified. The proposed 0.35% is a conservative and reasonable M2 noninferiority
margin that represents a clinically acceptable loss of effect to demonstrate noninferiority.
Also, in justification for the noninferiority margin selection in C-423, the Applicant referred to
FDA’s Draft Guidance for Industry Diabetes Mellitus: Developing Drugs and Therapeutic
Biologics for Treatment and Prevention (March 2008). The guidance states, “Typically, we
accept a noninferiority margin of 0.3 or 0.4 HbA1c percentage units provided this is no greater
than a suitably conservative estimate of the magnitude of the treatment effect of the active
control in previous placebo-controlled trials.”
While the reviewer considers the noninferiority margin selection of 0.35% to be in accordance
with FDA Guidance, the reviewer has one concern with the accuracy of the Applicant’s quoted
M1 estimate, 0.81%. In Charpentier 2001, the description was:
…Combination treatment was significantly more efficient in reducing HbA1c
levels than either glimepiride alone (difference in mean change 1.04%, 95% CI

288

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

0.81 to 1.27%; P<0.001) or metformin alone (difference in mean change
0.92%, 95% CI 0.63 to 1.21%; P<0.001). …
Since the primary comparison in C-480 is between bexagliflozin + metformin and glimepiride +
metformin, this reviewer considers a proper reference effect size estimate should be the lower
bound 0.63% of the 95% CI for the treatment effect of (glimepiride + metformin) versus
metformin from the reference paper (Charpentier et al. 2001). Since 50% of 0.63% is 0.315%,
while slightly lower than the selected 0.35%, it is still a reasonable choice. So, we have no
further concern on this issue.

Disposition
Subject disposition information for Trial C-480 is summarized in Table 152 and Table 153.

(21%).

16.4.5.3.2. Primary Endpoint and Key Secondary Efficacy
Results

Primary Endpoint – Primary Analysis
Table 159 summarizes the primary analysis results for C-448. Five (3.2%) and seven (4.5%)
subjects in the Bexagliflozin 20 mg group and the placebo group, respectively, had missing
HbA1c data at Week 24.
In the primary analysis based on the ITT analysis set, based on the return-to-baseline multiple
imputation for missing data coupled ANCOVA analysis, at Week 24, bexagliflozin statistically
significantly reduced HbA1c from baseline compared with placebo. The adjusted mean
difference was -0.27 (95% CI: -0.44, -0.09; one-sided p-value, 0.0017).
300

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Figure 60 displays the mean change from baseline in HbA1c (%) over 24 weeks by time and
treatment group. Data are observed values; vertical lines represent 95% confidence intervals. The
primary return-to-baseline analysis results at Week 24 is plotted separately.
In addition, tipping point sensitivity analysis results (Table 189) show that only under an
implausible situation will the treatment effect demonstrated by the primary analysis lack
significance.
Also based on the return-to-baseline multiply imputed HbA1c (%) values at Week 24, a binary
responder defined as subjects reaching the treatment goal of glycemic control (HbA1c [%] <7%)
variable was derived. The logistic regression model adjusted proportions were 31.3% and 18.1%
for the bexagliflozin 20 mg group and the placebo group, respectively.

While the sequential testing stopped at testing SBP, we know from the subgroup analysis results

16.4.5.3.3. Subgroup Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
Subgroup analyses showed that the HbA1c lowering effect was consistent across subgroup levels
(Figure 61). The subgroup level treatment effects associated with baseline renal function level
are discussed as a review issue in Section 6.3.1.

304

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

To further investigate the potential modulation effect of baseline HbA1c level, a linear regression
“subgroup” analysis using the continuous baseline HbA1c level based on the completers data
was carried out. Figure 62 shows a scatter plot of change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24
versus baseline HbA1c data, overlayed with the estimated by-arm group regression lines. In
general, in both bexagliflozin-treated subjects and in placebo subjects, higher baseline HbA1c
level is associated with a bigger change from baseline HbA1c levels at Week 24. The two byarm regression lines are near parallel, the difference in slope (slope in bexagliflozin arm - slope
in placebo arm) is -0.004 (standard error=0.113, p-value=0.97). The small size of the difference
and the p-value of 0.97 suggest that baseline HbA1c level is less likely to have any modulating
effect on the action of this SGLT2 inhibitor drug in this study population.
Figure 62. Exploration of the Modulation Effect of Baseline HbA1c Levels, Regression Analysis,
ITT, Completer Data at Week 24, Trial C-448

Source: FDA statistical reviewer
Note: The estimated difference in slope between the bexagliflozin arm and the placebo arm is 0.074 (standard error [SE]=0.127, pvalue=0.56). These results is derived from a simple linear regress model based on the completers data with change from baseline in
HbA1c at Week 24 as the response variable, and treatment, baseline HbA1c levels, and a treatment by baseline HbA1c levels
interaction as the predictor variables.
Abbreviations: HbA1c, hemoglobin A1c; ITT, intention-to-treat.

16.4.6. Trial C-476
16.4.6.1. Design, Trial C-476
Trial C-476 was a double-blind, placebo-controlled study to evaluate the placebo-adjusted
change in HbA1c from baseline after 24 weeks of exposure to bexagliflozin in type 2 diabetic
subjects with increased risk of cardiovascular adverse events. The cardiovascular outcome trial
(CVOT) data are reviewed by OB/DBVII and are covered in Section 17.1 of this review.
16.4.6.2. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial C-476
16.4.6.2.1. Efficacy Endpoints
The primary and key secondary endpoints listed in this subsection are shown in the study
prespecified hierarchical testing sequence.

Primary Endpoint
•

Change from baseline in HbA1c at Week 24

Key Secondary Endpoints
•
•
•

prescribed insulin to control their diabetes*

≥25 kg/m2

from baseline

* FDA Reviewer Comment: The Applicant newly added a key secondary endpoint: change in
HbA1c from baseline to Week 24 in randomized subjects who have been prescribed insulin to
control their diabetes in Protocol V7 (FDA reviewed) and described as a substudy in SAP V2
that was not submitted for FDA review (see Section 12). The Applicant newly added HbA1c
change from baseline to Week 24 in subjects who have been prescribed sulfonylurea as an(b) (4)
exploratory endpoint in SAP V2.

was composed of 1699 subjects.
A total of 428 (25%) subjects discontinued the study treatment; a slightly higher rate of
discontinuation was seen with placebo (29%) than with bexagliflozin 20 mg (23%). A lower
percentage of subjects treated with bexagliflozin 20 mg (2.0%) received glycemic rescue therapy
compared with placebo-treated subjects (2.6%). “Other” was documented as the most frequent
reason for treatment discontinuation for the two treatment groups (8% in the bexagliflozin 20 mg
arm, 12% in the placebo arm). Other most frequent reasons for treatment discontinuation
included “adverse event” and “subject withdrew consent.” The overall study completion rate was
86%, 11% higher than the treatment completion rate.

16.4.6.3.2. Primary Endpoint and Key Secondary Efficacy
Results

Primary Endpoint – Primary Analysis
Table 164 summarizes the primary analysis results for C-476. At Week 24, 79 (7.0%) and 34
(6.0%) subjects in the bexagliflozin 20 mg group and placebo group, respectively, had missing
HbA1c data (Table 164). HbA1c was significantly reduced from baseline with bexagliflozin
20 mg compared with placebo (adjusted mean difference −0.44, 95% CI [-0.53, -0.35];
p < 0.0001).
Figure 63 displays the mean change from baseline in HbA1c (%) over 168 weeks by time and
treatment group. Data are observed values; vertical lines represent 95% confidence intervals. The
primary return-to-baseline analysis results at Week 24 is plotted separately.

In addition, tipping point sensitivity analysis results (Table 190) show that only under an
implausible situation will the treatment effect demonstrated by the primary analysis lack
significance.
Also based on the multiply imputed HbA1c (%) values at Week 24, a responder defined as
reaching the treatment goal of glycemic control (HbA1c (%) <7%) was derived. The logistic
regression model adjusted proportions were 24.3% and 12.8% for the bexagliflozin 20 mg group
and the placebo group, respectively.

Key Secondary Endpoints – Primary Analysis
Table 165 summarizes changes from baseline in prespecified key secondary efficacy endpoints.
Bexagliflozin significantly decreased HbA1c at Week 24 in subjects who have been prescribed
insulin, body mass index at Week 48 (in those with baseline BMI ≥25 kg/m2), and SBP at Week
24 (in those with baseline SBP ≥140 mmHg) (p<0.0001, p < 0.0001 and p=0.0209, respectively).
More detailed analysis results are located in Section 16.5.6.
