17.3.12. Fournier’s Gangrene
Based on postmarketing reports of cases of necrotizing fasciitis of the perineum (Fournier’s
gangrene) associated with use of SGLT2 inhibitors, a potential safety signal for this adverse
event was identified by the Division of Pharmacovigilance-I (DPV-1) and a Tracked Safety Issue
was opened (TSI #1844) in 2018. The DPV-I review identified 12 cases of Fournier’s gangrene
associated with SGLT2 inhibitor use in the FDA Adverse Event Reporting System database and
medical literature, prompting a Drug Safety Communication on August 28, 2018 and concurrent
Safety Labeling Change that added Fournier’s gangrene to the Warnings and Precautions section
of the US prescribing information and patient Medication Guide for all products in the SGLT2
inhibitor class.
One case of Fournier’s gangrene was reported by the Applicant in a nondiabetic subject in the
(b) (6) was a 63randomized withdrawal essential hypertension Trial C-603B. Subject 603year-old, white, non-Hispanic male who developed Fournier’s gangrene while randomized to
bexagliflozin in the run-in period of C-603B. An abdominal/pelvic CT scan showed scrotal wall
thickening, subcutaneous emphysema, as well as enlarged retroperitoneal and pelvic sidewall
lymph nodes. He was treated with operative scrotal exploration and IV antibiotics. Notably,
medical history included HIV infection and prior surgery and prosthesis involving the scrotum.
The event was assessed as not likely related to the investigational product by the investigator.
Given the noted risk factors and potential alternative etiology, drug causality of the event cannot
be determined. Nonetheless, given that Fournier’s gangrene cannot be adequately assessed in the
premarket program conducted and given that it is considered a class effect, a Warnings and
Precautions statement will be included in the labeling.

381

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

17.3.13. Revascularization Procedures
As described in Section 7.7.1, the Applicant used a dedicated CRF to capture information on
amputation and revascularization procedures. See Section 7.7.1 for a detailed review of
amputation events. With regards to revascularization procedures, the CRF prompted the
investigator to select one of the following procedure types: cardiac catheterization, percutaneous
coronary intervention (PCI), coronary artery bypass graft (CABG), revascularization for
peripheral artery disease (PAD), carotid artery stenosis with revascularization, cardiac transplant,
or other (if other was selected, investigators were asked to specify additional information). The
revascularization procedures that occurred in C-476 are shown in Table 221. An imbalance was
observed for the number of CABG procedures that occurred in C-476 (2.5% versus 0.7% in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively). The Applicant posits that this imbalance may
represent a favorable drug effect, as patients chosen for CABG must be healthy enough to
withstand major surgery and a selection bias in which candidates from the active arm were
judged to be more fit for surgery may have been present. This seems an unlikely explanation
given what is known about bexagliflozin’s beneficial effects over the course of the trial. Overall,
the meaningfulness of the imbalance in CABG procedures is unclear, but the results from the
MACE meta-analysis provide reassurance that bexagliflozin does not increase the risk of major
adverse cardiovascular events.

17.3.14. Cardiovascular Safety
To show that bexagliflozin was not associated with an excess CV risk, a MACE meta-analysis
was performed according to the recommendations in the FDA Guidance titled Evaluating
Cardiovascular Risk in New Antidiabetic Therapies to Treat Type 2 Diabetes (December 2008).
This Guidance document represented the agency’s current thinking while the bexagliflozin
clinical program was being completed. While this Guidance has since been withdrawn and
replaced with the Guidance document titled Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus: Evaluating the Safety of
New Drugs for Improving Glycemic Control (March 2020), the evaluation of cardiovascular
safety performed by the Applicant is adequate to evaluate the impact of bexagliflozin on
cardiovascular safety.
A detailed review of the MACE meta-analysis is presented in Section 17.1. In short, the upper
bound of the 95% confidence interval for the HR for major adverse cardiovascular events
defined by a four-point composite endpoint (cardiovascular death, stroke, myocardial infarction,
or hospitalization for unstable angina) was less than 1.8 (HR: 0.80 [0.58, 1.09]) and the upper
bound of the 95% confidence interval for the hazard ratio defined by a three point composite
endpoint (cardiovascular death, stroke, or myocardial infarction) was less than 1.3 (HR: 0.82
382

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

[0.59, 1.13]). The upper bound in the latter case was greater than unity and hence superiority to
comparator for reduction of risk was not established. The conclusions were corroborated by
several sensitivity analyses. HRs favorable to bexagliflozin were observed for all subcomponents
with the exception of the risk of nonfatal stroke (HR: 1.2 [0.59, 2.46]), which may be due to few
events observed in this category. Subgroup analyses did not identify any meaningful differences
in HR by age, sex, race, geographical region, body mass index, disease duration, disease
severity, renal function, or systolic blood pressure. Overall, exposure to bexagliflozin is not
expected to unacceptably increase the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events in diabetic
patients.

17.4. Laboratory Findings
Blood samples for the evaluation of hematology, chemistry, and lipid parameters were collected
and assessed in all trials. Sampling timepoints varied by trial, but generally occurred at
screening, at the end of treatment (typically Week 24), and at the follow-up safety visit (typically
Week 26), as well as at prespecified intervals during each trial, e.g., every 6 to 12 weeks.
Urinalysis was also routinely performed, although results of urine glucose in urinalysis reports
were suppressed as not to unblind treatment assignment. The placebo-controlled pool was
deemed appropriate to characterize changes in laboratory parameters following bexagliflozin
exposure. In the analyses utilizing the placebo-controlled pool representing change in laboratory
parameters over time, data from the Week 14 follow-up visit from C-449 is excluded, and only
Trials C-419 and C-450 contribute to the Week 18, 24, and 26 timepoints. Data from other trials
(e.g., C-448 and C-476) are also presented where appropriate.

Hematology
The SGLT2 inhibitor pharmacologic class is associated with small increases in hemoglobin and
hematocrit, which may be attributable to osmotic diuresis and hemoconcentration. In the
placebo-controlled pool, a mean increase of 0.4 g/dL (2.7%) hemoglobin and 1.3% hematocrit
was observed from baseline to Week 24 in the bexagliflozin group, which remained slightly
elevated at the Week 26 follow-up visit, as opposed to a slight mean decrease in each parameter
in the placebo group (Figure 76). Increases in these parameters were slightly more pronounced in
C-476 and C-448 (data not shown). As shown in Table 222, a higher proportion of subjects had
hemoglobin increases >2 g/dL and >3 g/dL from baseline in the bexagliflozin group compared to
the placebo group in all trials.
It is clinically plausible that increases in hemoglobin and hematocrit could lead to an increased
likelihood of experiencing a thromboembolic event. As discussed in Section 17.1, the results
from the MACE+ meta-analysis were overall favorable to bexagliflozin. The nonfatal stroke
component of the MACE+ meta-analysis demonstrated a point estimate that was unfavorable to
bexagliflozin (HR: 1.2; 95% CI: 0.6, 2.5); however, limited events produced wide confidence
intervals that limit the interpretability of this data. Several additional analyses were completed to
address this concern utilizing data from C-476, which enrolled a subject population that may be
more likely to experience a thromboembolic event as compared to other trials in the clinical
program. In addition, approximately 21% of subjects in C-476 had a least one hemoglobin result
>2 g/dL above their baseline value (Table 222). As shown in Table 222, there were
proportionally fewer thromboembolic events in the bexagliflozin group compared to the placebo
383

Reference ID: 5112305

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

group as assessed by FMQs for “Thrombosis,” “Thrombosis (Arterial),” and “Thrombosis
(Venous),” as well as a CMQ that has been previously developed and utilized to assess
potentially meaningful thromboembolic events associated with SGLT2 inhibitor treatment.
Overall, thromboembolic events do not appear to be a safety issue for bexagliflozin based on the
results in this clinical program, but a meta-analysis of events across the class might better inform
this potential safety signal, which has a mechanistically plausible basis.
There were no meaningful differences in central tendencies between the bexagliflozin group and
the placebo group in the placebo-controlled pool for other hematology parameters, i.e.,
leukocytes, platelets, eosinophils, lymphocytes, and neutrophils (Figure 76). Regarding the
proportion of subjects with one or more values exceeding specified levels for hematology
parameters (Table 222), a difference was observed in subjects who experienced one or more
level 1 (<1000 cells/uL) decreases in lymphocyte values between the bexagliflozin group (5.6%)
and the placebo group (1.7%) in the placebo-controlled pool, which was not observed in other
trials. The clinical significance of this finding is unclear, although it is notable that an increased
incidence in the system organ class of “Infections and Infestations” was not observed in the
placebo-controlled pool (16.1% versus 17.7% in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups,
respectively).

Lipids
Fasting lipid measurements were completed in all trials, and subjects were queried to assess
compliance with an approximately 10 hours fast prior to blood draw to ensure lipid parameters
could be accurately assessed. Small increases in low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C)
have been associated with the SGLT2 inhibitor pharmacologic class, and changes in LDL-C are
currently described in the labeling for approved products. In the placebo-controlled pool, mean
changes in LDL-C of 1.5 mg/dL (1.4%) and -3.3 mg/dL (-3.1%) were observed from baseline to
Week 24 in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively (Figure 77). LDL-C was also
slightly elevated within the first 24 weeks of treatment of Trial C-476, with mean changes from
baseline to Week 24 of 3.1 mg/dL (4.1%) and 2.5 (3.2%) mg/dL in the bexagliflozin and placebo
groups, respectively, although these elevations decreased over time with mean changes returning
to baseline in both groups by Week 72 (data not shown). No consistent trends were observed in
C-476 regarding the proportion of subjects with at least one value exceeding certain LDL-C
thresholds; however, there was a greater proportion of subjects who had at least one value that
exceeded level 1 (>130 mg/dL) and level 2 (>160 mg/dL) in the bexagliflozin group as compared
to the placebo group in the placebo-controlled pool and C-448 (Table 224). Regarding other lipid
parameters, small increases in high-density lipoprotein cholesterol were observed, with mean
changes from baseline to Week 24 of 2.2 (4.3%) mg/dL and -0.2 (-0.4%) mg/dL in the
bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively, of the placebo-controlled pool. There was also a
decrease in triglycerides, with mean changes from baseline at Week 24 of -16 mg/dL (-9.3%)
and -6.9 (-3.4%) mg/dL in the bexagliflozin and placebo groups, respectively.
FDA Reviewer Comment: As presented in Section 17.1, the MACE+ meta-analysis supports that
bexagliflozin is not associated with increased risk for MACE, providing reassuring evidence that
the small changes in lipid parameters described herein do not adversely impact cardiac safety.

Liver Chemistry
ALT, AST, and total and direct bilirubin were measured in all trials. Alkaline phosphatase was
also measured in all trials with the exception of C-448 and in C-450. In the placebo-controlled
pool, there were no meaningful changes in central tendencies in liver chemistry parameters, with
minor decreases in ALT, AST, and alkaline phosphatase between baseline and Week 24 (Figure
78). Results were comparable in Trials C-476 and C-448 (data not shown). Outlier enzyme
elevations were generally balanced between treatment groups. See Section 17.3.5 Hepatic Injury
for additional analysis of potential DILI events.

NDA 214373
Brenzavvy (bexagliflozin)

Chemistry
In the placebo-controlled pool, no meaningful changes were observed from baseline to Week 24
in mean sodium, potassium, chloride, calcium, protein, and albumin (Figure 79).
Magnesium is regulated by the urinary system and hypermagnesemia is most often associated
with renal impairment in the absence of overdose scenarios, making this laboratory marker
relevant in assessing the impact of bexagliflozin on renal function. Clinical symptoms such as
nausea, lethargy, and headache may be seen when plasma levels exceed 4.8 mg/dL, while
concentrations higher than 7.2 mg/dL are associated with hypocalcemia, hypotension,
bradycardia, and EKG changes. There was an increase of 0.13 mg/dL (6.8%) in magnesium
levels in the bexagliflozin group compared to a -0.01 mg/dL (0.5%) decrease in the placebo
group in the placebo-controlled pool at week 24, which returned approximately to baseline levels
after drug withdrawal at the follow-up visit. Increases of approximately 0.32 mg/dL (18%) and
0.21 mg/dL (10%) from baseline to Week 24 were also observed in the bexagliflozin groups of
C-476 and C-448, respectively, as compared to an increase of 0.1 mg/dL (5%) and a decrease of
-0.02 mg/dL (1%) in the respective placebo groups. In line with these results, the placebocontrolled pool, C-476, and C-448 had a higher proportion of subjects with level 1 (>2.3 mg/dL)
increases in magnesium in the bexagliflozin group compared to the placebo group in each trial,
although it is reassuring that no subjects had magnesium values meeting the criteria for level 2
(>4 mg/dL) or Level 3 (>7 mg/dL) (Table 226). In addition, a search for AEs indicative of
elevated magnesium levels (i.e., PTs of “hypermagnesemia” or “blood magnesium increased”) in
bexagliflozin-exposed subjects did not reveal any events in any trial. While not currently
described in the labeling for approved SGLT2 inhibitors, increases in serum magnesium
associated with SGLT2 inhibitor treatment have been noted in prior clinical reviews (see Dr.
Hyon’s review for canagliflozin in DARRTS dated August 10, 2012) and reported in the
literature (Tang et al. 2016; Sasaki et al. 2017; Ray et al. 2020). The magnitude of serum
magnesium elevations observed in the clinical program do not appear clinically concerning.
Transient decreases in mean serum bicarbonate occurred in the placebo-controlled pool, with a
maximum mean decrease of -1.4 mEq/L in the bexagliflozin group compared to -0.5 mEq/L in
the placebo group at Week 6. Transient decreases were also observed in C-476 and C-448
although to a lesser extent (data not shown). In line with these results, there were also more
subjects who experienced at least one level 1 (<20 mEq/L), 2 (<18 mEq/L), and 3 (<15 mEq/L)
low bicarbonate values in the bexagliflozin groups of the placebo-controlled pool and C-476
compared to the placebo groups (Table 226). Similar trends were also observed for the
proportion of subjects with values exceeding level 1 and 2 low bicarbonate values for C-448. The
clinical meaningfulness of these findings is unclear. As discussed in Section 17.3.6, there was no
signal for DKA observed in the bexagliflozin clinical program; however, the event rate of DKA
may be too low to adequately assess in a premarket setting.
Serum phosphate increased 0.23 mg/dL (6.6%) in the bexagliflozin group as compared to
0.02 mg/dL (0.6%) in the placebo group from baseline to Week 24 in the placebo-controlled
pool. Similar trends were observed in C-476 and C-448 (data not shown). Minor increases in
serum phosphate levels have been previously characterized (e.g., see US prescribing information
for ertugliflozin).

Renal Function
See Sections 7.7.2 and 17.3.7 for a detailed discussion regarding drug-induced changes in renal
function.

17.5. Vital Signs
The Applicant assessed changes in blood pressure and heart rate in the clinical program. In the
placebo-controlled pool, a small mean reduction in SBP from baseline to Week 24 occurred in
the bexagliflozin group as opposed to a small mean increase in the placebo group (Figure 80).
Blood pressure changes were evaluated as secondary efficacy endpoints in the clinical program
and additional information characterizing the drug effect on blood pressure can be found in
Section 6.2.3.3. In addition, refer to Section 17.3.8 for additional information regarding an
assessment of hypotension in the clinical program. There were no noteworthy changes in heart
rate between treatment groups (data not shown).

17.6. QT
The Applicant conducted a thorough QT (TQT) study (C-428) to assess the proarrhythmic
potential of bexagliflozin. The protocol for this study was reviewed by the Agency prior to its
initiation under IND 103822. In this randomized, partially-blinded, three-period crossover study,
37 healthy subjects received bexagliflozin 100 mg (capsule formulation), placebo, and a single
oral dose of moxifloxacin 400 mg. The Applicant’s TQT study results were reviewed by the
Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies (IRT-CS), and their independent
analysis yielded similar results to those reported by the Applicant. The IRT-CS states that the
studied dose of 100 mg meets the recommendation for exposure coverage in a TQT study. The
IRT-CS confirmed that C-428, conducted using the capsule formulation of bexagliflozin, is
adequate to support the QT assessment for the to-be-marketed tablet formulation. The largest
upper bound of the 2-sided 90% CI for the mean difference between bexagliflozin and placebo
was below 10 ms, which is the threshold for regulatory concern as described in ICH E14
guidelines (Table 227). The IRT-CS concluded that a single dose of 100 mg bexagliflozin does
not cause drug-related QT prolongation (DARRTS review under NDA 214373 dated December
6, 2021 and under IND 103822 dated November 4, 2013). These findings are in accordance with
nonclinical studies, which indicated a low potential for QT prolongation.

Regarding other safety findings from C-428, there were no deaths, SAEs, or AEs of clinical
importance per ICH E14 guidelines (e.g., syncope, seizures, significant ventricular arrhythmias,
sudden cardiac death) that occurred in this study. Subjects who experienced a PR interval >200
ms or a QRS interval >100 ms were generally balanced between bexagliflozin and placebo
treatment periods (data not shown).

17.7. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
Safety analyses by demographic subgroup utilizing the placebo-controlled pool and C-476 were
performed to evaluate whether commonly occurring events and AESIs differed importantly
between subgroups (Table 228 and Table 229). For the placebo-controlled pool, grouped queries
(GQs) were utilized to align with analyses of this data pool presented in Section 6 and in the
labeling, and commonly occurring AEs (i.e., >2% and greater in the bexagliflozin group than the
placebo group) are presented. For C-476, CMQs derived by the Applicant were utilized to
examine AESIs by subgroup, and selected AESIs are presented. A trend between increased BMI
quartile and female GMI events in subjects treated with bexagliflozin was observed in both the
placebo-controlled pool and C-476. There were no concerning trends regarding age >65 years
and increased incidence of adverse reactions in either analysis. More females had UTI events in
both bexagliflozin and placebo groups.

18. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance:
Additional Information and
Assessment
Not applicable.

19. Other Drug Development
Considerations: Additional Information
and Assessment
Not applicable.

20. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office
of Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
Eight domestic clinical investigators were inspected. Investigators/study sites were selected
based on the ability of data from their site to potentially impact the overall benefit risk
assessment. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) concludes that no significant regulatory
violations were found upon inspection and that, based on the results of inspections and
regulatory assessments, C-419, C-423, C-448, C-450, C-476 and C-480 appear to have been
conducted adequately. See OSI’s review dated July 13, 2022 in DARRTS for additional
information.

21. Labeling Summary of Considerations
and Key Additional Information
The Applicant has submitted proposed labeling for bexagliflozin. The proposed labeling was
reviewed to ensure that the labeling is reflective of the data submitted in this Application. The
labeling was also reviewed for consistency with approved labeling for other SGLT2 inhibitor
products including JARDIANCE (empagliflozin), FARXIGA (dapagliflozin), STEGLATRO
(ertugliflozin), and INVOKANA (canagliflozin), as it relates to events considered to be class
effects. Significant labeling negotiations are summarized below.
(b) (4)

22. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
There were no issues identified that should be addressed by a postmarketing requirement (PMR)
or commitment (PMC) aside from the pediatric study required under the Pediatric Research
Equity Act (PREA) and the manufacturing PMCs described in Section 9. For further information
see the action letter for this application.
